The integration contour in the complex
s

Abstract




July 2023

Effective gravitational action for 2D massive Majorana

fermions on arbitrary genus Riemann surfaces

Manojna Namuduri and Adel Bilal

Laboratoire de Physique de l’École Normale Supérieure

PSL University, CNRS, Sorbonne Université, Université de Paris

24 rue Lhomond, F-75231 Paris Cedex 05, France

Abstract

We explore the effective gravitational action for two-dimensional massive EuclideanMajorana fermions in a small mass expansion, continuing and completing the study initiated in a previous paper
[
1
]
. We perform a detailed analysis of local zeta functions, heat kernels, and Green’s functions of the Dirac operator on arbitrary Riemann surfaces. We obtain the full expansion of the effective gravitational action to all orders in
m^{2}
. For genus one and larger, this requires the understanding of the role of the zero-modes of the (massless) Dirac operator which is worked out.

Besides the Liouville action, at order
m^{0}
, which only involves the background metric and the conformal factor
\sigma
, the various contributions to the effective gravitational action at higher orders in
m^{2}
can be expressed in terms of integrals of the renormalized Green’s function at coinciding points of the squared (massless) Dirac operator, as well as of higher Green’s functions. In particular, at order
m^{2}
, these contributions can be re-written as a term
\int e^{2\sigma}\,\sigma
characteristic of the Mabuchi action, much as for 2D massive scalars, as well as several other terms that are multi-local in the conformal factor
\sigma
and involve the Green’s functions of the massless Dirac operator and the renormalized Green’s function, but for the background metric only.

1
Introduction

1.1
Motivation and outline of the paper

Quantum field theory on a fixed curved Lorentzian (space-time) or Euclidean (space) manifold has been extensively studied for more than half a century. Standard references include
[
2
]
. One of the most prominent early manifestations was the discovery of Hawking radiation near a black hole horizon
[
3
]
. In a full theory of quantum gravity one should, of course, also do the functional integral over all geometries which means summing over topologies and, for each topology, over the inequivalent metrics. This is a notoriously difficult problem. The usual way to decompose the problem is to first do the functional integral over the “matter” fields giving the so-called matter partition function, or equivalently the effective gravitational action, and deal with the functional integral over the geometries only in a second step. This is, of course, similar to what one often does for non-abelian gauge theories where one first integrates over the matter fields for a fixed gauge field configuration yielding an effective action
S_{\rm eff}[A]
of the gauge fields only, and then deals with the problems of gauge fixing only in a second step. In particular, this allows to discuss important issues like anomalies already at the level of this effective action
[
4
]
. Here we will be interested similarly in determining an effective action for two-dimensional gravity and matter systems on spaces of Euclidean signature.

More precisely, the functional integral over the matter field(s) which we denote generically by
\Psi
, computed with a fixed metric
g
on a given manifold
{\cal M}
, defines the matter partition function as
Z_{\rm mat}[g]=\int{\cal D}\Psi\exp\left(-S_{\rm mat}[g,\Psi]\right)
. In terms of this, the effective gravitational action is defined, as usual, as the ratio of
Z_{\rm mat}[g]
and
Z_{\rm mat}[\widehat{g}]
, where we consider
\widehat{g}
as a reference metric :

\exp\big(-S_{\rm grav}[g,\widehat{g}]\big)=\frac{Z_{\rm mat}[g]}{Z_{\rm mat}[\widehat{g}]}\ .

(1.1)

In two dimensions, the metric depends on 3 functions, but using the diffeomorphism invariance one can essentially fix two of them, leaving only one geometrically relevant function which can be chosen as an overall conformal factor
e^{2\sigma(x)}
. Hence, for fixed topology and finitely many fixed (“modular”) parameters
\tau_{i}
we can always pick a reference metric
\widehat{g}\equiv\widehat{g}[\tau_{i}]
such that any other metric (corresponding to the same
\tau_{i}
) can be written, up to diffeomorphisms, as
1
1

1

This is a global statement. Locally one can go further and choose so-called isothermal coordinates
[
5
]
which are such that
g_{\mu\nu}(x)=e^{2\lambda(x)}\,\delta_{\mu\nu}
, but obviously this cannot be done globally unless
\int\sqrt{g}{\cal R}=8\pi(1-{\tt g})
vanishes.

g_{\mu\nu}(x)=e^{2\sigma(x)}\,\widehat{g}_{\mu\nu}(x)\ .

(1.2)

We may then equivalently consider that
S_{\rm grav}
depends on
\widehat{g}
and
\sigma
:
S_{\rm grav}[g,\widehat{g}]\equiv S_{\rm grav}[\sigma,\widehat{g}]
.
The definition (
1.1
) implies that
S_{\rm grav}
must satisfy a cocycle identity

S_{\rm grav}[g_{3},g_{2}]+S_{\rm grav}[g_{2},g_{1}]=S_{\rm grav}[g_{3},g_{1}]\ .

(1.3)

The best-known example is the effective gravitational action for conformal matter coupled to 2D gravity which is the Liouville action
[
6
]

S_{\rm Liouville}[g,\widehat{g}]\equiv S_{\rm Liouville}[\sigma,\widehat{g}]=\int{\rm d}^{2}x\sqrt{\widehat{g}}\,\big(\sigma\widehat{\Delta}\sigma+\widehat{\cal R}\sigma\big),\quad g=e^{2\sigma}\widehat{g}\ ,

(1.4)

where
\widehat{\cal R}
is the scalar curvature for the metric
\widehat{g}
, and

S_{\rm grav}[g,\widehat{g}]=-\frac{c}{24\pi}S_{\rm Liouville}[g,\widehat{g}]\ ,

(1.5)

where
c
is the central charge of the conformal matter system, namely
c=1
for a massless scalar and
c=\frac{1}{2}
for a massless Majorana spinor.
An even simpler example satisfying this cocycle identity is the “cosmological constant action”

S_{\rm c}[g,\widehat{g}]=\mu_{0}\int{\rm d}^{2}x(\sqrt{g}-\sqrt{\widehat{g}})=\mu_{0}(A-A_{0})\ .

(1.6)

This action typically is also present as a counterterm, in addition to
S_{\rm Liouville}
, to renormalize certain divergences that might be present.

But these are not the only two-dimensional gravitational actions, satisfying the cocycle condition, that can be constructed and have been studied in the mathematical literature. Other appropriate functionals are the Mabuchi and Aubin-Yau actions
[
7
,
8
]
, to be defined in the next subsection. These latter functionals involve not only the conformal factor
\sigma
but also the Kähler potential
\Phi
, and can also be generalized to higher-dimensional Kähler manifolds, where they appear in relation with the characterization of constant scalar curvature metrics
[
8
]
.
In the context of a two-dimensional effective action, they appeared first in ref.
[
9
]
where the matter partition function of non-conformal matter like a massive scalar field was studied. It was shown there that in this case the gravitational action as defined by (
1.1
) contains these Mabuchi and Aubin-Yau actions as first-order in
m^{2}
corrections to the Liouville action. Higher-order corrections in
m^{2}
to the effective gravitational action for such massive scalar fields have been obtained
2
2

2

Note that at present we call
S_{\rm grav}[g,\widehat{g}]
what was called
S_{\rm grav}[\widehat{g},g]
in refs
[
9
,
13
,
10
,
11
]
.

in
[
10
]
. The interesting extension to two-dimensional manifolds with boundaries was studied in
[
11
]
.

The fixed-area partition function of quantum gravity, with a gravitational action being a sum of the Liouville and Mabuchi actions, has been studied at one loop in
[
12
]
and at two and three loops in
[
13
]
. In a different approach, a rigourous mathematical construction of the functional integral based on the coupling of the Liouville and Mabuchi actions has been obtained in ref
[
14
]
by means of probabilistic tools.

So far, references
[
9
,
10
,
11
]
considered the effective gravitational action of a two-dimensional massive scalar field. It is natural to try to extend this to the case of two-dimensional massive fermions. The most “elementary” 2D fermion is a Majorana fermion. As we will discuss below, this corresponds to a real anti-commuting spinor field with a Majorana-type mass term. Since the corresponding Dirac operator can be chosen purely imaginary, classically, at the level of the Dirac equation, one can impose a Majorana (reality) condition, on the spinor. In the quantum theory however, we need to expand the Majorana field on the eigenfunctions of this purely imaginary Dirac operator, and then these eigenfunctions are necessarily complex. One might ask whether it is then appropriate to talk about Majorana fermions in the quantum theory. However, we can obtain the partition function in terms of the eigenvalues of the (real) square of the Dirac operator whose eigenfunctions can all be chosen to be real. Moreover, we will show that we can consistently do the functional integral over a
real
anti-commuting spinor field. If one is interested instead in a (complex) Dirac spinor, the corresponding gravitational action is easily seen to be simply twice the one for a Majorana spinor.

In a previous paper
[
1
]
involving one of the present authors, the study of this effective gravitational action for massive Majorana fermions was initiated.
3
3

3

After the present paper was posted on arXiv, the authors of
[
15
]
kindly brought their reference to our attention, where they also study the effective gravitational action, up to terms quadratic in the curvature, for two-dimensional massive scalar, Dirac and vector matter fields, along the heat kernel methods as worked out in
[
16
]
. Their emphasis is mostly on obtaining the beta-functions.

While at first this appeared to be a simple generalisation of the case of the scalar field, it actually turned out to be technically quite non-trivial. Much as for the scalar case, the renormalized Green’s functions at coinciding points played an important role. In order to do a small mass expansion one must be able to define these Green’s functions also for the massless theory in which case the contributions of the zero-modes (of the massless Dirac operator) must be subtracted. For spherical topology there are no zero-modes and then this subtlety does not occur. For this reason,
[
1
]
established the form of the effective gravitational action only for spherical topology and only up to order
m^{2}
. Further technical subtleties implied that even in this case the result was more complicated than the one obtained in
[
9
]
or
[
10
]
for the massive scalar field. Nevertheless, a term characteristic of the Mabuchi action emerged.

In the present paper we will generalise this result and establish the effective gravitational action for arbitrary genus Riemann surfaces (without boundaries), and in an expansion to all orders in
m^{2}A
. We will show that this expansion has a finite radius of convergence !

An important ingredient which enabled us to go beyond spherical topology was the understanding of the structure of the projectors on the zero-modes of the massless Dirac operator. Indeed, the massless Dirac operator transforms in a particularly simple way under local conformal transformation (which is neither the case for the massive Dirac operator, nor for the non-zero eigenvalue equation of the Dirac operator, even for zero mass). This implies that the zero-modes of the massless Dirac operator for metric
g
are related by a very simple conformal rescaling
4
4

4

This result is implicit in
[
17
,
18
]
and was spelled out more explicitly in an early draft of
[
19
]
.

to the zero-modes of the massless Dirac operator for metric
\widehat{g}
, and then a similarly simple relation exists between the zero-mode projectors for metrics
g
and
\widehat{g}
. This allows us to characterise the zero-mode contributions in general, valid on Riemann surfaces of arbitrary genus.

The study of the massive and massless Dirac operator
D
and of its square
D^{2}
, of their associated Greens’s functions and local zeta-functions and local heat kernels occupied a major part in
[
1
]
and will again occupy an important part of the present paper. However, here we will be able to do a much more systematic study of these quantities and prove several important relations, some of which were only conjectured in
[
1
]
. As a result, we are able to obtain the effective gravitational action as an all-orders expansion in powers of
m^{2}
with each term in the expansion expressed as an integral over the manifold of these Green’s functions, appropriately renormalized, as well as higher Green’s functions at coinciding points. As already mentioned, a detailed knowledge of the contributions of the zero-modes (for zero mass) is required to perform the small-mass expansion correctly.

We will see that besides the leading Liouville term, we get a new order
m^{0}
contribution when zero-modes of the massless Dirac operator are present. It might seem surprising to get a “new” order
m^{0}
contribution in the massive theory that seemed not to be there in the massless theory. Actually, we will see that this same contribution does arise in the massless theory. However, in the massless theory one could modify or even remove these new terms by appropriately changing the definition of the matter partition function. On the other hand, in the massive theory there is no reason to do such a redefinition and these zero-mode related contributions are genuinely present.

At order
m^{2}
we get a cosmological constant action, and a local
\int\sqrt{\widehat{g}}\,\sigma e^{2\sigma}
term characteristic of the Mabuchi action,
as well as some further “non-local terms” involving integrals of the Green’s functions computed with the metric
g
. We have been able to rewrite the latter as some multi-local functionals in the conformal factor
\sigma
(i.e. multiple integrals involving the conformal factor at the different integration points) and furthermore only involving the Green’s functions computed in the background metric
\widehat{g}
, as well as a finite number of area-like parameters. It would, of course, be desirable to rewrite all terms as purely local (single) integrals expressed in terms of local quantities like the conformal factor, the Kähler potential or some generalisation thereof, and the metric and curvature. However, as usual with effective actions, there is no reason that they should be local, and we think at present that our multi-local form (or some equivalent formulation) is the best one can achieve.

The effective gravitational action is of course to be used if one wants to compute the functional integral over the geometries, which involves in particular a functional integration over the conformal factor
{\cal D}\sigma
, similarly to what was done in
[
12
]
at one loop or
[
13
]
for higher loops. The form of the effective gravitational action we have obtained explicitly displays this
\sigma
-dependence, having it separated from the quantities that only depend on the fixed “background” metric
\widehat{g}
. This explicit separation should facilitate this integration over the geometries.

We have tried to write the present paper in a hopefully self-contained and pedagogical way. It is organised as follows. In the second part of this introduction we recall a few facts about the Liouville, Mabuchi and Aubin-Yau actions and their stationary points, and then briefly recall, as a warm-up exercise, how one can show that the effective gravitational action for a massless scalar field is the Liouville action. In the next section we introduce the relevant differential operators : massive Dirac operator
D
, its square
D^{2}
, and the scalar and spinoral Laplacians. We discuss their eigenvalue problems in some detail and exhibit the eigenvalues and eigenfunctions for the simple example of the flat torus. We work out the transformations of
D
and of
D^{2}
under conformal changes of the metric (both infinitesimal and finite).
We also work out exactly how the zero-modes of the (massless) Dirac operator change under the local conformal rescalings, allowing us to precisely define the zero-mode projectors that are crucial ingredients for the small mass expansion. Indeed, as already mentioned, these projectors have to be subtracted later-on from the Green’s function in order to define the Green’s functions of the massless theory around which the small-mass expansion will be done.

In section 3 we discuss the fermionic functional integral defining the matter partition function and obtain the gravitational action. We show precisely how it is related to the product of the eigenvalues of the Dirac operator
D
and how this is related to the corresponding zeta-function. We offer also a few remarks about the convergence of these zeta-functions and their definition by analytic continuation. We show how these zeta-functions change under infinitesimal conformal rescalings of the metric.

Then, section 4 contains most of the important technical results : we embark on a detailed study of the different Green’s functions, local zeta-functions
\zeta_{\pm}(s,x,y)
and local heat kernels
K_{\pm}(t,x,y)
. We express precisely how the change of the zeta function (expressing the gravitational action) under infinitesimal conformal rescalings
\delta\sigma
is related to integrals of the local zeta functions times
\delta\sigma
.
We prove a precise correspondence between the local zeta function
\zeta_{-}(s,x,y)
associated with
D
and the local zeta function
\zeta_{+}(s+\frac{1}{2},x,y)
associated with
D^{2}
. This relation allows us to make precise statements
5
5

5

In particular, this allows us to correct some inexact conjectures made in
[
1
]
about the small-
t
asymptotics of
K_{-}(t,x,x)
, as well as about the poles of
\zeta_{-}(s,x,x)
at negative half-integers which turn out not to be there.

about the singularity structures of both local zeta functions
\zeta_{\pm}
, as well as about the small-
t
asymptotics of the heat kernel
K_{-}(t,x,y)
, solely from the knowledge of the corresponding asymptotics of
K_{+}(t,x,y)
that is worked out in the appendix.

All this is put together in section 5 to obtain the variation of the effective gravitational action. We will be able to isolate the zero-mode contributions for arbitrary topology and thus obtain results valid on Riemann surfaces of arbitrary genus. The variation of the gravitational action is obtained as a total variation of a converging series expansion in powers of
m^{2}
. “Integrating” these variations at any order in
m^{2}
gives the corresponding gravitational action at any order in terms of integrals over higher and higher Green’a function (computed in the metric
g
) at coinciding points.
We then work out how the order
m^{2}
contribution can be re-expressed in terms of the Mabuchi type term
\int\sqrt{\widehat{g}}\,\sigma\,e^{2\sigma}
and integrals and multiple integrals involving only the conformal factor
\sigma
at the different integration points, as well as the Green’s function of
D
for the background metric
\widehat{g}
and the renormalized Green’s function of
D^{2}
at coinciding points, also in the background metric
\widehat{g}
. We expect that a similar rewriting can also be performed on the contributions of order
m^{4}
and higher.

In appendix A we give the small-
t
asymptotics of the heat kernel
K_{+}(t,x,y)
by solving recursively the associated (heat) differential equation. We do this first quite generally for positive second-order differential operators and then specialise our results to the squared Dirac operator. No such recursive solution is available for the corresponding
K_{-}
since it does not have a simple initial condition.
In appendix B we discuss some properties of this
K_{-}(t,x,y)
for the flat torus in which case we have explicit eigenvalues and eigenfunctions at our disposal, confirming the general properties obtained in section 4.

Results largely overlapping with ours, in particular concerning the zero-mode projectors, have been obtained independently in
[
19
]
. Their treatment of the orthonormalisation of the zero-modes allowed us to correct a slight error in the first version of the present paper.

1.2
Some (more or less) well-known gravitational actions : Liouville, Mabuchi and Aubin-Yau

Besides the Liouville action
S_{\rm Liouville}
which we already defined in (
1.4
), and the somewhat trivial cosmological constant action
S_{\rm c}
given in (
1.6
) there are two other, more or less well-known gravitational actions, namely the Mabuchi and Aubin-Yau actions.
Let us briefly recall the definitions and basic properties of these gravitational actions.
While the Liouville action (
1.4
) can be written in terms of
\widehat{g}
and the conformal factor
\sigma
, the Mabuchi and Aubin-Yau actions are formulated using also the Kähler potential
\Phi
. The conformal factor
\sigma
and the Kähler potential
\Phi
are related by (we may take this as the definition of the Kähler potenial
\Phi
)

g=e^{2\sigma}\widehat{g}\quad,\quad e^{2\sigma}=\frac{A}{\widehat{A}}\left(1-\frac{1}{2}\widehat{A}\,\widehat{\Delta}\Phi\right)\quad,\quad\Delta=\frac{1}{\sqrt{g}}\partial_{\mu}\big(\sqrt{g}g^{\mu\nu}\partial_{\nu}\big)=e^{-2\sigma}\widehat{\Delta}\ ,

(1.7)

where
\widehat{\Delta}
denotes the (scalar) Laplacian for the metric
\widehat{g}
with area
\widehat{A}=\int{\rm d}^{2}x\sqrt{\widehat{g}}
.
The second relation in this equation (
1.7
) shows that the Kähler potential describes different metrics of the same total area
A
, and the latter needs to be introduced as an additional variable. This separation becomes particularly clear if we write out how the variations of
\sigma
are related to the variations of
\Phi
and
A
. It follows from (
1.7
) that

\delta\sigma=\frac{\delta A}{2A}-\frac{A}{4}\Delta\delta\Phi\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad\delta\left(\frac{e^{2\sigma}}{A}\right)=-\frac{1}{2}\,\widehat{\Delta}\,\delta\Phi\ .

(1.8)

In terms of the Kähler potential
\Phi
, the area
A
and the conformal factor
\sigma
, the Mabuchi action on a Riemann surface of genus
{\tt g}
can then be written as
[
9
]

S_{\rm M}[\widehat{g},g]=\int{\rm d}^{2}x\sqrt{\widehat{g}}\left[2\pi({\tt g}-1)\Phi\,\widehat{\Delta}\,\Phi+\Bigl(\frac{8\pi(1-{\tt g})}{\widehat{A}}-\widehat{\cal R}\Bigr)\Phi+\frac{4}{A}\,\sigma\,e^{2\sigma}\right]\ ,

(1.9)

and the Aubin-Yau action as

S_{\rm AY}[\widehat{g},g]=-\int{\rm d}^{2}x\sqrt{\widehat{g}}\left[\frac{1}{4}\Phi\,\widehat{\Delta}\,\Phi-\frac{\Phi}{\widehat{A}}\right]\ .

(1.10)

As already mentioned, they both satisfy a cocycle identity analogous to (
1.3
) and were shown
[
9
]
to appear in the effective gravitational action of a (two-dimensional) massive scalar field in the term of first order in an expansion in
m^{2}A
. Note that we can rewrite the Mabuchi action as

S_{\rm M}=8\pi(1-{\tt g})S_{\rm AY}+\int{\rm d}^{2}x\sqrt{\widehat{g}}\left(\frac{4}{A}\,\sigma\,e^{2\sigma}-\widehat{\cal R}\Phi\right)\ .

(1.11)

Let us now give the variations of the Liouville, Mabuchi and Aubin-Yau actions under a conformal variation of the metric (equivalently encoded in
\delta\sigma
or in
\delta\Phi
and
\delta A
, at fixed
\widehat{g}
and hence fixed
\widehat{A}
). First, the Aubin-Yau action only depends on
\Phi
, not on
A
, and

\displaystyle\delta S_{\rm AY}[\widehat{g},g]\hskip-5.69054pt

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\hskip-5.69054pt-\int{\rm d}^{2}x\sqrt{\widehat{g}}\left[\frac{1}{2}\delta\Phi\,\widehat{\Delta}\,\Phi-\frac{\delta\Phi}{\widehat{A}}\right]=\frac{1}{A}\int{\rm d}^{2}x\sqrt{\widehat{g}}\left[-\frac{A}{2}\,\widehat{\Delta}\,\Phi+\frac{A}{\widehat{A}}\right]\delta\Phi=\frac{1}{A}\int{\rm d}^{2}x\sqrt{\widehat{g}}\,e^{2\sigma}\,\delta\Phi

(1.12)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{A}\int{\rm d}^{2}x\sqrt{g}\ \delta\Phi\ .

Next, we have

\displaystyle\delta\ \int{\rm d}^{2}x\sqrt{\widehat{g}}\left(\frac{4}{A}\sigma e^{2\sigma}-\widehat{\cal R}\Phi\right)=\int{\rm d}^{2}x\sqrt{\widehat{g}}\left(\delta\sigma\,\frac{4}{A}e^{2\sigma}+4\sigma\,\delta\Big(\frac{e^{2\sigma}}{A}\Big)-\widehat{\cal R}\,\delta\Phi\right)

\displaystyle=2\frac{\delta A}{A}-\int{\rm d}^{2}x\sqrt{\widehat{g}}\left(2\,\widehat{\Delta}\,\sigma+\widehat{\cal R}\right)\delta\Phi\ ,

(1.13)

where we used the fact that the Laplace operator
\widehat{\Delta}
can be freely integrated by parts since we consider only manifolds without boundaries. Finally, as is well-known, in two dimensions the curvature scalars
{\cal R}
and
\widehat{\cal R}
are related by

{\cal R}=e^{-2\sigma}\Big(\widehat{\cal R}+2\widehat{\Delta}\,\sigma\Big)\ .

(1.14)

This implies, in particular, that
\sqrt{g}\,{\cal R}=\sqrt{\widehat{g}}\,(\widehat{\cal R}+2\widehat{\Delta}\,\sigma)
.
We can then rewrite (
1.2
) as

\delta\ \int{\rm d}^{2}x\sqrt{\widehat{g}}\left(\frac{4}{A}\sigma e^{2\sigma}-\widehat{\cal R}\Phi\right)=2\frac{\delta A}{A}-\int{\rm d}^{2}x\sqrt{g}\,{\cal R}\,\delta\Phi\ .

(1.15)

Combining this with (
1.11
) and (
1.12
) gives the variation of the Mabuchi action as

\delta S_{\rm M}[\widehat{g},g]=2\frac{\delta A}{A}-\int{\rm d}^{2}x\sqrt{g}\,\left({\cal R}-\frac{8\pi(1-{\tt g})}{A}\right)\delta\Phi\ .

(1.16)

We see that the stationary points of the Mabuchi action are given by metrics of constant area
A
and constat curvature
{\cal R}=\frac{8\pi(1-{\tt g})}{A}
. Indeed, any Riemann surface of genus
{\tt g}
admits a constant curvature metric and then, since
\int\sqrt{g}\,{\cal R}=8\pi(1-{\tt g})
one necessarily has
{\cal R}=\frac{8\pi(1-{\tt g})}{A}
.

One has similarly for the variation of the Liouville action
S_{\rm Liouville}
given in (
1.4
).

\delta S_{\rm Liouville}[g,\widehat{g}]=\delta\,\int{\rm d}^{2}x\sqrt{\widehat{g}}\,\big(\sigma\widehat{\Delta}\sigma+\widehat{\cal R}\sigma\big)=\int{\rm d}^{2}x\sqrt{\widehat{g}}\,\big(2\delta\sigma\widehat{\Delta}\sigma+\widehat{\cal R}\delta\sigma\big)=\int{\rm d}^{2}x\sqrt{g}\,{\cal R}\,\delta\sigma\ .

(1.17)

This is the form of the variation which we will use later-on. But we can also re-express
\delta\sigma
in terms of
\delta A
and
\delta\Phi
so that

\delta S_{\rm Liouville}[g,\widehat{g}]=\int{\rm d}^{2}x\sqrt{g}\,R\Big(\frac{\delta A}{2A}-\frac{A}{4}\Delta\delta\Phi\Big)=4\pi(1-{\tt g})\,\frac{\delta A}{A}-\frac{A}{4}\int{\rm d}^{2}x\sqrt{g}\,\Delta{\cal R}\,\delta\Phi\ ,

(1.18)

which shows that the stationary points of the Liouville action at constant area
A
are given by metrics such that
\Delta{\cal R}=0
, i.e. by constant curvature metrics. As before, then necessarily
{\cal R}=\frac{8\pi(1-{\tt g})}{A}
.

We have seen that the Liouville and Mabuchi actions admit metrics of constant scalar curvature as “solutions of their equations of motion” under the constraint of fixed area. Although not obvious from the previous equation, the variation of the Aubin-Yau action when restricted to the space of Bergmann metrics is similarly related to metrics of constant scalar curvature
[
8
]
.

1.3
Recap : The gravitational action for a massless scalar field

Before embarking on the technical complications for the Majorana fermions, it is maybe good to quickly remind the reader of (parts of) the much simpler computation of the gravitational action for a massless (i.e. conformal) scalar field, leading to the Liouville action.

For a massless scalar field
\phi
on a 2 dimensional (euclidean) manifold without boundary, with metric
g_{\mu\nu}
, the action is

S[g,\phi]=\int{\rm d}^{2}x\sqrt{g}\,g^{\mu\nu}\nabla_{\mu}\phi\nabla_{\nu}\phi=-\int{\rm d}^{2}x\sqrt{g}\,\phi\Delta\phi\ ,

(1.19)

where, as in (
1.7
),

\Delta=\frac{1}{\sqrt{g}}\partial_{\nu}\big(\sqrt{g}\,g^{\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}\big)\equiv g^{\mu\nu}\nabla_{\nu}\nabla_{\mu}

is the scalar Laplace operator. It is hermitian with respect to the usual scalar product
(f,h)=\int{\rm d}^{2}x\sqrt{g}\,f^{*}(x)\,h(x)
. Consider the eigenvalue problem of
-\Delta
:

-\Delta f_{n}(x)=\lambda_{n}f_{n}(x)\ .

(1.20)

Since
\Delta
is a real operator these eigenfunctions can be chosen to be real. As always they can also be chosen to be orthonormal:

\int{\rm d}^{2}x\sqrt{g}\,f_{n}(x)f_{m}(x)=\delta_{nm}\ .

(1.21)

Of course, there are infinitely many eigenfunctions and eigenvalues, so that the index
n
runs over an infinite set (e.g. over the non-negative integers).
One can then see, since the action
S
is non-negative, that all eigenvalues are
\lambda_{n}\geq 0
. (This is why we consider
-\Delta
which is a positive operator, rather than
\Delta
.) Note that there is always a single zero-mode, i.e. an eigenfunction
f_{0}
with eigenvalue
\lambda_{0}=0
. Indeed, the constant function
f_{0}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{A}}
is such that
\partial_{\nu}f_{0}=0
and, hence,
-\Delta f_{0}=0
, and moreover
f_{0}
is correctly normalised. Conversely, if
\Delta f_{0}=0
one has
\int\sqrt{g}\,g^{\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}f_{0}\partial_{\nu}f_{0}=0
and then
f_{0}
must be constant.

One can then develop any
\phi(x)
on the basis of these eigenfunctions :

\phi(x)=\sum_{n}c_{n}f_{n}(x)\ .

(1.22)

Inserting this into the action and using the orthonormality of the
f_{n}
one gets

S[g,\phi]=\int{\rm d}^{2}x\sqrt{g}\phi(-\Delta\phi)=\sum_{n\neq 0}\lambda_{n}c_{n}^{2}\ .

(1.23)

To compute the so-called matter partition function (the field
\phi
is considered to be the matter) for fixed metric
g_{\mu\nu}
:

Z_{\rm matter}[g]=\int{\cal D}\phi\ e^{-S[g,\phi]}\ ,

(1.24)

we also need to define the functional integral measure
{\cal D}\phi
in such a way that one integrates over all possible field configurations
\phi(x)
. This is achieved via the expansion (
1.22
) by integrating over all the expansion coefficients
c_{k}
:

{\cal D}\phi=\sqrt{A}\int\prod_{k\neq 0}{\rm d}c_{k}\ ,

(1.25)

where we “arbitrarily” excluded the integration over the zero-mode coefficient
c_{0}
(since it would lead to an infinite factor in (
1.24
)), and inserted instead a factor
\sqrt{A}
.
Then

\int{\cal D}\phi\ e^{-S[g,\phi]}=\sqrt{A}\int\Big(\prod_{k\neq 0}{\rm d}c_{k}\Big)\ e^{-\sum_{n\neq 0}\lambda_{n}c_{n}^{2}}=\sqrt{A}\prod_{n\neq 0}\sqrt{\frac{\pi}{\lambda_{n}}}={\cal N}\,\sqrt{A}\,\big(\,{\rm Det}\,^{\prime}(-\Delta)\big)^{-1/2}\ ,

(1.26)

where
{\cal N}
is some (infinite) normalisation coefficient, and
\,{\rm Det}\,^{\prime}
denotes the determinant computed as the product of all non-zero eigenvalues. One thus has

Z_{\rm matter}[g]={\cal N}\sqrt{A}\big(\,{\rm Det}\,^{\prime}(-\Delta_{g})\big)^{-1/2}\ ,

(1.27)

where we added a subscript
g
on
\Delta
to remind us that this is the Laplacian for metric
g
.
The gravitational action is defined as

e^{-S_{\rm grav}[\widehat{g},g]}=\frac{Z_{\rm matter}[g]}{Z_{\rm matter}[\widehat{g}]}=\sqrt{\frac{A}{\widehat{A}}}\,\Big(\frac{\,{\rm Det}\,^{\prime}(-\Delta_{g})}{\,{\rm Det}\,^{\prime}(-\Delta_{\widehat{g}})}\Big)^{-1/2}\ ,

(1.28)

where the factors
{\cal N}
now have cancelled.

One can go on, and rewrite this again in terms of the eigenvalues as

\displaystyle S_{\rm grav}[\widehat{g},g]=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{n\neq 0}\log\lambda_{n}(g)-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{n\neq 0}\log\lambda_{n}(\widehat{g})-\frac{1}{2}\log\frac{A}{\widehat{A}}\ .

(1.29)

These sums over eigenvalues typically are divergent but are efficiently regularized (and ”renormalized”!) by the well-known technique of zeta-function regularisation
[
20
]
:
One introduces the zeta-function
6
6

6

Since
n=0
is excluded from the sum, this rather corresponds to what we would denote as
\widetilde{\zeta}_{g}
in the main part of this paper.

\zeta_{g}(s)
of the operator
-\Delta_{g}
as

\zeta_{g}(s)=\sum_{n\neq 0}\frac{1}{(\lambda_{n}(g))^{s}}\quad\Rightarrow\quad\zeta_{g}^{\prime}(0)=-\sum_{n\neq 0}\log\lambda_{n}(g)\ ,

(1.30)

so that

S_{\rm grav}[\widehat{g},g]=-\frac{1}{2}\zeta_{g}^{\prime}(0)+\frac{1}{2}\zeta_{\widehat{g}}^{\prime}(0)-\frac{1}{2}\log\frac{A}{\widehat{A}}\\
.

(1.31)

Although this is a nice expression, it is not very explicit unless we know all the eigenvalues and are able to compute (
1.30
). Instead, one determines the variation of the zeta function under an infinitesimal conformal variation of the metric and then tries to “integrate” these infinitesimal changes to get the finite difference (
1.31
). Obviously, from (
1.30
)

\delta\zeta_{g}(s)=-s\sum_{n\neq 0}\frac{\delta\lambda_{n}}{\lambda_{n}^{s+1}}\ ,

(1.32)

where
\lambda_{n}=\lambda_{n}(g)
and
\delta\lambda_{n}
is its variation when the metric is changed as
g\to e^{2\delta\sigma}g
.
Under such a variation of the metric, the Laplace operator
\Delta=e^{-2\sigma}\widehat{\Delta}
changes as
\Delta\to e^{-2\sigma-2\delta\sigma}\widehat{\Delta}=e^{-2\delta\sigma}\Delta=\Delta-2\delta\sigma\Delta
, i.e.
\delta\Delta=-2\delta\sigma\Delta
. Then there is a corresponding change
\delta\lambda_{n}
of the eigenvalues and change
\delta f_{n}
of the eigenfunctions. Comparing the eigenvalue equations for
-\Delta
and
-(\Delta+\delta\Delta)
we have

-(\Delta+\delta\Delta)(f_{n}+\delta f_{n})=(\lambda_{n}+\delta\lambda_{n})(f_{n}+\delta f_{n})\quad\Rightarrow\quad-\Delta\delta f_{n}-\delta\Delta f_{n}=\lambda_{n}\delta f_{n}+\delta\lambda_{n}f_{n}\ .

(1.33)

We multiply this last equation with
f_{n}
and integrate
\int{\rm d}^{2}x\sqrt{g}...
. Then the first term gives
-\int d^{2}x\sqrt{g}f_{n}\Delta\delta f_{n}=-\int d^{2}x\sqrt{g}(\Delta f_{n})\delta f_{n}=\lambda_{n}\int d^{2}x\sqrt{g}f_{n}\delta f_{n}
which cancels a corresponding term on the right-hand side. Thus we are left with

-\int{\rm d}^{2}x\sqrt{g}f_{n}(x)\ \delta\Delta f_{n}=\delta\lambda_{n}\int{\rm d}^{2}x\sqrt{g}(f_{n}(x))^{2}=\delta\lambda_{n}

(1.34)

Now, recall that
-(\delta\Delta)f_{n}=2\delta\sigma\Delta f_{n}=-2\delta\sigma\lambda_{n}f_{n}
so that

\delta\lambda_{n}=-2\lambda_{n}\int{\rm d}^{2}x\sqrt{g}\,\delta\sigma(x)\,(f_{n}(x))^{2}\ .

(1.35)

This is to be inserted into (
1.32
) :

\delta\zeta_{g}(s)=2s\sum_{n\neq 0}\frac{\int{\rm d}^{2}x\sqrt{g}\,\delta\sigma(x)\,(f_{n}(x))^{2}}{\lambda_{n}^{s}}=2s\int{\rm d}^{2}x\sqrt{g}\,\delta\sigma(x)\,\sum_{n\neq 0}\frac{(f_{n}(x))^{2}}{\lambda_{n}^{s}}

(1.36)

If we also define the (bi-) local zeta-function as

\zeta_{g}(s,x,y)=\sum_{n\neq 0}\frac{f_{n}(x)f_{n}(y)}{\lambda_{n}^{s}}\ ,

(1.37)

this can be rewritten as

\delta\zeta_{g}(s)=2s\int{\rm d}^{2}x\sqrt{g}\,\delta\sigma(x)\,\zeta_{g}(s,x,x)\ .

(1.38)

We now take the derivative with respect to
s
. This yields two terms, but at
s=0
only one of them contributes :

\delta\zeta^{\prime}_{g}(0)\equiv\frac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}s}\zeta_{g}(s)\big|_{s=0}=2\int{\rm d}^{2}x\sqrt{g}\,\delta\sigma(x)\,\zeta_{g}(0,x,x)\ .

(1.39)

There is a standard relation between the heat kernel
K(t,x,y)
for an operator and the corresponding local zeta function
\zeta(s,x,y)
:

K(t,x,y)=\sum_{n\neq 0}e^{-\lambda_{n}t}f_{n}(x)f_{n}(y)\quad,\quad\zeta(s,x,y)=\frac{1}{\Gamma(s)}\int_{0}^{\infty}{\rm d}t\,t^{s-1}\,K(t,x,y)\ .

(1.40)

Possible singularities of the integral arise from the integration region of small
t
and one can show that the integral defines an analytic function for
\Re s>1
which can be analytically continued to the whole complex
s
-plane except for poles at
s=1,0,-1,-2,\ldots
. However,
\Gamma(s)
also has poles at
s=0,-1,-2,\ldots
and dividing by
\Gamma(s)
cancels the poles of the integral, except at
s=1
. We see that
\zeta(s,x,y)
has a pole at
s=1
and that its finite values at
s=0,-1,-2,\ldots
are determined by the singularities of the integral due to the small-
t
behaviour of the heat kernel. But the small-
t
behaviour of the heat kernel is well-known, in particular

K(t,x,x)\sim_{t\to 0}\frac{1}{4\pi t}\Big(1+\frac{{\cal R}(x)}{6}\ t+{\cal O}(t^{2})\Big)-\frac{1}{A}\ ,\ .

(1.41)

where the
-\frac{1}{A}
comes from subtracting the zero-mode
f_{0}^{2}=\frac{1}{A}
which is not included in the sum (
1.40
).
From this one gets

\zeta(0,x,x)=\frac{1}{24\pi}{\cal R}(x)-\frac{1}{A}\ ,

(1.42)

which when inserted in (
1.39
) gives

\delta S_{\rm grav}[\widehat{g},g]\,=\,-\frac{1}{2}\delta\zeta_{g}^{\prime}(0)-\frac{\delta A}{2A}\,=\,-\frac{1}{24\pi}\int{\rm d}^{2}x\sqrt{g}\,\delta\sigma(x)\,{\cal R}(x)\ .

(1.43)

We recognise
\delta S_{\rm Liouville}
and conclude

S_{\rm grav}[\widehat{g},g]=-\frac{1}{24\pi}S_{\rm Liouville}[\widehat{g},g]\ .

(1.44)

This is a famous result, first obtained (using a different method) by Polyakov in 1981
[
6
]
.

2
The Dirac operator for Majorana spinors on a curved manifold

In this section we define the relevant Dirac operator
D
in 2 Euclidean dimensions, with a Majorana mass term, and relate its square
D^{2}
to the spinorial Laplace operator and scalar curvature. We discuss the eigenvalue problem of this Dirac operator and how it changes under (local) conformal transformations. Most importantly, we obtain a sufficient characterisation of the zero-modes of the massless Dirac operator and of the corresponding projectors on these zero-modes, which we will need in section 5. We first look at flat space, not only because it is simpler but also because it provides the formula valid in any given local orthonormal frame of the curved space. The material of the first part of this section is standard or already contained in
[
1
]
but we re-derive the relevant formula for a self-contained presentation.

2.1
Majorana spinors in flat 2D space :
\gamma
-matrices and Dirac operator

In two flat Euclidean dimensions we use labels
a=1,2
. Recall that the 3 Pauli matrices satisfy
\sigma_{j}\sigma_{k}=\delta_{jk}{\bf 1}+i\epsilon_{jkl}\sigma_{l}
. We can then choose two real, hermitian
\gamma
-matrices as

\gamma^{1}=\sigma_{x}\ ,\quad\gamma^{2}=\sigma_{z}\quad\Rightarrow\quad\{\gamma^{a},\gamma^{b}\}=2\delta^{ab}\,{\bf 1}\quad,\quad(\gamma^{a})^{\dagger}=\gamma^{a}\quad,\quad(\gamma^{a})^{T}=\gamma^{a}\quad,\quad(\gamma^{a})^{*}=\gamma^{a}\ .

(2.1)

The chirality matrix (the analogue of
\gamma_{5}
in 4 dimensions) then is

\gamma_{*}=i\gamma^{1}\gamma^{2}=\sigma_{y}\quad\Rightarrow\quad\gamma_{*}^{\dagger}=\gamma_{*}\quad,\quad\gamma_{*}^{T}=\gamma_{*}^{*}=-\gamma_{*}\ ,

(2.2)

and, of course,
\gamma_{*}
anticommutes with
\gamma^{a}
.
These are all
2\times 2
matrices and, hence, a spinor
\psi
has two components that are a priori complex.

In 2 Euclidean dimensions the “Lorentz” group is just the rotation group SO(2) which has a single generator
J^{12}
. In the spin-
\frac{1}{2}
representation as carried by the Dirac spinor this (hermitian) generator is
J^{12}=-\frac{i}{4}[\gamma^{1},\gamma^{2}]=-\frac{i}{2}\gamma^{1}\gamma^{2}=-\frac{1}{2}\gamma_{*}
and the representation of a finite rotation by an angle
\alpha
on a spinor
\psi
is given by the matrix

D(\alpha)=e^{i\alpha J^{12}}=e^{-i\alpha\gamma_{*}/2}
. More precisely, this means that under such a rotation, acting also on the coordinates
7
7

7

Below, on a curved manifold, we will consider separately the notions of covariance under rotations of the spinor and under transformations of the coordinates.

as
{x^{\prime}}^{a}=\Lambda^{a}_{\ b}x^{b}
, the spinor transforms as

\psi(x)\to\psi^{\prime}(x^{\prime})=D(\alpha)\psi(x)
.
Now, with the choice (
2.1
) of the
\gamma
-matrices, the matrix
D(\alpha)
is real and
\psi^{*}
transforms exactly as
\psi
. Furthermore since
\gamma_{*}
commutes with
D(\alpha)
,
\gamma_{*}\psi
also transforms as
\psi
. Finally, using the relation
D(\alpha)^{-1}\gamma^{\mu}D(\alpha)=\Lambda^{\mu}_{\ \rho}\,\gamma^{\rho}
one also shows that
({\partial\hskip-6.25958pt/}\psi)^{\prime}(x^{\prime})=D(\alpha){\partial\hskip-6.25958pt/}\psi(x)
.
Hence, all
\psi^{*}
,
\gamma_{*}\psi
and
{\partial\hskip-6.25958pt/}\psi
all transform exactly as
\psi
. In particular, it makes sense to impose the Majorana condition
\psi^{*}=\psi
, imposing that the two components
\psi_{1}
and
\psi_{2}
of
\psi
are both real, or write an equation combining
i{\partial\hskip-6.25958pt/}\psi
and
m\gamma_{*}\psi
.

Note that the spinors are anti-commuting objects obeying
\psi_{\alpha}\psi_{\beta}=-\psi_{\beta}\psi_{\alpha}
, and one has for a complex spinor
\psi^{\dagger}\psi=(\psi^{*})^{\tt T}\psi=\psi_{1}^{*}\psi_{1}+\psi_{2}^{*}\psi_{2}
. The complex conjugation of such an expression is defined as the hermitian conjugate and involves reversing the order of the terms, i.e.

(\chi_{a}\psi_{\beta})^{*}=\psi_{\beta}^{*}\chi_{\alpha}^{*}\ .

(2.3)

We then see that
(\psi_{1}^{*}\psi_{1})^{*}=\psi_{1}^{*}(\psi_{1}^{*})^{*}=\psi_{1}^{*}\psi_{1}
and
\psi^{\dagger}\psi
is indeed real, and
\int m\psi^{\dagger}\psi
is the standard (real) mass term in the action for a Dirac spinor

For anti-commuting Majorana, i.e. real spinors, however, a mass term like
\int m\psi^{\dagger}\psi=\int m\psi^{\tt T}\psi=m\int(\psi_{1}\psi_{1}+\psi_{2}\psi_{2})
vanishes. We can, nevertheless, introduce a non-vanishing, real mass-term as
\int\psi^{\dagger}m\gamma_{*}\psi=m\int(\psi_{1}(-i)\psi_{2}+\psi_{2}i\psi_{1})=-2im\int\psi_{1}\psi_{2}
. This is indeed real since
(\psi_{1}\psi_{2})^{*}=\psi_{2}\psi_{1}=-\psi_{1}\psi_{2}
. The action for such a Majorano spinor then reads

S=\int\psi^{\dagger}(i{\partial\hskip-6.25958pt/}+m\gamma_{*})\psi\ .

(2.4)

The corresponding Dirac operator

D=i{\partial\hskip-6.25958pt/}+m\gamma_{*}

(2.5)

is hermitian with respect to the standard inner product on the space of Dirac spinors,

(\chi,\psi)=\int\chi^{\dagger}(x)\psi(x)=\int(\chi_{1}^{*}\psi_{1}+\chi_{2}^{*}\psi_{2})=(\psi,\chi)^{*}\quad,\quad(O\chi,\psi)=(\chi,O^{\dagger}\psi)\ .

(2.6)

Indeed, taking the hermitian conjugate of
D
involves taking the hermitian conjugate of the
\gamma
matrices, (which we have chosen to be hermitian), complex conjugation of the
i
and an integration by parts of
\partial_{\mu}
. Hence

D^{\dagger}=D\ .

(2.7)

In particular, we also see that
S=(\psi,D\psi)=(D\psi,\psi)=(\psi,D\psi)^{*}=S^{*}
which confirms that the action is real.
Next, one has

D^{2}=(i{\partial\hskip-6.25958pt/}+m\gamma_{*})(i{\partial\hskip-6.25958pt/}+m\gamma_{*})=-{\partial\hskip-6.25958pt/}^{2}+im({\partial\hskip-6.25958pt/}\gamma_{*}+\gamma_{*}{\partial\hskip-6.25958pt/})+m^{2}=-{\partial\hskip-6.25958pt/}^{2}+m^{2}=-\partial_{\mu}\partial^{\mu}+m^{2}

which incorporates the correct Euclidean continuation of the mass-shell condition
p_{\mu}p^{\mu}+m^{2}=0
.

One might ask why we want to study Majorana spinors with the Dirac operator
D
and not simply (complex) Dirac spinors with an ordinary mass term and corresponding action

S=\int\psi^{\dagger}(i{\partial\hskip-6.25958pt/}+m)\psi
. However, the square of the corresponding Dirac operator
\widetilde{D}=i{\partial\hskip-6.25958pt/}+m
is
\widetilde{D}^{2}=(i{\partial\hskip-6.25958pt/}+m)^{2}=-\partial_{\mu}\partial^{\mu}+m^{2}+2im{\partial\hskip-6.25958pt/}
and this does
not
correspond to anything simple or physical. (It is
(i{\partial\hskip-6.25958pt/}-m)(i{\partial\hskip-6.25958pt/}+m)
that instead gives the mass-shell condition.) This is one of the reasons we will focus on the action (
2.4
) and corresponding Dirac operator (
2.5
).
Note that this Dirac operator
D

is a purely imaginary. Indeed, the
\gamma^{\mu}
are real and
\gamma_{*}
is purely imaginary. Thus
iD
is a real operator and the Dirac equation
D\psi=(i{\partial\hskip-6.25958pt/}+m\gamma_{*})\psi=0
admits real solutions
\psi
. However, we will be interested in the corresponding eigenvalue problem
D\psi_{n}\equiv(i{\partial\hskip-6.25958pt/}+m\gamma_{*})\psi_{n}=\lambda_{n}\psi_{n}
.
Since
D
is hermitian, the eigenvalues
\lambda_{n}
are real and then the
\psi_{n}
cannot be real, but must be complex. We will discuss this eigenvalue problem more generally below, on curved space.

2.2
Curved space :
\gamma
-matrices, covariant derivatives, Dirac operator and spinorial Laplacian

To define spinors on a curved space (-time) one needs to introduce locally orthonormal frames which is done by introducing the so-called viel-bein
e^{a}_{\mu}
. To define the Dirac operator one needs to define the covariant derivative
\nabla_{\mu}
which involves the spin connection
\omega^{ab}_{\mu}
which is related to the
e^{a}_{\mu}
by the zero-torsion condition. In this subsection we recall how this is done and we also establish a few useful relations. Of course, the equations in this subsection are well-known to supergravity practitioners, but some are still somewhat non-trivial, and we find it useful to re-derive them here for further reference. Since most of the discussion is not specific to two dimensions we first discuss arbitrary
n
-dimensional spaces (with Euclidean signature
8
8

8

The whole discussion can also be repeated for Lorentzian signature, but one would need to pay attention to the upper or lower positions of the flat indices
a,b,\ldots
also.

)
and only set
n=2
in the end. Our detailed discussion also allows us to make some statements about possible zero-modes of the Dirac operator that will be useful in the sequel.

2.2.1
Local orthonormal frames and spin connection (in
n
dimensions)

Starting from the metric tensor written using the coordinate one-forms
{\rm d}x^{\mu}
, one introduces the one-forms
e^{a}
of the local orthonormal frame as

g_{\mu\nu}(x)\,{\rm d}x^{\mu}\otimes{\rm d}x^{\nu}=\delta_{ab}\,e^{a}(x)\otimes e^{b}(x)\ .

(2.8)

Both sets of indices
\mu,\nu,\ldots
and
a,b,\ldots
take values
1,2,\ldots n
.
We can always express the
e^{a}
in terms of the
{\rm d}x^{\mu}
and vice versa:

e^{a}(x)=e^{a}_{\mu}(x)\,{\rm d}x^{\mu}\quad,\quad{\rm d}x^{\mu}=E^{\mu}_{b}(x)\,e^{b}(x)\quad,\quad e^{a}_{\mu}(x)\,E^{\mu}_{b}(x)=\delta^{a}_{b}\quad,\quad E^{\mu}_{b}(x)e^{b}_{\nu}(x)=\delta^{\mu}_{\nu}\ .

(2.9)

As indicated here, the viel-bein
e^{a}_{\mu}
and inverse viel-bein
E^{\mu}_{a}
depend on the point
x
on the manifold. Combining (
2.9
) and (
2.8
) results in the relations

g_{\mu\nu}\,E^{\mu}_{a}E^{\nu}_{b}=\delta_{ab}\quad,\quad\delta_{ab}\,e^{a}_{\mu}e^{b}_{\nu}=g_{\mu\nu}\quad\text{and}\quad\ \delta^{ab}E^{\mu}_{a}E^{\nu}_{b}=g^{\mu\nu}\quad,\quad g^{\mu\nu}e^{a}_{\mu}e^{b}_{\nu}=\delta^{ab}\ .

(2.10)

The
\delta_{ab}
is to be interpreted as the metric in the local orthonormal frames. The
e^{a}_{\mu}
and
E^{\mu}_{a}
implement the change of basis between these orthonormal local frames and the coordinate frames but, of course, in general they cannot be interpreted as Jacobian matrices for a change of coordinates. Since the metric in the orthonormal frame is
\delta_{ab}
and the inverse metric
\delta^{ab}=\delta_{ab}
, raising and lowering the corresponding indices
a,b,\ldots
has no effect and we write indifferently
T^{ab}\equiv T^{a}_{\ b}
etc. Of course this does not apply to the “coordinate indices”
\mu,\nu,\ldots
.

The relevant connection in the local orthonormal frame is the spin-connection
\omega^{ab}_{\mu}
. It is defined in terms of the connection coefficients
\Gamma^{c}_{ab}
in the local orthonormal frame
as
\omega^{a}_{\ b\mu}=\Gamma^{a}_{cb}e^{c}_{\mu}
. The
\Gamma^{c}_{ab}
can be obtained from the
\Gamma^{\rho}_{\nu\mu}
in the coordinate frame through the (inhomogeneous) transformation rules for the connection coefficients as
\Gamma^{c}_{ab}=E_{a}^{\mu}E_{b}^{\nu}\,\Gamma^{\rho}_{\mu\nu}e^{c}_{\rho}+E_{a}^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}E_{b}^{\rho}e^{c}_{\rho}
. However, since we will only work on Riemannian manifolds, where the torsion vanishes and the metric is covariantly constant, the spin-connection can be more easily obtained through the zero-torsion condition which is most compactly written as an equation for the spin-connection one-form
\omega^{ab}=\omega^{ab}_{\mu}{\rm d}x^{\mu}
using the exterior derivative
{\rm d}
:

{\rm d}e^{a}+\omega^{ab}\wedge e^{b}=0\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad\partial_{\mu}e^{a}_{\nu}-\partial_{\nu}e^{a}_{\mu}+\omega^{ab}_{\mu}e^{b}_{\nu}-\omega^{ab}_{\nu}e^{b}_{\mu}=0.

(2.11)

Requiring the metric to be covariantly constant implies that the spin-connection is antisymmetric in
a
and
b
:

\nabla_{\mu}g_{\nu\rho}=0\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad\nabla_{\mu}\delta_{ab}=0\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad\omega_{ab\mu}=-\omega_{ba\mu}\ .

(2.12)

Then there are
n\times\frac{n(n-1)}{2}
independent components
\omega^{ab}_{\mu}
, while (
2.11
) provides exactly the same number of equation, hence determinig the spin-connection completely in terms of the
e^{a}_{\mu}
. It is not too difficult to derive the following useful relations

\displaystyle\nabla_{\mu}e^{a}_{\nu}

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle\partial_{\mu}e^{a}_{\nu}+\omega^{ab}_{\mu}e^{b}_{\nu}-\Gamma^{\rho}_{\mu\nu}e^{a}_{\rho}=0\ ,

\displaystyle\nabla_{\mu}E_{a}^{\nu}

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle\partial_{\mu}E_{a}^{\nu}+\Gamma^{\nu}_{\mu\rho}E^{\rho}_{a}-E^{\nu}_{b}\omega^{ba}_{\mu}=0\ .

(2.13)

Finally we note that we obtain the Riemann curvature tensor and curvature 2-form from the spin connection as

R^{ab}\equiv\frac{1}{2}R^{ab}_{\ \ \mu\nu}{\rm d}x^{\mu}\wedge{\rm d}x^{\nu}={\rm d}\omega^{ab}+\omega^{ac}\wedge\omega^{cb}\quad,\quad\ R^{a}_{\ b\mu\nu}=e^{a}_{\lambda}E_{b}^{\rho}R^{\lambda}_{\ \rho\mu\nu}.

(2.14)

Let us now discuss the Dirac matrices.
The Dirac-matrices
\gamma^{a}
of the previous subsection satisfied the Clifford algebra
\{\gamma^{a},\gamma^{b}\}=2\delta^{ab}{\bf 1}
as appropriate in flat space.
This means that they are also the appropriate Dirac matrices in the local orthonormal frames. To avoid confusion, we temporarily denote these Dirac-matrices as
\tilde{\gamma}^{a}
so that

\{\tilde{\gamma}^{a},\tilde{\gamma}^{b}\}=2\delta^{ab}{\bf 1}

(2.15)

One then defines the curved-space Dirac matrices as

\gamma^{\mu}=E^{\mu}_{a}\,\tilde{\gamma}^{a}\quad\Rightarrow\quad\{\gamma^{\mu},\gamma^{\nu}\}=E^{\mu}_{a}E^{\nu}_{b}\,\{\tilde{\gamma}^{a},\tilde{\gamma}^{b}\}=E^{\mu}_{a}E^{\nu}_{b}\,2\delta^{ab}{\bf 1}=2g^{\mu\nu}{\bf 1}\ .

(2.16)

We assume again that all
n
matrices
\tilde{\gamma}^{a}
are chosen to be hermitian so that also

(\gamma^{\mu})^{\dagger}=\gamma^{\mu}\ .

(2.17)

To simplify the notation, we will usually not put the tilde over the flat-space
\gamma
-matrices with the convention that
\gamma^{\mu},\gamma^{\nu},\gamma^{\rho}
etc are always curved-space
\gamma
-matrices and
\gamma^{a},\gamma^{b},\gamma^{c}
etc are always flat-space
\gamma
-matrices. The only potential source of confusion comes when the indices take numerical values, but we will always use the convention that
\gamma^{1},\ \gamma^{2},\ldots
are flat-space
\gamma
-matrices.

2.2.2
Covariant derivatives (in
n
dimensions)

Let us now recall how to construct the appropriate covariant derivative acting a the spinor
\psi
. A spinor transforms (locally) in the spin representation of the local Lorentz group
SO(n-1,1)
or at present - since we are in Euclidean signature - the local rotation group
SO(n)
as

\psi(x)\to\psi^{\prime}(x)=L_{\rm Dirac}(x)\psi(x)\equiv\exp\Big(\frac{i}{2}\theta^{cd}(x)J^{\rm Dirac}_{cd}\Big)\psi(x)\ ,

(2.18)

where the
\theta^{cd}(x)=-\theta^{dc}(x)
are the (local) rotation angles in the
cd
plane, and the
J^{\rm Dirac}_{cd}=-J^{\rm Dirac}_{dc}
are the corresponding hermitian
SO(n)
generators in the spin / Dirac representation

J^{\rm Dirac}_{cd}=-\frac{i}{2}\gamma_{cd}\equiv-\frac{i}{4}[\gamma_{c},\gamma_{d}]\quad,\quad(J^{\rm Dirac}_{cd})^{\dagger}=J^{\rm Dirac}_{cd}

(2.19)

It follows from the Clifford algebra satisfied by the
\gamma^{a}
that the
J^{\rm Dirac}_{cd}
indeed satisfy the
SO(n)
algebra

[J_{cd},J_{ef}]=-i\big(\delta_{de}J_{cf}-\delta_{df}J_{ce}-\delta_{ce}J_{df}+\delta_{cf}J_{de}\big)\ .

(2.20)

Note that the local
SO(n)
transformations (
2.18
) do
not
act on the coordinates : we have the same
x
before and after the transformation.
The corresponding local
SO(n)
transformation of the basis forms
e^{a}
is

e^{a}(x)\to{e^{\prime}}^{a}(x)=\big(L_{\rm vect}(x)\big)^{ab}\ e^{b}(x)=\Big[\exp\Big(\frac{i}{2}\theta^{cd}(x)J^{\rm vect}_{cd}\Big)\Big]^{ab}\ e^{b}(x)\ ,

(2.21)

where

(J^{\rm vect}_{cd})^{ab}=-i\big(\delta^{a}_{c}\delta^{b}_{d}-\delta^{b}_{c}\delta^{a}_{d}\big)\ .

(2.22)

are the purely imaginary and anti-symmetric, hence hermitian, generators of the vector representation of
SO(n)
. Of course, they also satisfy the
SO(n)
-algebra (
2.20
).

How does the spin-connection transform under such a local
SO(n)
transformation? The torsion being a tensor, its vanishing cannot be affected by a (local) change of basis. Hence, in the new frame we must still have

{\rm d}{e^{\prime}}^{a}+{\omega^{\prime}}^{ab}{e^{\prime}}^{b}=0
.
Using
{\rm d}{e^{\prime}}^{a}={\rm d}\big(L^{ab}e^{b})={\rm d}L^{ab}e^{b}+L^{ab}{\rm d}e^{b}=\big({\rm d}L^{ac}-L^{ab}\omega^{bc}\big)e^{c}
, we get

{\omega^{\prime}}^{ab}=L^{ac}\omega^{cd}(L^{-1})^{db}-({\rm d}L^{ac})(L^{-1})^{cb}=L^{ac}\omega^{cd}(L^{-1})^{db}+L^{ac}({\rm d}L^{-1})^{cb}\ ,

(2.23)

showing that the
\omega^{ab}
transform exactly as a gauge field in the adjoint representation of the local rotation group. Indeed, the matrices
L
appearing in (
2.23
)
are what we above called
L_{\rm vect}
and we have

\omega^{ab}=\frac{i}{2}\omega^{cd}(J^{\rm vect}_{cd})^{ab}
. But the transformation of the
\omega^{cd}_{\mu}
cannot not depend on the representation we choose, so that we also have

\frac{i}{2}{\omega^{\prime}}^{cd}(x)J^{\rm Dirac}_{cd}=\frac{i}{2}\omega^{cd}(x)\,L_{\rm Dirac}(x)\,J^{\rm Dirac}_{cd}\,L^{-1}_{\rm Dirac}(x)+L_{\rm Dirac}(x)){\rm d}L^{-1}_{\rm Dirac}(x)\ .

(2.24)

The covariant derivative
\nabla_{\mu}
of a spinor
\psi(x)
should be defined, as usual, such that under a local
SO(n)
rotation
\nabla_{\mu}\psi(x)
transforms exactly as
\psi(x)
.
If we define

\nabla_{\mu}\psi=\partial_{\mu}\psi+\frac{i}{2}\omega^{cd}_{\mu}J^{\rm Dirac}_{cd}\psi\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad\nabla_{\mu}\psi_{\alpha}=\partial_{\mu}\psi_{\alpha}+\frac{i}{2}\omega^{cd}_{\mu}(J^{\rm Dirac}_{cd})_{\alpha\beta}\psi_{\beta}

(2.25)

it is straightforward to check, using (
2.18
) and (
2.24
) that we have indeed

\nabla^{\prime}_{\mu}\psi^{\prime}(x)\equiv\big(\partial_{\mu}+\frac{i}{2}{\omega^{\prime}}^{cd}_{\mu}(x)J^{\rm Dirac}_{cd}\big)\big(L_{\rm Dirac}(x)\psi(x)\big)=L_{\rm Dirac}(x)\nabla_{\mu}\psi(x)\ ,

(2.26)

where
\nabla^{\prime}_{\mu}
involves
{\omega^{\prime}}^{cd}_{\mu}
. Note again that
x
is unchanged so that
\nabla^{\prime}_{\mu}
involves
\partial_{\mu}
and not some
\partial^{\prime}_{\mu}
.
Later-on we will also encounter covariant derivatives acting on other objects transforming in different representations and, accordingly, these covariant derivatives will include other connection terms. If we want to insist that we are dealing exactly with the covariant derivative acting on the spinor as just defined we will write
\nabla_{\mu}^{\rm sp}
so that

\nabla_{\mu}^{\rm sp}\equiv\partial_{\mu}+\frac{i}{2}\omega^{cd}_{\mu}J^{\rm Dirac}_{cd}=\partial_{\mu}+\frac{1}{4}\omega^{cd}_{\mu}\gamma_{cd}\quad,\qquad\nabla_{\mu}\psi=\nabla_{\mu}^{\rm sp}\psi\ .

(2.27)

As a general rule, a covariant derivative
\nabla_{\mu}
acting on some quantity
F_{(i)}
transforming in a representation
{\cal R}_{i}
of the local rotation group is alway defined in such a way that
\nabla_{\mu}F_{(i)}
transforms in the same representation
{\cal R}_{i}
. Just as we wrote
\nabla^{\rm sp}
to emphasise this fact we could write
\nabla_{\mu}^{(i)}
, but one usually doesn’t. It follows from this remark, that the covariant derivative obeys the Leibnitz rule,

\nabla_{\mu}(F_{(i)}F_{(j)})=(\nabla_{\mu}F_{(i)})F_{(j)}+F_{(i)}\nabla_{\mu}F_{(j)}\ .

(2.28)

It similarly follows that in an integral of a quantity that is a scalar under the local rotation group, the covariant derivatives can be freely integrated by parts (possibly up to a boundary term).
One application of these rules is the following identity we will need later-on

-\int\sqrt{g}\,(\nabla_{\mu}f)\psi^{\dagger}\gamma^{\mu}\chi=\int\sqrt{g}\,f\,\big((\nabla_{\mu}^{\rm sp}\psi)^{\dagger}\gamma^{\mu}\chi+\psi^{\dagger}\gamma^{\mu}\nabla_{\mu}^{\rm sp}\chi\big)\ ,

(2.29)

where
f=f(x)
is a scalar (so that
\nabla_{\mu}f=\partial_{\mu}f
) and
\psi=\psi(x)
and
\chi=\chi(x)
are Dirac (or Majorana) spinors. It is relatively straightforward to check this identity explicitly by integrating by parts, using
\frac{\partial_{\mu}\sqrt{g}}{\sqrt{g}}=\Gamma_{\mu\sigma}^{\sigma}
,
\gamma^{\mu}=E^{\mu}_{a}\gamma^{a}
and the relations
(
2.2.1
), as well as the hermiticity of the
J_{cd}^{\rm Dirac}
and the commutation relation of the latter with the
\gamma^{a}
.

Let us insist that we have discussed local, i.e.
x
-dependent frame transformations by some
L(x)
in the appropriate representation of
SO(n)
. We have not made any coordinate transformation. In the present formalism, local
SO(n)
transformations and coordinate transformations are totally decoupled. One may, of course, also do a coordinate transformation
x^{\mu}\to{x^{\prime}}^{\mu}=f^{\mu}(x^{\nu})
. Under such a coordinate transformation the spinor
\psi
, as well as all frame vectors like
v^{a}
are inert, i.e. behave as scalar quantities. For a vector e.g. one goes from the
v^{a}
to the
v^{\mu}
by the appropriate change of basis, i.e.
v^{\mu}=E^{\mu}_{a}v^{a}
and it is the transformation of the
E^{\mu}_{a}
under coordinate transformations, namely
{E^{\prime}}^{\mu}_{a}=\frac{\partial{x^{\prime}}^{\mu}}{\partial x^{\nu}}E^{\nu}_{a}
, that implies that the
v^{\mu}
will indeed transform as the components of a coordinate vector, i.e.
{v^{\prime}}^{\mu}=\frac{\partial{x^{\prime}}^{\mu}}{\partial x^{\nu}}v^{\nu}
.

Obviously, both
\partial_{\mu}
and
\omega^{ab}_{\mu}
transform under coordinate transformations as the covariant components of a coordinate vector and, hence, so does
\nabla_{\mu}\psi
. Let us temporarily denote the quantities in the
x^{\prime}
coordinates by a tilde (rather than a prime which we used for the local
SO(n)
transformations). Since
\psi
behaves as a scalar quantity under these coordinate transformation we then have
\tilde{\psi}(x^{\prime})=\psi(x)
and thus indeed

\tilde{\nabla}_{\mu}\tilde{\psi}(x^{\prime})=\frac{\partial x^{\nu}}{\partial{x^{\prime}}^{\mu}}\nabla_{\nu}\psi(x)\ .

(2.30)

Next, the Dirac operator should be the obvious curved space generalisation of (
2.5
), i.e.
D=i{\nabla\hskip-8.53581pt/}+m\gamma_{*}
where we define the operator
{\nabla\hskip-8.53581pt/}
as

{\nabla\hskip-8.53581pt/}=\gamma^{\mu}\,\nabla_{\mu}=\gamma^{a}E_{a}^{\mu}\,\nabla_{\mu}\quad\Rightarrow\quad{\nabla\hskip-8.53581pt/}\,\psi\equiv{\nabla\hskip-8.53581pt/}^{\rm\;sp}\psi=\gamma^{\mu}\,\nabla_{\mu}^{\rm sp}\psi=\gamma^{a}E_{a}^{\mu}\,\nabla_{\mu}^{\rm sp}\psi\ .

(2.31)

We have just seen that under coordinate transformations
\nabla_{\mu}^{\rm sp}\psi
transforms as the covariant components of a coordinate vector, while
E^{\mu}_{a}
transforms as the contravariant components. Of course, the
\gamma^{a}
are just numerical matrices that do not change. Hence,
{\nabla\hskip-8.53581pt/}\,\psi
is a scalar under coordinate transformations, just as
\psi
.
Next, under a local Lorentz transformation
L
, we have seen that
\nabla_{\mu}^{\rm sp}\psi
transforms with
L_{\rm Dirac}
while
{E^{\prime}}^{\mu}_{a}=(L_{\rm vect})_{ab}E^{\mu}_{b}
. Hence
9
9

9

Note that
L_{\rm Dirac}
, just as
\gamma^{a}
, are matrices while
(L_{\rm vect})_{ab}
are the components of the matrix
L_{\rm vect}
. Hence,
(L_{\rm vect})_{ab}
commutes with
L_{\rm Dirac}
and with
\gamma^{a}
. Also, just as in flat space, we have

L_{\rm Dirac}^{-1}\gamma^{a}L_{\rm Dirac}=(L_{\rm vect})_{ac}\gamma^{c}
.

\displaystyle{\nabla\hskip-8.53581pt/}\,\psi(x)=\gamma^{a}E^{\mu}_{a}(x)\nabla_{\mu}^{\rm sp}\psi(x)\ \ \to\ \ \gamma^{a}{E^{\prime}}^{\mu}_{a}(x)\nabla^{{}^{\prime}\rm sp}_{\mu}\psi^{\prime}(x)=\gamma^{a}\,\big((L_{\rm vect})_{ab}E^{\mu}_{b}\big)\,\big(L_{\rm Dirac}\nabla_{\mu}^{\rm sp}\psi(x)\big)

\displaystyle=(L_{\rm vect})_{ab}\ L_{\rm Dirac}\ \big(L_{\rm Dirac}^{-1}\gamma^{a}L_{\rm Dirac}\big)\,E^{\mu}_{b}\,\nabla_{\mu}^{\rm sp}\psi(x)=(L_{\rm vect})_{ab}\ L_{\rm Dirac}\ (L_{\rm vect})_{ac}\gamma^{c}\,E^{\mu}_{b}\,\nabla_{\mu}^{\rm sp}\psi(x)

\displaystyle=L_{\rm Dirac}\ \gamma^{b}E^{\mu}_{b}\,\nabla_{\mu}^{\rm sp}\psi(x)=L_{\rm Dirac}\ {\nabla\hskip-8.53581pt/}\,\psi(x)\ .

(2.32)

This means that under both, coordinate transformations and local Lorentz transformations,
{\nabla\hskip-8.53581pt/}^{\rm\;sp}\psi
transforms exactly as
\psi
. In particular, this implies that the appropriate covariant derivative
\nabla_{\nu}
acting on
{\nabla\hskip-8.53581pt/}\,\psi
is again
\nabla^{\rm sp}_{\nu}
:

\nabla_{\nu}{\nabla\hskip-8.53581pt/}\,\psi=\nabla^{\rm sp}_{\nu}{\nabla\hskip-8.53581pt/}\,\psi=\nabla^{\rm sp}_{\nu}{\nabla\hskip-8.53581pt/}^{\rm\;sp}\psi\quad\Rightarrow\quad{\nabla\hskip-8.53581pt/}\,{\nabla\hskip-8.53581pt/}\,\psi={\nabla\hskip-8.53581pt/}^{\rm\;sp}{\nabla\hskip-8.53581pt/}^{\rm\;sp}\,\psi=\gamma^{\mu}\nabla^{\rm sp}_{\mu}\gamma^{\nu}\nabla^{\rm sp}_{\nu}\,\psi\ .

(2.33)

However,
{\nabla\hskip-8.53581pt/}\,{\nabla\hskip-8.53581pt/}\,\psi
is
not

\gamma^{\mu}\gamma^{\nu}\nabla_{\mu}^{\rm sp}\nabla_{\nu}^{\rm sp}\psi
. Instead one has, of course

{\nabla\hskip-8.53581pt/}\,{\nabla\hskip-8.53581pt/}\,\psi=\gamma^{\mu}\nabla_{\mu}\big(\gamma^{\nu}\nabla_{\nu}^{\rm sp}\psi\big)
and the question is how to “commute” the
\nabla_{\mu}
with the
\gamma^{\nu}
. On the one hand,
from the usual rules about how the covariant derivative should act on coordinate and frame indices we have

\nabla_{\mu}\gamma^{a}=\partial_{\mu}\gamma^{a}+\omega_{\mu}^{ab}\gamma^{b}=\omega_{\mu}^{ab}\gamma^{b}\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad\nabla_{\mu}\gamma^{\nu}=\partial_{\mu}\gamma^{\nu}+\Gamma^{\nu}_{\mu\rho}\gamma^{\rho}\ ,

(2.34)

where the equivalence of both relations is due to
E^{\mu}_{a}
being covariantly constant, cf (
2.2.1
). On the other hand,
\nabla_{\mu}
also is a matrix, just like the
\gamma^{\nu}
, and one must also take into account the non-vanishing commutator. This then leads to

\nabla_{\mu}(\gamma^{\nu}\nabla_{\nu}\psi)=\gamma^{\nu}\nabla_{\mu}\nabla_{\nu}\psi\ ,

(2.35)

where on the right-hand side the covariant derivatives are, of course,
\nabla_{\mu}\nabla_{\nu}\psi=\nabla_{\mu}^{\rm sp}\nabla_{\nu}^{\rm sp}\psi-\Gamma_{\mu\nu}^{\rho}\nabla_{\rho}^{\rm sp}\psi
. Formally, the
\nabla_{\mu}
just passes through the
\gamma^{\nu}
. One could say that the contribution from the commutator cancels the contribution from
(\nabla_{\mu}\gamma^{\nu})
! In any case, we have

{\nabla\hskip-8.53581pt/}\,{\nabla\hskip-8.53581pt/}\,\psi\equiv(\gamma^{\mu}\nabla_{\mu})(\gamma^{\nu}\nabla_{\nu})\psi=\gamma^{\mu}\gamma^{\nu}\nabla_{\mu}\nabla_{\nu}\psi=\gamma^{\mu}\gamma^{\nu}\Big(\nabla_{\mu}^{\rm sp}\nabla_{\nu}^{\rm sp}-\Gamma_{\mu\nu}^{\rho}\nabla_{\rho}^{\rm sp}\Big)\psi\ .

(2.36)

Next, using
\gamma^{\mu}\gamma^{\nu}=g^{\mu\nu}+\gamma^{\mu\nu}
(where, of course,

\gamma^{\mu\nu}=\frac{1}{2}[\gamma^{\mu},\gamma^{\nu}]
) we have

{\nabla\hskip-8.53581pt/}\,{\nabla\hskip-8.53581pt/}\,\psi=\gamma^{\mu}\gamma^{\nu}\nabla_{\mu}\nabla_{\nu}\psi=g^{\mu\nu}\nabla_{\mu}\nabla_{\nu}\psi+\gamma^{\mu\nu}\nabla_{\mu}\nabla_{\nu}\psi=g^{\mu\nu}\nabla_{\mu}\nabla_{\nu}\psi+\frac{1}{2}\gamma^{\mu\nu}[\nabla_{\mu},\nabla_{\nu}]\psi\ .

(2.37)

The commutator of two covariant derivatives is related to the curvature tensor as

[\nabla_{\mu},\nabla_{\nu}]=\frac{i}{2}R_{\mu\nu}^{\ \ cd}J_{cd}\ ,

(2.38)

where
J_{cd}
are the
SO(n)
generators in the appropriate representation.
10
10

10

For a vector e.g. one has

[\nabla_{\mu},\nabla_{\nu}]v^{a}=\frac{i}{2}R_{\mu\nu}^{\ \ cd}(J_{cd}^{\rm vect})^{ab}v^{b}=\frac{1}{2}R_{\mu\nu}^{\ \ cd}(\delta_{c}^{a}\delta_{d}^{b}-\delta_{d}^{a}\delta_{c}^{b})v^{b}=R_{\mu\nu}^{\ \ ab}v^{b}

which is indeed the familiar formula.

When acting on
\psi
the generators are those in the Dirac representation and

[\nabla_{\mu},\nabla_{\nu}]\psi=\frac{i}{2}R_{\mu\nu}^{\ \ cd}(J_{cd}^{\rm Dirac})\psi=\frac{1}{4}R_{\mu\nu}^{\ \ cd}\gamma_{cd}\psi\ .

(2.39)

Inserting this into (
2.37
) we get

{\nabla\hskip-8.53581pt/}\,{\nabla\hskip-8.53581pt/}\,\psi=\Delta_{\rm sp}\,\psi+\frac{1}{8}\gamma^{\mu\nu}R_{\mu\nu}^{\ \ cd}\gamma_{cd}\psi\ .

(2.40)

where we defined the spinoral Laplace operator as

\begin{array}[]{|c|}\hline\cr\\
\displaystyle\Delta_{\rm sp}=g^{\mu\nu}\Big(\nabla_{\mu}^{\rm sp}\nabla_{\nu}^{\rm sp}-\Gamma_{\mu\nu}^{\rho}\nabla_{\rho}^{\rm sp}\Big)\ .\\
\\
\hline\cr\end{array}

(2.41)

Finally, in any even dimension
n
, there is an appropriate chirality matrix
\gamma_{*}\sim\frac{1}{n!}\epsilon_{\mu_{1}\ldots\mu_{n}}\gamma^{\mu_{1}}\ldots\gamma^{\mu_{n}}=\frac{1}{n!}\epsilon_{a_{1}\ldots a_{n}}\gamma^{a_{1}}\ldots\gamma^{a_{n}}
. This shows that this chirality matrix is the same as one defines in flat space. We have
\{\gamma_{*},\gamma^{a}\}=\{\gamma_{*},\gamma^{\mu}\}=0
. Including if necessary
11
11

11

In Euclidean signature one has
\gamma_{*}=i\gamma^{1}\ldots\gamma^{n}
for
n=4k+2
and
\gamma_{*}=\gamma^{1}\ldots\gamma^{n}
for
n=4k
.

a factor of
i
we have
\gamma_{*}^{2}={\bf 1}
and
\gamma_{*}^{\dagger}=\gamma_{*}
(provided one has chosen hermitian
\gamma^{a}
as we did). One can then define the hermitian, massive Dirac operator as the curved-space generalisation of (
2.5
) :

D=i{\nabla\hskip-8.53581pt/}+m\gamma_{*}\ .

(2.42)

Since
J_{cd}^{\rm Dirac}
commutes with
\gamma_{*}
we see that

\nabla_{\mu}^{\rm sp}
commutes with
\gamma_{*}
and the operator
{\nabla\hskip-8.53581pt/}=\gamma^{\mu}\nabla_{\mu}^{\rm sp}
anticommutes with
\gamma_{*}
.
We then have

\begin{array}[]{|c|}\hline\cr\\
\displaystyle D^{2}=-{\nabla\hskip-8.53581pt/}\,{\nabla\hskip-8.53581pt/}+m^{2}=-\Delta_{\rm sp}-\frac{1}{8}\gamma^{\mu\nu}R_{\mu\nu}^{\ \ cd}\gamma_{cd}+m^{2}\ .\\
\\
\hline\cr\end{array}

(2.43)

2.2.3
Back to two dimensions

In two dimensions the general formula simplify drastically. The local rotation group
SO(2)
is abelian and only has a single generator
J_{12}
. In its Dirac representation there is only
J_{12}^{\rm Dirac}=-\frac{i}{2}\gamma_{12}=-\frac{i}{2}\gamma_{1}\gamma_{2}=-\frac{1}{2}\gamma_{*}
as already discussed in subsection 2.1.
Similarly, the spin-connection
\omega^{ab}
also is just a single one-form
\omega^{12}=-\omega^{21}
. We then have

\omega^{cd}_{\mu}\gamma_{cd}=2\omega^{12}_{\mu}\gamma_{12}=-2i\omega^{12}_{\mu}\gamma_{*}\ .

(2.44)

We find it convenient to define

\omega_{\mu}=2\omega^{12}_{\mu}
,
so that

\begin{array}[]{|c|}\hline\cr\\
\displaystyle\nabla_{\mu}^{\rm sp}=\partial_{\mu}-\frac{i}{4}\omega_{\mu}\gamma_{*}\quad,\quad\omega_{\mu}=2\omega^{12}_{\mu}\ .\\
\\
\hline\cr\end{array}

(2.45)

Note that since
\gamma_{*}=\sigma_{y}
is purely imaginary,
\nabla_{\mu}^{\rm sp}
is a real (anti-hermitian) differential operator. Obviously, it is a
2\times 2
-matrix, and so is
{\nabla\hskip-8.53581pt/}=\gamma^{\mu}\,\nabla_{\mu}^{\rm sp}=\gamma^{a}E_{a}^{\mu}\,\nabla_{\mu}^{\rm sp}
.

Moreover, in two dimensions
\gamma^{\mu\nu}R_{\mu\nu}^{\ \ cd}\gamma_{cd}
simplifies as

\gamma^{\mu\nu}R_{\mu\nu}^{\ \ cd}\gamma_{cd}=\gamma^{ab}R_{ab}^{\ \ cd}\gamma_{cd}=4\gamma^{12}R_{12}^{\ \ 12}\gamma_{12}=4(-i\gamma_{*})^{2}R_{12}^{\ \ 12}=-4R_{12}^{\ \ 12}=-2{\cal R}\ .

(2.46)

where
{\cal R}=R_{12}^{\ \ 12}+R_{21}^{\ \ 21}=2R_{12}^{\ \ 12}
is the scalar Ricci curvature.
Thus

\begin{array}[]{|c|}\hline\cr\\
\displaystyle D^{2}=-{\nabla\hskip-8.53581pt/}\,{\nabla\hskip-8.53581pt/}+m^{2}=-\Delta_{\rm sp}+\frac{1}{4}{\cal R}+m^{2}\ ,\\
\\
\hline\cr\end{array}

(2.47)

where the spinorial Laplacian
\Delta_{\rm sp}
was defined in (
2.41
).
Just as
\nabla_{\mu}^{\rm sp}
, the spinorial Laplacian is a
2\times 2
-matrix differential operator. More precisely, it has a piece proportional to the identity matrix and involving the scalar Laplacian
and a piece proportional to
\gamma_{*}
:

\Delta_{\rm sp}={\bf 1}_{2\times 2}\Big(\Delta_{\rm scalar}-\frac{1}{16}\omega^{\mu}\omega_{\mu}\Big)-\frac{i}{4}\,\gamma_{*}\,\Big((\nabla_{\mu}\omega^{\mu})+2\omega^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}\Big)\ ,

(2.48)

where the scalar Laplacian is defined by (
1.7
), or equivalently by

\Delta_{\rm scalar}=g^{\mu\nu}(\partial_{\mu}\partial_{\nu}-\Gamma^{\rho}_{\mu\nu}\partial_{\rho})\ .

(2.49)

The hermitian, massive Dirac operator
D=i{\nabla\hskip-8.53581pt/}+m\gamma_{*}
was already defined in (
2.42
). With our 2-dimensional real
\gamma^{a}
it is moreover purely imaginary.
For later reference, let us note that with our conventions it is very explicitly given by

D=i\sigma_{x}{\cal D}_{1}+i\sigma_{z}{\cal D}_{2}+m\sigma_{y}\quad,\quad{\cal D}_{1}=E_{1}^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}-\frac{1}{4}E_{2}^{\mu}\omega_{\mu}\quad,\quad{\cal D}_{2}=E_{2}^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}+\frac{1}{4}E_{1}^{\mu}\omega_{\mu}\ .

(2.50)

The eigenvalue problem of
D
is

D\psi_{n}\equiv(i{\nabla\hskip-8.53581pt/}+m\gamma_{*})\psi_{n}=\lambda_{n}\psi_{n}\ .

(2.51)

Since
D
is hermitian, its eigenvalues
\lambda_{n}
are real. On the other hand,
D
is purely imaginary and then the eigenfunctions
\psi_{n}
cannot be real (unless
\lambda_{n}=0
). Taking the complex conjugate of (
2.51
) we see that
\psi_{n}^{*}
is also an eigenfunction but with eigenvalue
-\lambda_{n}
:

D\psi_{n}^{*}=-\lambda_{n}\psi_{n}^{*}\ .

(2.52)

For all
\lambda_{n}\neq 0
, within any couple
(\psi_{n},\psi_{n}^{*})
we decide to call
\psi_{n}
the eigenfunction with the positive eigenvalue. With this convention we always have

\lambda_{n}\geq 0\ .

(2.53)

We let

\psi_{n}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(\chi_{n}+i\phi_{n})\ ,

(2.54)

with real
\chi_{n}
and
\phi_{n}

and then taking the real and imaginary parts of (
2.51
) gives

\begin{array}[]{|c|}\hline\cr\\
\displaystyle D\chi_{n}=i\lambda_{n}\phi_{n}\quad,\quad D\phi_{n}=-i\lambda_{n}\chi_{n}\ .\\
\\
\hline\cr\end{array}

(2.55)

Of course, the
\chi_{n}
,
\ \phi_{n}
,
\ \psi_{n}
and
\psi_{n}^{*}
are all eigenfunctions of
D^{2}
with eigenvalue
\lambda_{n}^{2}
:

D^{2}\chi_{n}=\lambda^{2}_{n}\chi_{n}\quad,\quad D^{2}\phi_{n}=\lambda^{2}_{n}\phi_{n}\quad,\quad D^{2}\psi_{n}=\lambda^{2}_{n}\psi_{n}\quad,\quad D^{2}\psi^{*}_{n}=\lambda^{2}_{n}\psi^{*}_{n}\ .

(2.56)

The Dirac operator
D
, as well as
i{\nabla\hskip-8.53581pt/}
, are hermitian with respect to the inner product

(\Psi_{1},\Psi_{2})=\int{\rm d}^{2}x\sqrt{g}\,\Psi_{1}^{\dagger}\Psi_{2}\ .

(2.57)

As usual, the eigenfunctions
\psi_{n}
corresponding to different
\lambda_{n}
are orthogonal. Similarly, for the
\psi_{n}^{*}
. Also all
\psi_{n}^{*}
are orthogonal to all
\psi_{k}
(as long as
\lambda_{n}\neq 0
). Equivalently, assuming the eigenfunctions to be also normalised, we have for the real and imaginary parts

(\chi_{n},\chi_{k})=\delta_{nk}\quad,\quad(\phi_{n},\phi_{k})=\delta_{nk}\quad,\quad(\chi_{n},\phi_{k})=0\quad(\lambda_{n}\neq 0)\ .

(2.58)

Note that the eigenfunctions of
D
(the
\psi_{n}
and
\psi_{n}^{*}
) are automatically eigenfunctions of
D^{2}
, but the converse is not necessarily true as is examplified by the
\chi_{n}
and
\phi_{n}
. What is true is that within any eigenspace of
D^{2}
with eigenvalue
\Lambda_{n}=\lambda_{n}^{2}
one can find linear combinations (corresponding precisely to the
\psi_{n}
and
\psi_{n}^{*}
) that are eigenfunctions of
D
with eigenvalues
+\lambda_{n}
and
-\lambda_{n}
.

2.3
The example of the flat torus with arbitrary periods

It will be helpful to be able to check our general statements in the sequel of this paper against at least one example where we explicitly know the eigenvalues and eigenfunctions. The simplest example is the flat torus. Then the metric is just
g_{\mu\nu}=\delta_{\mu\nu}
and
{\nabla\hskip-8.53581pt/}={\partial\hskip-6.25958pt/}
. Of course, being flat, we will not be able to appreciate any effects of curvature.
The relevant Dirac operator then is

D=i{\partial\hskip-6.25958pt/}+m\gamma_{*}=i\sigma_{x}\partial_{1}+i\sigma_{z}\partial_{2}+\sigma_{y}m=i\,\begin{pmatrix}\partial_{2}&\partial_{1}-m\\
\partial_{1}+m&-\partial_{2}\end{pmatrix}\quad,\quad D^{2}=-(\partial_{1}^{2}+\partial_{2}^{2})+m^{2}\ .

(2.59)

There are still different tori corresponding to different periods.
12
12

12

For fermions we could also consider different spin structures, i.e. periodic or anti-periodic boundary conditions around one or the other circle of the torus, leading to four different spin structures. Here we will only investigate the doubly periodic boundary conditions, which are the only ones for which zero-modes are present.

The flat “square” torus :
We will shortly consider the flat torus with arbitrary periods, but we begin by looking
at the “square” torus corresponding to a square of length
2\pi
with opposite sides identified.
The eigenvalue problem for
D^{2}
, i.e.
D^{2}\psi_{n}=\lambda_{n}^{2}\psi_{n}
yields already

\lambda_{n}^{2}\equiv\lambda_{\vec{n}}^{2}=n_{1}^{2}+n_{2}^{2}+m^{2}\quad,\quad\psi_{n}\sim e^{in_{1}x^{1}+in_{2}x^{2}}\ .

(2.60)

We denote by
\lambda_{\vec{n}}
the positive square root of
\lambda_{\vec{n}}^{2}
. Inserting
the ansatz

\psi_{\vec{n}}(x^{1},x^{2})=\begin{pmatrix}a\\
b\end{pmatrix}e^{in_{1}x^{1}+in_{2}x^{2}}

into
D\psi_{\vec{n}}=\lambda_{\vec{n}}\psi_{\vec{n}}
gives the two equivalent equations

-(n_{1}+im)b=(\lambda_{\vec{n}}+n_{2})a
and
-(n_{1}-im)a=(\lambda_{\vec{n}}-n_{2})b
.
This is easily solved, up to a common normalisation, which we choose such that
\int{\rm d}^{2}x\,\psi_{\vec{n}}^{\dagger}\psi_{\vec{n}}=1
:

\psi_{\vec{n}}(\vec{x})=\frac{1}{2\pi\sqrt{2\lambda_{\vec{n}}(\lambda_{\vec{n}}+n_{2})}}\begin{pmatrix}n_{1}+im\\
-\lambda_{\vec{n}}-n_{2}\end{pmatrix}e^{in_{1}x^{1}+in_{2}x^{2}}\ .

(2.61)

Since
\lambda_{\vec{n}}
only depends on
n_{1}^{2}+n_{2}^{2}
, each eigenvalue is (at least) fourfold degenerate.
13
13

13

In the present example where both circles have the same radius, there is a further degeneracy under the exchange of
n_{1}
and
n_{2}
.

Also

\psi_{\vec{n}}^{*}=-\psi_{-\vec{n}}\big|_{\lambda_{\vec{n}}\to-\lambda_{\vec{n}}}\ ,

(2.62)

which shows that
\psi_{\vec{n}}^{*}
is indeed an eigenfunction of
D
with eigenvalue
-\lambda_{\vec{n}}
. Thus the complete set of eigenfunctions is the set of all
\psi_{\vec{n}}
and all
\psi_{\vec{n}}^{*}
.

Flat torus with arbitrary periods :

A general flat two-dimensional torus is obtained as the quotient of
{\bf R}^{2}
by a lattice
\Gamma
where
\Gamma
is generated by two linearly independent vectors
\vec{\omega}_{1}
and
\vec{\omega}_{2}
, i.e. we identify any two points of
{\bf R}^{2}
that differ by
p\vec{\omega}_{1}+q\vec{\omega}_{2}
with integer
p,q
. With respect to an orthonormal basis
\vec{e}_{1},\ \vec{e}_{2}
we can write
\vec{x}=x^{1}\vec{e}_{1}+x^{2}\vec{e}_{2}
,
\ \vec{\omega}_{1}=\omega_{11}\vec{e}_{1}+\omega_{12}\vec{e}_{2}
and
\vec{\omega}_{2}=\omega_{21}\vec{e}_{1}+\omega_{22}\vec{e}_{2}
, so that one identifies
x^{1}\simeq x^{1}+p\omega_{11}+q\omega_{21}
and
x^{2}\simeq x^{2}+p\omega_{12}+q\omega_{22}
. The formula simplify, if one chooses the basis
\vec{e}_{1},\ \vec{e}_{2}
such that
\vec{e}_{1}
is aligned with
\vec{\omega}_{1}
which means
\omega_{12}=0
. Also, by scaling the coordinates appropriately one may assume that
\omega_{11}=2\pi
. With this choice one writes

\vec{\omega}_{1}=2\pi\,\vec{e}_{1}\quad,\quad\vec{\omega}_{2}=2\pi\big(\tau_{1}\vec{e}_{1}+\tau_{2}\vec{e}_{2}\big)\quad,\quad x^{1}\simeq x^{1}+2\pi(p+\tau_{1}q)\quad,\quad x^{2}\simeq x^{2}+2\pi\tau_{2}q\ ,

(2.63)

with integer
p
and
q
.
If we use a complex coordinate
z=x^{1}+ix^{2}
instead, the periodicity is

z\simeq z+2\pi\big(p+\tau q)\quad,\quad\tau=\tau_{1}+i\tau_{2}\quad,\quad\tau_{2}>0\ \ .

(2.64)

The area of the torus now is given by the area of the fundamental cell which is a parallelogram with sides
\vec{\omega}_{1}
and
\vec{\omega}_{2}
. Hence the area is
A=2\pi\times 2\pi\tau_{2}
.
Note that the case
\tau_{1}=0
corresponds to a “rectangular” torus.
The “wave-vectors”
\vec{k}
are such that
\vec{k}\cdot\big(p\vec{\omega}_{1}+q\vec{\omega}_{2}\big)\in 2\pi{\bf Z}
for all integer
p,q
. They form the “dual lattice” and are given by

\vec{k}=n_{1}\vec{e}_{1}+\frac{n_{2}-\tau_{1}n_{1}}{\tau_{2}}\vec{e}_{2}\quad,\quad n_{1},n_{2}\in{\bf Z}\ ,

(2.65)

as one can easily check.
This means that (
x^{1}=\frac{z+\bar{z}}{2},\ x^{2}=\frac{z-\bar{z}}{2i}
)

\exp\big(i\vec{k}_{\vec{n}}\cdot\vec{x}\big)=\exp\big(in_{1}x^{1}+i\frac{n_{2}-\tau_{1}n_{1}}{\tau_{2}}x^{2}\big)=\exp\Big(-\frac{1}{2\tau_{2}}\big((n\bar{\tau}-m)z-(n\tau-m)\bar{z}\big)\Big)\ .

(2.66)

has the correct periodicity properties (
2.63
), resp. (
2.64
).

The Dirac operator
D
is still the same as in (
2.59
) since the present torus is still flat, only the periodic boundary conditions are different. Then also
D^{2}=-(\partial_{1}^{2}+\partial_{2}^{2})+m^{2}
as before and, hence,
D^{2}e^{i\vec{k}_{\vec{n}}\cdot\vec{x}}=\lambda^{2}_{\vec{n}}e^{i\vec{k}_{\vec{n}}\cdot\vec{x}}
with

\lambda^{2}_{\vec{n}}=\vec{k}^{2}_{\vec{n}}+m^{2}=n_{1}^{2}+\big(\frac{n_{2}-\tau_{1}n_{1}}{\tau_{2}}\big)^{2}+m^{2}\ .

(2.67)

If we denote
k_{1}=n_{1}
and
k_{2}=\frac{n_{2}-\tau_{1}n_{1}}{\tau_{2}}
then almost exactly as before
14
14

14

The only difference is the appearance of the additional normalisation factor
\frac{1}{\sqrt{\tau_{2}}}
since now the area of the torus is
2\pi\times 2\pi\tau_{2}
instead of
(2\pi)^{2}
.

we get for the eigenfunction of
D

\psi_{\vec{n}}(\vec{x})=\frac{1}{2\pi\sqrt{2\tau_{2}\lambda_{\vec{n}}(\lambda_{\vec{n}}+k_{2})}}\begin{pmatrix}k_{1}+im\\
-\lambda_{\vec{n}}-k_{2}\end{pmatrix}e^{ik_{1}x^{1}+ik_{2}x^{2}}\ ,

(2.68)

together with the complex conjugate functions which still satisfy

\psi_{\vec{n}}^{*}=-\psi_{-\vec{n}}\big|_{\lambda_{\vec{n}}\to-\lambda_{\vec{n}}}\ .

(2.69)

Zero-modes of
D
:
As discussed in general above, for
m\neq 0
there are no zero-modes of
D
, while for
m=0
the zero-modes correspond to
\vec{k}_{\vec{n}}=0
, i.e.
n_{1}=n_{2}=0
. However, in this case
(
2.61
) and (
2.68
) are indeterminate. We may instead first take to
n_{1}=n_{2}=0
and then consider the limit
m\to 0
which gives the two zero-modes of definite chirality :

\psi_{\vec{0}}=\frac{1}{2\pi\sqrt{2\tau_{2}}}\begin{pmatrix}i\\
-1\end{pmatrix}\quad,\quad\psi_{\vec{0}}^{*}=\frac{1}{2\pi\sqrt{2\tau_{2}}}\begin{pmatrix}-i\\
-1\end{pmatrix}\quad,\quad\gamma_{*}\psi_{\vec{0}}=\psi_{\vec{0}}\quad,\quad\gamma_{*}\psi_{\vec{0}}^{*}=-\psi_{\vec{0}}\ .

(2.70)

Below, we will define the “projector” on the zero-modes in general as
P_{0}(x,y)=\sum_{i}\psi_{0,i}(x)\psi_{0,i}^{\dagger}(y)
. At present, for the general flat torus this simply gives

P_{0}=\psi_{\vec{0}}\,\psi_{\vec{0}}^{\dagger}+\psi_{\vec{0}}^{*}\,{\psi_{\vec{0}}^{*}}^{\dagger}=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{2}}\frac{1}{2\tau_{2}}\begin{pmatrix}1&-i\\
i&1\end{pmatrix}+{\rm c.c}=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{2}\tau_{2}}\ {\bf 1}_{2\times 2}=\frac{1}{A}\ {\bf 1}_{2\times 2}\ ,

(2.71)

where
A=(2\pi)^{2}\tau_{2}
is the area of the torus.

2.4
Conformal transformation of the Dirac operator

As discussed in the introduction, we will consider two metrics
g_{\mu\nu}
and
\widehat{g}_{\mu\nu}
that are conformally equivalent, i.e. that are simply related by a local (
x
-dependent) conformal factor which we write as
e^{2\sigma}\equiv e^{2\sigma(x)}
:

\begin{array}[]{|c|}\hline\cr\\
\displaystyle g_{\mu\nu}=e^{2\sigma}\,\widehat{g}_{\mu\nu}\ .\\
\\
\hline\cr\end{array}

(2.72)

We will consider
\widehat{g}_{\mu\nu}
as a reference metric (also referred to as the background metric) and
g_{\mu\nu}
as a “general” metric obtained by varying the conformal factor
e^{2\sigma(x)}
. This view is particularly useful in two dimensions where, by a change of coordinates, any metric can be brought into the form
e^{2\sigma(x)}\widehat{g}_{\mu\nu}
with a fixed reference metric
\widehat{g}_{\mu\nu}
. In particular, in two dimensions we have the relation (
1.14
) between the curvature scalar
{\cal R}
for the metric
g
and the one
\widehat{\cal R}
for the metric
\widehat{g}
, namely
{\cal R}=e^{-2\sigma}\big(\widehat{\cal R}+2\widehat{\Delta}\,\sigma\big)
, as well as

\sqrt{g}=\sqrt{\widehat{g}}\,e^{2\sigma}\ .

(2.73)

Since
\sqrt{\widehat{g}}\,\widehat{\Delta}_{\rm scalar}=\partial_{\mu}\big(\widehat{g}^{\mu\nu}\sqrt{\widehat{g}}\,\partial_{\nu}\big)
we see that
\sqrt{g}\,{\cal R}=\sqrt{\widehat{g}}\,\widehat{\cal R}+\text{total derivative}
, which is the reason why the integral
\int\sqrt{g}\,{\cal R}
is independent of the conformal deformation and it is a topological invariant : its value only depends on the topology of the two-dimensional manifold
{\cal M}
. Indeed, it equals
4\pi\chi({\cal M})
where
\chi({\cal M})
is the Euler characteristic of
{\cal M}
