. For a manifold without boundaries the latter is given in terms of the genus
{\tt g}
as
\chi({\cal M})=2(1-{\tt g})
, so that
\int_{{\cal M}}\sqrt{g}{\cal R}=8\pi(1-{\tt g})
.
We already noted that the Laplace operators
\Delta
and
\widehat{\Delta}
are simply related by
\Delta=e^{-2\sigma}\widehat{\Delta}
. We now want to establish how the operators
{\nabla\hskip-8.53581pt/}
and
\widehat{\nabla\hskip-8.53581pt/}
are related.

2.4.1
Dirac operator in
n
dimension

It is not difficult to work this out in general dimension
n
. Below, we will then specialise to
n=2
.
Writing the metric in terms of the local frame forms
e^{a}
as in (
2.8
) we see that there is also a corresponding conformal rescaling of the frame forms as
e^{a}=e^{\sigma}\hat{e}^{a}
and correspondingly for the vielbeins
e^{a}_{\mu}
and their inverses
E^{\mu}_{a}
as

e^{a}_{\mu}=e^{\sigma}\hat{e}^{a}_{\mu}\quad,\quad E_{a}^{\mu}=e^{-\sigma}\hat{E}_{a}^{\mu}\ ,

(2.74)

How are the spin-connections
\hat{\omega}^{ab}
and
\omega^{ab}
related ?
Writing out the zero-torsion condition for
e^{a}=(e^{\sigma}\hat{e}^{a})
and
\omega^{ab}
, and using the one for

\hat{e}^{a}
and
\hat{\omega}^{ab}
, gives

0={\rm d}e^{a}+\omega^{ab}e^{b}=e^{\sigma}\Big[{\rm d}\sigma\,\hat{e}^{a}-\hat{\omega}^{ab}\hat{e}^{b}+\omega^{ab}\hat{e}^{b}\Big]\ ,

(2.75)

which is solved as

\omega^{ab}_{\mu}=\hat{\omega}^{ab}_{\mu}+(\hat{e}^{a}_{\mu}\hat{E}^{b\lambda}-\hat{e}^{b}_{\mu}\hat{E}^{a\lambda})\partial_{\lambda}\sigma\ .

(2.76)

It follows that

\nabla_{\mu}^{\rm sp}=\partial_{\mu}+\frac{1}{4}\omega^{cd}_{\mu}\gamma_{cd}=\ \hat{\nabla}_{\mu}^{\rm sp}+\frac{1}{2}\hat{e}^{c}_{\mu}\hat{E}^{d\lambda}\partial_{\lambda}\sigma\,\gamma_{cd}

(2.77)

Using
\gamma^{\mu}=E^{\mu}_{a}\gamma^{a}=e^{-\sigma}\hat{E}^{\mu}_{a}\gamma^{a}=e^{-\sigma}\hat{\gamma}^{\mu}
, we arrive at

{\nabla\hskip-8.53581pt/}=e^{-\sigma}\big(\widehat{\nabla\hskip-8.53581pt/}+\frac{1}{2}\gamma^{c}\hat{E}^{d\lambda}\partial_{\lambda}\sigma\,\gamma_{cd}\big)=e^{-\sigma}\Big(\widehat{\nabla\hskip-8.53581pt/}+\frac{n-1}{2}\,(\hat{\partial\hskip-6.25958pt/}\,\sigma)\Big)\ ,

(2.78)

where we used
\gamma^{c}\gamma_{cd}=(n-1)\gamma_{d}
. This can also be written as

{\nabla\hskip-8.53581pt/}=e^{-\frac{n+1}{2}\sigma}\widehat{\nabla\hskip-8.53581pt/}\ e^{\frac{n-1}{2}\sigma}\ .

(2.79)

For an infinitesimal variation
\delta\sigma
of the conformal factor this yields

\delta{\nabla\hskip-8.53581pt/}=-\delta\sigma{\nabla\hskip-8.53581pt/}+\frac{n-1}{2}\gamma^{\lambda}\partial_{\lambda}\delta\sigma\equiv-\delta\sigma{\nabla\hskip-8.53581pt/}+\frac{n-1}{2}\,\big({\partial\hskip-6.25958pt/}\,\delta\sigma\big)\ .

(2.80)

Consider now the Dirac operator
D=i{\nabla\hskip-8.53581pt/}+m\gamma_{*}
as given in (
2.42
).
Obviously, under a conformal rescaling the term
m\gamma_{*}
does not change, so that

D=i{\nabla\hskip-8.53581pt/}+m\gamma_{*}=e^{-\frac{n+1}{2}\sigma}\,i\widehat{\nabla\hskip-8.53581pt/}\ e^{\frac{n-1}{2}\sigma}+m\gamma_{*}\quad\Rightarrow\quad\delta D=i\delta{\nabla\hskip-8.53581pt/}=-\delta\sigma\,i{\nabla\hskip-8.53581pt/}+\frac{n-1}{2}\,i({\partial\hskip-6.25958pt/}\,\delta\sigma)\ .

(2.81)

One can also work out the variation of
D^{2}
which requires some care, with the result

\delta D^{2}=-2\delta\sigma\,(D^{2}-m^{2})+({\partial\hskip-6.25958pt/}\delta\sigma){\nabla\hskip-8.53581pt/}-(n-1)g^{\mu\nu}(\partial_{\mu}\delta\sigma)\nabla_{\nu}^{\rm sp}-\frac{n-1}{2}(\Delta_{\rm scalar}\delta\sigma)\ .

(2.82)

Actually, we will not need this formula. The relation (
2.79
) and its direct consequences (
2.80
) and (
2.81
) is all we will need.

2.4.2
Dirac operator in two dimensions

Let us now specialise to 2 dimensions i.e.
n=2
in the previous formula. Then

\begin{array}[]{|c|}\hline\cr\\
\displaystyle{\nabla\hskip-8.53581pt/}=e^{-\frac{3}{2}\sigma}\widehat{\nabla\hskip-8.53581pt/}\ e^{\frac{1}{2}\sigma}\ ,\\
\\
\hline\cr\end{array}

(2.83)

as well as

\delta D=-\delta\sigma(D-m\gamma_{*})+\frac{i}{2}\,({\partial\hskip-6.25958pt/}\,\delta\sigma)

(2.84)

\delta D^{2}=-2\delta\sigma\,(D^{2}-m^{2})+\gamma^{\mu\nu}(\partial_{\mu}\delta\sigma)\nabla_{\nu}^{\rm sp}-\frac{1}{2}(\Delta_{\rm scalar}\delta\sigma)\ ,

(2.85)

where
\gamma^{\mu\nu}=\epsilon^{\mu\nu}i\gamma_{*}
.
Let us check that the factors
e^{-\frac{3}{2}\sigma}
and
e^{\frac{1}{2}\sigma}
in (
2.83
) are exactly what is needed for
i{\nabla\hskip-8.53581pt/}
to be hermitian :
Indeed,
\widehat{\nabla\hskip-8.53581pt/}
is hermitian for the metric
\widehat{g}
. Then

\displaystyle(\psi_{1},i{\nabla\hskip-8.53581pt/}\,\psi_{2})

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle\int{\rm d}^{2}z\sqrt{g}\,\psi_{1}^{\dagger}i{\nabla\hskip-8.53581pt/}\,\psi_{2}=\int{\rm d}^{2}z\sqrt{\widehat{g}}\,e^{2\sigma}\,\psi_{1}^{\dagger}\,e^{-\frac{3}{2}\sigma}i\widehat{\nabla\hskip-8.53581pt/}\,\big(e^{\frac{1}{2}\sigma}\psi_{2}\big)

(2.86)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int{\rm d}^{2}z\sqrt{\widehat{g}}\,\,\big(i\widehat{\nabla\hskip-8.53581pt/}\,(e^{\frac{1}{2}\sigma}\psi_{1})\big)^{\dagger}\,\big(e^{\frac{1}{2}\sigma}\psi_{2}\big)=\int{\rm d}^{2}z\sqrt{g}\,\,\big(i{\nabla\hskip-8.53581pt/}\psi_{1}\big)^{\dagger}\,\psi_{2}\equiv(i{\nabla\hskip-8.53581pt/}\,\psi_{1},\psi_{2})\ ,

so that
i{\nabla\hskip-8.53581pt/}
is hermitian for the metric
g
. Actually, the same argument applies in
n
dimensions with (
2.79
) and
\sqrt{g}=e^{n\sigma}\sqrt{\widehat{g}}
.
We see that we coud have derived the formula (
2.79
) and (
2.83
) solely from the requirement that
i{\nabla\hskip-8.53581pt/}\,
is hermitian for the metric
g
if
i\widehat{\nabla\hskip-8.53581pt/}\,
is hermitian for the metric
\widehat{g}
.

2.5
Zero-modes

We will now collect a few results and remarks one can rather easily derive about the zero-modes of the
massless
Dirac operator
i{\nabla\hskip-8.53581pt/}
on a two-dimensional manifold without boundaries.

We will easily re-derive that there are no zero-modes of
i{\nabla\hskip-8.53581pt/}\,
for spherical topology, and we explicitly obtain the zero-modes for toroidal topology (with doubly periodic spin structure). For genus
{\tt g}\geq 2
we do not have explicit formulae for the zero-modes, but we can nevertheless sufficiently characterise their dependences on the conformal factor, as needed later-on. It is actually known
[
18
,
17
]
that for genus
{\tt g}\geq 3
, even the number of zero-modes depends on the conformal class of the metric and no simple formula for their number can exist.

2.5.1
General remarks about zero-modes

First recall that
i{\nabla\hskip-8.53581pt/}\
is purely imaginary so that if
i{\nabla\hskip-8.53581pt/}\,\psi_{0}=0
then also
i{\nabla\hskip-8.53581pt/}\,\psi^{*}_{0}=0
. Hence, if
\psi_{0}
is a complex zero-mode, then
\psi_{0}^{*}
is another zero-mode.

As already noticed,
i{\nabla\hskip-8.53581pt/}
anticommutes with
\gamma_{*}
. Then, for
m\neq 0
,
D=i{\nabla\hskip-8.53581pt/}+m\gamma_{*}
neither commutes nor anti-commutes with
\gamma_{*}
and we cannot have eigenfunctions of
D
of definite chirality (i.e. being also eigenfunctions of
\gamma_{*}
). However, for
m=0
,
D=i{\nabla\hskip-8.53581pt/}\
anti-commutes with
\gamma_{*}
, so that
\gamma_{*}\psi_{n}
is eigenfunction of
D
with eigenvalue
-\lambda_{n}
. Thus for
\lambda_{n}\neq 0
(and
m=0
),
\psi_{n}
and
\gamma_{*}\psi_{n}
necessarily are orthogonal. For
\lambda_{n}=0
(and
m=0
), however, this is not the case. Instead one can always choose a basis of definite chirality eigenfunctions. Indeed, if
\psi_{0}
is a zero-mode of
i{\nabla\hskip-8.53581pt/}
without being an eigenstate of
\gamma_{*}
then
\gamma_{*}\psi_{0}
and
\psi_{0}
are two linearly independent zero-modes and one can form the two linear combinations
\psi_{0,\pm}=\frac{1}{2}(1\pm\gamma_{*})\psi_{0}
. Obviously, the
\psi_{0,\pm}
now are zero-modes of definite chirality. As is well known, the difference of the number of positive and negative chirality zero-modes of
i{\nabla\hskip-8.53581pt/}\,
is called its index. Since
\gamma_{*}=\sigma_{y}=\begin{pmatrix}0&-i\\
i&0\end{pmatrix}
, the positive chirality eigenfunctions must be
\sim\begin{pmatrix}1\\
i\end{pmatrix}
and the negative chirality eigenfunctions must be
\sim\begin{pmatrix}1\\
-i\end{pmatrix}
. We see that they are intrinsically complex, and complex conjugation switches the chirality. Hence there are as many positive chirality as negative chirality zero-modes, i.e. the index of
i{\nabla\hskip-8.53581pt/}
is zero. Indeed, this index is related to the Pontryagin invariant (see e.g.
[
4
]
) which vanishes in
n=4k+2
dimensions, and in particular in 2 dimensions.

On the other hand,
D^{2}
commutes with
\gamma_{*}
, and one can then take all the eigenfunctions (zero and non-zero modes) of
D^{2}
to have definite chirality. From the discussion of the previous paragraph it is then clear that in general these definite chirality eigenfunctions of
D^{2}
are
not
eigenfunctions of
D
.

There are some simple standard arguments about when zero-modes can exist. First of all, assuming the eigenfunctions to be normalised,

\lambda_{n}^{2}-m^{2}=(\psi_{n},(D^{2}-m^{2})\psi_{n})=(\psi_{n},(i{\nabla\hskip-8.53581pt/})^{2}\psi_{n})=(i{\nabla\hskip-8.53581pt/}\psi_{n},i{\nabla\hskip-8.53581pt/}\psi_{n})\geq 0\ .

(2.87)

We see that

\begin{array}[]{|c|}\hline\cr\\
\displaystyle\lambda_{n}^{2}>m^{2}\ ,\quad\text{unless $i{\nabla\hskip-8.53581pt/}\,\psi_{0}=0$ in which case $\lambda_{0}^{2}=m^{2}$}\ .\\
\\
\hline\cr\end{array}

(2.88)

In particular, if
m\neq 0
we always have
\lambda_{n}^{2}\geq m^{2}>0
and there cannot be any zero-modes of the massive Dirac operator.
Furthermore, if
i{\nabla\hskip-8.53581pt/}\,
admits a zero-mode, then the smallest eigenvalue is
\lambda_{0}=m
.

Next, we have

(\psi_{n},(-\Delta_{\rm sp})\psi_{n})=-\int{\rm d}^{2}x\sqrt{g}\,\psi_{n}^{*}\,g^{\mu\nu}\nabla_{\mu}\nabla_{\nu}\psi_{n}=\int{\rm d}^{2}x\sqrt{g}(\nabla_{\mu}^{\rm sp}\psi_{n})^{*}g^{\mu\nu}\nabla_{\nu}^{\rm sp}\psi_{n}\equiv\alpha^{2}\geq 0\ .

(2.89)

Since the metric
g^{\mu\nu}
is positive definite,
\alpha=0
means that
\nabla_{\mu}^{\rm sp}\psi_{n}=0
for both
\mu=1
and
\mu=2
, i.e.
\psi_{n}
must be covariantly constant. However, since
[\nabla_{1},\nabla_{2}]\psi\sim{\cal R}\gamma_{*}\psi
, we see that a covariantly constant spinor can only exist on a manifold with vanishing curvature,
{\cal R}=0
everywhere, i.e. only on the flat torus. For all other two-dimensional manifolds without boundary (and metrics) one has
\alpha^{2}>0
.
Next, it follows from (
2.47
) that

\lambda_{n}^{2}-m^{2}=\alpha^{2}+\frac{1}{4}\int\sqrt{g}\,{\cal R}\,\psi_{n}^{\dagger}\psi_{n}=\alpha^{2}+\frac{1}{4}\langle{\cal R}\rangle_{n}\ ,

(2.90)

where
\langle{\cal R}\rangle_{n}
denotes the “mean curvature in the state
\psi_{n}
”
If we choose a constant curvature metric,
{\cal R}=\frac{8\pi}{A}(1-{\tt g})

where
{\tt g}
is the genus of the Riemann surface, this becomes

\lambda_{n}^{2}-m^{2}=\alpha^{2}+\frac{2\pi}{A}(1-{\tt g})\ .

(2.91)

We will show soon that there is a simple relation between the zero-modes of the metric
\widehat{g}
and the zero-modes of the metric
g=e^{2\sigma}\widehat{g}
. In particular, we may choose
\widehat{g}
to be the constant curvature metric and then use (
2.91
) to argue about the existence of the corresponding zero-modes or not. In particular,
we see that for spherical topology (
{\tt g}=0
) the right-hand side is
\alpha^{2}+\frac{2\pi}{A}>0
so that
\lambda_{n}^{2}>m^{2}
and the Dirac operator cannot have any zero-mode even for
m=0
. For the flat torus we have
\lambda_{n}^{2}=m^{2}+\alpha^{2}
and for
m=0
one can have zero-modes provided
\alpha=0
, meaning that the zero-modes must be covariantly constant spinors that precisely exist (only) on the flat torus as we have just seen. Then there are also corresponding zero-modes on an arbitrary torus.
15
15

15

Since the latter cannot be covarianty constant and hence must have
\alpha^{2}>0
they necessarily have a negative
mean
curvature :
\langle{\cal R}\rangle_{0}=-4\alpha^{2}
.

Finally, for genus two and larger, the right-hand side of (
2.91
) can vanish and there are zero-modes of the massless Dirac operator.

Remarks on comparing eigenvalues for vanishing and non-vanishing mass :

Let us denote by
\pm\lambda_{n}^{(0)}
the positive and negative eigenvalues of
D
for
m=0
, i.e. of
i{\nabla\hskip-8.53581pt/}\
:
i{\nabla\hskip-8.53581pt/}\,\psi^{(0)}_{\pm n,i}=\pm\lambda_{n}^{(0)}\psi^{(0)}_{\pm n,i}
, where the additional index
i
for the corresponding eigenfunctions takes into account that the eigenvalues
\pm\lambda_{n}^{(0)}
can be degenerate. Now, for
\pm\lambda_{n}^{(0)}\neq 0
these eigenfunctions are in general not eigenstates of
\gamma_{*}
and, hence, the
\psi^{(0)}_{\pm n,i}
are
not
eigenfunctions of
D=i{\nabla\hskip-8.53581pt/}+m\gamma_{*}
. Obviously then, there cannot be any linear relation between the
\lambda_{n}
and the
\lambda_{n}^{(0)}
. However, we have
D^{2}\psi^{(0)}_{\pm n,i}=\big((i{\nabla\hskip-8.53581pt/})^{2}+m^{2}\big)\psi^{(0)}_{\pm n,i}=\big((\pm\lambda_{n}^{(0)})^{2}+m^{2}\big)\psi^{(0)}_{\pm n,i}
, so that the
\psi^{(0)}_{\pm n,i}
are eigenfiunctions of
D^{2}
with eigenvalues

\lambda_{n}^{2}=(\lambda_{n}^{(0)})^{2}+m^{2}\ .

(2.92)

This means that the eigenfunctions
\psi_{n}
corresponding to
\lambda_{n}
can be identified with some linear combination of the
\psi^{(0)}_{\pm n,i}
, and similarly for the
\psi_{-n}=\psi_{n}^{*}
corresponding to
-\lambda_{n}
.

2.5.2
Zero-modes on a general torus

Before discussing the case of arbitrary genus, let us first consider the zero-modes on the torus where we can give very explicit results. The general torus is defined by a) the periodicity conditions of the flat torus and b) a metric given by
g_{\mu\nu}=e^{2\sigma(x)}\delta_{\mu\nu}
(i.e.
\widehat{g}_{\mu\nu}=\delta_{\mu\nu}
), where
\sigma(x)\equiv\sigma(x^{1},x^{2})
must also satisfy the appropriate periodicity conditions. Thus from (
2.83
) we get

D=ie^{-3\sigma(x)/2}{\partial\hskip-6.25958pt/}\,e^{\sigma(x)/2}+m\gamma_{*}=e^{-3\sigma(x)/2}\big(i\sigma_{x}\partial_{1}+i\sigma_{z}\partial_{2}\big)e^{\sigma(x)/2}+m\sigma_{y}\ .

(2.93)

The derivative and the mass terms behave differently under the conformal transformations. However, we see that
for zero mass
, the zero-modes
\psi_{0,i}
are simply determined by

D^{m=0}\psi_{0,i}(x)=i{\nabla\hskip-8.53581pt/}\,\psi_{0,i}(x)=0\quad\Rightarrow\quad i{\partial\hskip-6.25958pt/}\big(e^{\sigma(x)/2}\psi_{0,i}(x)\big)=0\ ,

(2.94)

and one identifies
e^{\sigma(x)/2}\psi_{0}(x)
with the zero-modes
\widehat{\psi}_{0,i}
of
i{\partial\hskip-6.25958pt/}
, i.e. the zero-modes of the flat torus as determined above in (
2.70
). Actually, this argument generalises to arbitrary genus as we will discuss shortly. What is special for the torus is that we know explicitly the
\widehat{\psi}_{0,i}
. Hence the two zero-modes of
i{\nabla\hskip-8.53581pt/}\,
are

\psi_{0,1}(x)=c\,e^{-\sigma(x)/2}\,\widehat{\psi}_{0,1}(x)=c\ \frac{e^{-\sigma(x)/2}}{2\pi\sqrt{2\tau_{2}}}\begin{pmatrix}i\\
-1\end{pmatrix}\quad,\quad\psi_{0,2}(x)=c\,e^{-\sigma(x)/2}\,\widehat{\psi}_{0,2}(x)=c\ \frac{e^{-\sigma(x)/2}}{2\pi\sqrt{2\tau_{2}}}\begin{pmatrix}-i\\
-1\end{pmatrix}\ ,

(2.95)

where
c
is a normalisation constant to be determined.
Indeed, the normalisation now is defined with respect to the metric
g=e^{2\sigma}\widehat{g}
as (no sum over
i
)

1=\int{\rm d}^{2}x\sqrt{g(x)}\,\psi_{0,i}^{\dagger}(x)\psi_{0,i}(x)=\int{\rm d}^{2}x\sqrt{\widehat{g}}\,e^{2\sigma(x)}\frac{|c|^{2}}{(2\pi)^{2}\tau_{2}}e^{-\sigma(x)}=\frac{|c|^{2}}{(2\pi)^{2}\tau_{2}}\int{\rm d}^{2}x\sqrt{\widehat{g}}\,e^{\sigma(x)}\ .

(2.96)

Of course, the integral in the last expression is
not
the area which would be
A=\int{\rm d}^{2}x\sqrt{\widehat{g}}\,e^{2\sigma(x)}
. Also note that
(2\pi)^{2}\tau_{2}
is the area of the torus with metric
\widehat{g}
, i.e. for
\sigma=0
. We now define the “area-like” parameters

A_{(n)}=\int{\rm d}^{2}x\sqrt{\widehat{g}}\,e^{n\sigma(x)}\quad\Rightarrow\quad A_{(2)}=A\ ,\quad A_{(0)}=\widehat{A}=(2\pi)^{2}\tau_{2}\ .

(2.97)

Hence, up to a choice of phase,
c=\sqrt{\frac{\widehat{A}}{A_{(1)}}}=2\pi\sqrt{\frac{\tau_{2}}{A_{(1)}}}
, and

\psi_{0,1}(x)=\frac{e^{-\sigma(x)/2}}{\sqrt{2A_{(1)}}}\begin{pmatrix}i\\
-1\end{pmatrix}\quad,\quad\psi_{0,2}(x)=\frac{e^{-\sigma(x)/2}}{\sqrt{2A_{(1)}}}\begin{pmatrix}-i\\
-1\end{pmatrix}\ .

(2.98)

It follows that the projector on the zero-modes is

P_{0}(x,y)=\sum_{i=1}^{2}\psi_{0,i}(x)\,\psi_{0,i}^{\dagger}(y)=\frac{e^{-(\sigma(x)+\sigma(y))/2}}{A_{(1)}}\ {\bf 1}_{2\times 2}=\frac{e^{-(\sigma(x)+\sigma(y))/2}}{\int{\rm d}^{2}z\sqrt{\widehat{g}}\,e^{\sigma}}\ {\bf 1}_{2\times 2}\ ,

(2.99)

which now depends on
x
and
y
, but is still proportional to the unit matrix (as for the flat torus).

The zero-modes
\psi_{0,1}
and
\psi_{0,2}
as defined in (
2.98
) have definite chirality and then also are eigenfunctions of
D
with non-zero mass. Indeed, we have
\gamma_{*}\psi_{0,1}=\psi_{0,1}
and
\gamma_{*}\psi_{0,2}=-\psi_{0,2}
and then

D\psi_{0,1}=m\psi_{0,1}\quad,\quad D\psi_{0,2}=-m\psi_{0,2}\ .

(2.100)

Thus, the above projector
P_{0}
also is the projector on the eigen-modes of
D
, that become zero-modes when the mass is taken to zero.

2.5.3
Zero-modes of
i{\nabla\hskip-8.53581pt/}\,
for arbitrary genus
{\tt g}\geq 1

We have seen before that for genus zero (spherical topology) there are no zero-modes of
i{\nabla\hskip-8.53581pt/}\,
, and we have just discussed the two zero-modes for a general genus-one manifold. Now we want to establish some further general results valid for arbitrary genus. For higher genus surfaces there are more zero-modes, but in any case there are only finitely many of them. As already mentioned, their number depends
[
18
,
17
]
on the spin structure and, for genus
{\tt g}\geq 3
also on the conformal class of the metric, i.e. on the
\widehat{g}(\tau_{i})
. Of course, within any conformal class this number is fixed, and we have explicitly related the
\psi_{0,i}
to the
\widehat{\psi}_{0,i}
.
As usual, we suppose that the zero-modes are all ortho-normalised :

i{\nabla\hskip-8.53581pt/}\,\psi_{0,i}=0\quad,\quad\int{\rm d}^{2}x\sqrt{g(x)}\,\psi^{\dagger}_{0,i}(x)\psi_{0,j}(x)=\delta_{ij}\ .

(2.101)

As already discussed, these zero-modes can be chosen to have definite chirality, so that even for non-vanishing mass they continue to be eigenstates of the Dirac operator :

D\psi_{0,i}=m\gamma_{*}\psi_{0.i}=\pm m\,\psi_{0,i}
.
Recall that the zero-modes of definite chirality necessarily are complex and
\psi_{0,i}
and
\psi^{*}_{0,i}
have opposite chirality and, hence, that there must be an even number
2n_{0}
of zero-modes,
n_{0}
with positive chirality and
n_{0}
with negative chirality, resulting in a vanishing index of
i{\nabla\hskip-8.53581pt/}
. Let us then choose the labelling of the zero-modes such that
\psi_{0,i},\ i=1,\ldots n_{0}
have positive chirality and
\psi_{0,i},\ i=n_{0}+1,\ldots 2n_{0}
have negative chirality, and moreover such that
\psi_{0,i}^{*}=\psi_{0,i+n_{0}},\ i=1,\ldots n_{0}
.

The projector on these zero-modes then is

P_{0}(x,y)=\sum_{i=1}^{2n_{0}}\psi_{0,i}(x)\,\psi_{0,i}^{\dagger}(y)=\sum_{i=1}^{n_{0}}\big(\psi_{0,i}(x)\,\psi_{0,i}^{\dagger}(y)+\psi^{*}_{0,i}(x)\,{\psi^{*}_{0,i}}^{\dagger}(y)\big)\ .

(2.102)

This is indeed a projector in the sense that we have, by (
2.101
), the relation

\int{\rm d}^{2}z\sqrt{g(z)}P_{0}(x,z)P_{0}(z,y)=P_{0}(x,y)\ .

(2.103)

Since the zero-modes were chosen to have definite chirality
\pm 1
it follows that

\gamma_{*}P_{0}(x,y)\gamma_{*}=P_{0}(x,y)\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad\big[\gamma_{*},P_{0}(x,y)\big]=0\ .

(2.104)

Now, the only matrices that commute with
\gamma_{*}
are
{\bf 1}_{2\times 2}
and
\gamma_{*}
itself.
It follows that
P_{0}
has the following matrix structure

P_{0}(x,y)=p_{0,0}(x,y)\,{\bf 1}_{2\times 2}+p_{0,*}(x,y)\,\gamma_{*}\ .

(2.105)

One can also argue, by choosing a basis of real zero-modes instead, that
P_{0}(x,x)
is real and hermitian, excluding the
\gamma_{*}
-piece for coinciding points. However, this argument does not exclude a
\gamma_{*}
-piece for
x\neq y
.
For an arbitrary torus (with modular parameter
\tau
and conformal factor
\sigma
), we could explicitly show that the matrix-structure is indeed
P_{0}(x,y)\sim{\bf 1}_{2\times 2}
, and it would be nice to show this for arbitrary genus.

It will also be useful to define similarly

Q_{0}(x,y)=\sum_{i=1}^{n_{0}}\big(\psi_{0,i}(x)\,\psi_{0,i}^{\dagger}(y)-\psi^{*}_{0,i}(x)\,{\psi^{*}_{0,i}}^{\dagger}(y)\big)\ .

(2.106)

Obviously

Q_{0}(x,y)=\gamma_{*}P_{0}(x,y)=P_{0}(x,y)\gamma_{*}=p_{0,0}(x,y)\,\gamma_{*}+p_{0,*}(x,y)\,{\bf 1}_{2\times 2}\ ,

(2.107)

so that

\int{\rm d}^{2}z\sqrt{g(z)}Q_{0}(x,z)Q_{0}(z,y)=\int{\rm d}^{2}z\sqrt{g(z)}P_{0}(x,z)P_{0}(z,y)=P_{0}(x,y)\ .

(2.108)

2.5.4
Conformal variation of the zero-modes and of the zero-mode projector

We will need the variation of the zero-modes under conformal transformations. Fortunately this can be easily obtained exactly, generalising the above discussion for the torus. Recall that under
g=e^{2\sigma}\widehat{g}
we have
{{\nabla\hskip-8.53581pt/}}=e^{-3\sigma/2}\widehat{\nabla\hskip-8.53581pt/}\,e^{\sigma/2}
.
Then
{\nabla\hskip-8.53581pt/}\,\psi_{0,i}=0
is equivalent to
\widehat{\nabla\hskip-8.53581pt/}\,(e^{\sigma/2}\psi_{0,i})=0
so that
e^{\sigma/2}\psi_{0,i}
is a zero-mode of
\widehat{\nabla\hskip-8.53581pt/}\,
and we can identify
e^{\sigma(x)/2}\psi_{0,i}(x)\sim\widehat{\psi}_{0,i}(x)
,
i.e. the zero-modes of
{\nabla\hskip-8.53581pt/}
are
\psi_{0,i}(x)\sim e^{-\sigma(x)/2}\widehat{\psi}_{0,i}(x)
. However, a priori, they are not ortho-normalised. Instead, we can consider the linear combinations of the
n_{0}
positive chirality zero-modes

\psi_{0,k}(x)=\sum_{j=1}^{n_{0}}\,e^{-\sigma(x)/2}\,\widehat{\psi}_{0,i}(x)\,c_{ik}\ ,

(2.109)

and the corresponding complex conjugate relation for the negative chirality zero-modes. Imposing the ortho-normality of the
\psi_{0,k}
leads to

\delta_{kl}=\sum_{i,j}c_{ik}^{*}{\cal P}_{0,ij}c_{jl}\ ,

(2.110)

where we have defined the (constant) matrix
{\cal P}_{0}
as

{\cal P}_{0,ij}=\int{\rm d}^{2}x\sqrt{\widehat{g}(x)}\,e^{\sigma(x)}\ \widehat{\psi}_{0,i}^{\,{\dagger}}(x)\widehat{\psi}_{0,j}(x)\ .

(2.111)

Although the

{\cal P}_{0,ij}
are constants, they are actually functionals of the conformal factor
\sigma
.
In matrix notation (
2.110
) reads
{\bf 1}=c^{\dagger}{\cal P}_{0}\,c
so that
{\cal P}_{0}=(c^{-1})^{\dagger}c^{-1}
and
{\cal P}_{0}^{-1}=c\,c^{\dagger}
which shows that
{\cal P}_{0}
is hermitian.
16
16

16

Of course these relations only determine the matrix
c
up to right multiplication with any unitary matrix, consistent with the fact that this just leads to another orthonormal basis of the positive chirality zero-modes
\psi_{0,k}
.

In components this reads
c_{ki}c_{li}^{*}=({\cal P}_{0})^{-1}_{kl}
, and
inserting (
2.109
) into the above definition of the zero-mode projector
P_{0}
we find

P_{0}(x,y)=\sum_{i=1}^{n_{0}}\psi_{0,i}(x)\psi_{0,i}^{\dagger}(y)+{\rm c.c.}=e^{-(\sigma(x)+\sigma(y))/2}\,\sum_{i,j=1}^{n_{0}}\big(({\cal P}_{0}^{-1})_{ij}\widehat{\psi}_{0,i}(x)\widehat{\psi}_{0,j}^{\,{\dagger}}(y)\,+\,{\rm c.c.}\big)\ .

(2.112)

Since
Q_{0}=\gamma_{*}P_{0}=P_{0}\gamma_{*}
,
\ \ Q_{0}
obeys a completely analogous relation except that the negative chirality modes contribute with a minus sign to the sum. Hence, it will be enough if we further discuss
P_{0}
only.
For our later computations it will also be useful to introduce the following notations

\displaystyle P_{0}(x,y)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle e^{-\sigma(x)/2}{\overline{P}}_{0}(x,y)e^{-\sigma(y)/2}\quad,\quad{\overline{P}}_{0}(x,y)=\sum_{i,j=1}^{n_{0}}\big(({\cal P}_{0}^{-1})_{ij}\widehat{\psi}_{0,i}(x)\widehat{\psi}_{0,j}^{\,{\dagger}}(y)\,+\,{\rm c.c.}\big)\ ,

(2.113)

\displaystyle\widehat{P}_{0}(x,y)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i}\big(\widehat{\psi}_{0,i}(x)\widehat{\psi}^{\dagger}_{0,i}(y)+{\rm c.c.}\big)\ .

(2.114)

One must keep in mind that the quantity
{\overline{P}}_{0}(x,y)
still depends on
\sigma

due to the appearance of
e^{\sigma}
in the ortho-normalisation matrix
{\cal P}_{0}
of the zero-modes. Hence, the overall
\sigma
dependences of the projectors are rather non-trivial, but perfectly explicit.
Under an infinitesimal conformal variation with
\delta\sigma
we have

\delta P_{0}(x,y)=-\frac{1}{2}\big(\delta\sigma(x)+\delta\sigma(y)\big)P_{0}(x,y)-\Big[\sum_{i=1}^{n_{0}}(c^{\dagger}\delta{\cal P}_{0}c)_{ij}\ \psi_{0,i}(x)\psi_{0,j}^{\,{\dagger}}(y)\,+\,{\rm c.c.}\Big]\ .

(2.115)

A somewhat simpler quantity is

\int{\rm d}^{2}y\sqrt{g}\,\delta\sigma(y)\,{\rm tr}\,P_{0}(y,y)=\sum_{i,j=1}^{n_{0}}({\cal P}_{0}^{-1})_{ij}\delta{\cal P}_{0,ji}\,+\,{\rm c.c.}=2\,\delta\,\,{\rm Tr}\,\log{\cal P}_{0}=2\,\delta\,\log\det{\cal P}_{0}\ ,

(2.116)

where here
\,{\rm Tr}\,
and
\det
denote the trace and determinant of the
n_{0}\times n_{0}
matrices and the factor 2 occurs since
{\cal P}_{0}
is hermitian and hence the trace is real.

How shall we interpret the quantities
{\cal P}_{0,ij}
? We have seen that for
the example of a torus,
n_{0}=1
and
\widehat{\psi}_{0,i}^{\,{\dagger}}(z)\widehat{\psi}_{0,i}(z)=\frac{1}{\widehat{A}}
, where
\widehat{A}=\int{\rm d}^{2}z\sqrt{\widehat{g}}
. Hence there is a single
{\cal P}_{0}
which equals
{\cal P}_{0}=\frac{A_{(1)}}{\widehat{A}}
with
A_{(1)}=\int{\rm d}^{2}z\sqrt{\widehat{g}}\,e^{\sigma}
an area-like constant. Similarly, on a general genus Riemann surface we will interpret the
{\cal P}_{0,ij}
as a finite number of area-like parameters. Of course, the appearance of a term
({\cal P}^{-1}_{0})_{ij}
is non-local in
\sigma
but it is no more non-local than a factor
\frac{1}{A}
.

3
Fermionic matter partition function and gravitational action

3.1
Fermionic functional integral for Majorana spinors

We define the matter partition function for fermionic matter with action
S=\int{\rm d}^{2}x\sqrt{g}\,\psi^{\dagger}D\psi
, where
D=i{\nabla\hskip-8.53581pt/}+m\gamma_{*}
, on a two-dimensional manifold with metric
g
as the functional integral

Z_{\rm mat}[g]=\int{\cal D}\Psi\exp\left(-S[g,\Psi]\right)\quad,\quad S[g,\Psi]=\int{\rm d}^{2}x\sqrt{g}\,\Psi^{\dagger}D_{g}\Psi\ ,

(3.1)

where we wrote
D_{g}
to insist that this is the Dirac operator
D
for the metric
g
(and corresponding vielbein
e
and spin connection
\omega
). We want to consider here only
Majorana spinors

\Psi
, which means that
\Psi(x)
is an arbitrary anti-commuting
real
spinor and
\Psi^{\dagger}\equiv\Psi^{\rm T}
. The Dirac operator is purely imaginary. Nevertheless, our action is real, as it should. Indeed, recall that for anti-commuting objects
a
and
b
, complex conjugation is defined as hermitian conjugation and reverses the order, so that
(ab)^{*}=b^{*}a^{*}
. Now
(D_{g}\Psi)^{*}=-D_{g}\Psi
. Then

(S[g,\Psi])^{*}=\int\big(\Psi^{\rm T}D_{g}\Psi\big)^{*}=\int((D_{g}\Psi)^{*})^{\rm T}\Psi=-\int(D_{g}\Psi)^{\rm T}\Psi=+\int\Psi^{\rm T}D_{g}\Psi=S[g,\Psi]
, where we used the anti-commutativity in the last step.

Before going on, let us show that if we considered instead a Dirac spinor, i.e. an anti-commuting
complex
spinor
\Psi_{\rm Dirac}=\Psi+i\widetilde{\Psi}
, where
\Psi
and
\widetilde{\Psi}
are two anti-commuting
real
(Majorana) spinors, the action for the Dirac spinor (with the same Dirac operator
D
) would simply be the sum of the actions for the real and imaginary parts,
\Psi
and
\widetilde{\Psi}
, separately without any cross-term, namely

S[g,\Psi_{\rm Dirac}]=S[g,\Psi]+S[g,\widetilde{\Psi}]\ .

(3.2)

Indeed,

S[g,\Psi_{\rm Dirac}]=\int\Psi_{\rm Dirac}^{\dagger}D\Psi_{\rm Dirac}=\int(\Psi+i\widetilde{\Psi})^{\dagger}D(\Psi+i\widetilde{\Psi})=S[g,\Psi]+S[g,\widetilde{\Psi}]-i\int\big(\widetilde{\Psi}^{\dagger}D\Psi-\Psi^{\dagger}D\widetilde{\Psi}\big)\ ,

(3.3)

and, using the hermiticity of
D
and the fact that
\Psi
and
\widetilde{\Psi}
are real and anti-commuting and that
D
is purely imaginary, one easily shows that
\int\big(\widetilde{\Psi}^{\dagger}D\Psi-\Psi^{\dagger}D\widetilde{\Psi}\big)=0
.
It then follows rather straightforwardly that one has for the corresponding partition functions

Z^{\rm Dirac}_{\rm mat}[g]=\int{\cal D}\Psi_{\rm Dirac}\exp\left(-S[g,\Psi_{\rm Dirac}]\right)=\int{\cal D}\Psi{\cal D}\widetilde{\Psi}\exp\left(-S[g,\Psi]-S[g,\widetilde{\Psi}]\right)=\Big(Z_{\rm mat}[g]\Big)^{2}\ .

(3.4)

Thus the matter partition function for a Dirac spinor is simply the square of the matter partition function for a Majorana spinor, and we may thus consider the latter as the more “fundamental” object to study. Hence, for the remainder of this paper we will consider Majorana spinors only.

We can then expand the anti-commuting real (Majorana) spinor
\Psi
on a complete set of real eigenfunctions of
D_{g}
:

\Psi(x)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\mu}}\Big(\sum_{n}(b_{n}\chi_{n}(x)+c_{n}\phi_{n}(x))+\sum_{i}d_{i}\psi_{0,i}(x)\Big)\ .

(3.5)

Here and in the following it is understood that the
\phi_{n}
and
\chi_{n}
are the real eigenfunctions of
D_{g}^{2}
, satisfying
D_{g}\phi_{n}=-i\lambda_{n}\chi_{n}
and
D_{g}\chi_{n}=i\lambda_{n}\phi_{n}
(cf (
2.55
)) for strictly positive eigenvalues
\lambda_{n}>0
. In particular, they are orthonormalised according to
(
2.58
).
The orthonormalized zero-modes, if present, are denoted
\psi_{0,i}
.
Recall that zero-modes are present only for
m=0
and genus
{\tt g}\geq 1
and then there are only finitely many of them. Of course, they are also orthogonal to the
\phi_{n}
and
\chi_{n}
.

The eigenfunctions
\chi_{n},\ \phi_{n}
and
\psi_{0,i}
are real,
commuting
functions, providing an orthonormalized basis. Since
\psi
is anticommuting, the expansion coefficients
b_{n},c_{n},d_{i}
must be real and
anti
-commuting. Then

\Psi^{\dagger}(x)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\mu}}\Big(\sum_{n}(b_{n}\chi^{\dagger}_{n}(x)+c_{n}\phi^{\dagger}_{n}(x))+\sum_{i}d_{i}\psi^{\dagger}_{0,i}(x)\Big)\ ,

(3.6)

were actually
\chi^{\dagger}_{n}=\chi^{\rm T}_{n}
,
\phi^{\dagger}_{n}=\phi^{\rm T}_{n}
,
\psi_{0,i}^{\dagger}=\psi_{0,i}^{\rm T}
.
We have also introduced an arbitrary mass scale
\mu
so that these coefficients
b_{n},c_{n},d_{i}
are dimensionless. Indeed, from the normalisation condition of the eigenmodes one sees that the
\chi_{n}
,
\phi_{n}
and
\psi_{0,i}
have engineering dimension one, i.e.
\chi_{n}\sim\phi_{n}\sim\psi_{0,i}\sim\mu
, and
since
\Psi
must have dimension
\frac{1}{2}
so that the action

\int{\rm d}^{2}x\sqrt{g}\,\Psi^{\dagger}D_{g}\Psi
is dimensionless, we see from (
3.5
) that
b_{n},\ c_{n},\ d_{i}
are indeed dimensionless thanks to this explicit factor
\frac{1}{\sqrt{\mu}}
.

Inserting the expansions (
3.5
) and (
3.6
) into the action
S
as given in (
3.1
) we get, thanks to the orthonomality of the eigenfunctions and the anticommutativity of the expansion coefficients
b_{n}
and
c_{n}

S[g,\Psi]=\int{\rm d}^{2}x\sqrt{g}\,\Psi^{\dagger}D_{g}\Psi=2i\sum_{n}\frac{\lambda_{n}}{\mu}c_{n}b_{n}\ ,

(3.7)

where of course only the non-zero modes contribute. Note that, despite the explicit appearance of the factor
i
, this expression is real as we have shown before. Indeed,
(ic_{n}b_{n})^{*}=-ib_{n}c_{n}=+ic_{n}b_{n}
.

The functional integral measure
{\cal D}\Psi
is defined in terms of grassmann integrals over these coefficients
b_{n}
and
c_{n}
as
{\cal D}\Psi=\int\prod_{l}{\rm d}b_{l}{\rm d}c_{l}
, but not including the
d_{i}
. For grassmannian variables the integrals are non-vanishing only if each
b_{n}
and
c_{n}
appears exactly once in the integrand. Expanding the exponential then gives

Z_{\rm mat}[g]=\int\prod_{l}{\rm d}b_{l}\,{\rm d}c_{l}\exp\left(-2i\sum_{n}\frac{\lambda_{n}}{\mu}c_{n}b_{n}\right)={\cal N}\prod_{n}\frac{\lambda_{n}}{\mu}\ ,

(3.8)

where
{\cal N}
is some overall (infinite) “normalisation” constant that will drop out in the end.
The product is only over all strictly positive eigenvalues
\lambda_{n}
. In principle one could have also done the integral over the zero-mode coefficients
d_{i}
but then one needs to insert an extra factor
\prod_{i}d_{i}
inside the functional integral to get a non-vanishing result. This result would have been the same as in (
3.8
), where we excluded the zero-mode integration from the beginning. Since there is a certain arbitrariness in dealing with the zero-modes, if present, one can also define
Z_{\rm mat}
with some zero-mode related factor included (like e.g.
\sqrt{A}
in the case of a scalar matter field, see
[
10
]
). We will come back to this point later.

The gravitational action was defined in (
1.1
) as

Z_{\rm mat}[g]=e^{-S_{\rm grav}[g,\widehat{g}]}Z_{\rm mat}[\widehat{g}]
, or equivalently
S_{\rm grav}[g,\widehat{g}]=-\log\frac{Z_{\rm mat}[g]}{Z_{\rm mat}[\widehat{g}]}\ ,

so that

S_{\rm grav}[g,\widehat{g}]=-\log\prod_{n}\frac{\lambda_{n}[g]}{\mu}+\log\prod_{n}\frac{\lambda_{n}[\widehat{g}]}{\mu}\ .

(3.9)

Since the product over
n
only involves the non-zero eigenvalues this is sometimes denoted as
\prod_{n}^{\prime}
, but in our convention
n
runs only over the non-zero eigenvalues anyway, so the prime is not necessary.
We may also rewrite this in terms of the determinant of
D^{2}
, which has
eigenvalues
\lambda_{n}^{2}
for the
\chi_{n}
and
\lambda_{n}^{2}
for the
\phi_{n}
, and possibly
0
for the
\psi_{0,i}
. The determinant of
D^{2}
computed on the space of non-zero-modes is conventionally denoted by
\,{\rm Det}\,^{\prime}
so that here we are interested in
\,{\rm Det}\,^{\prime}D^{2}
:

\,{\rm Det}\,^{\prime}D^{2}=\big(\prod_{n}\lambda_{n}^{2}\big)^{2}\ ,

(3.10)

and

\begin{array}[]{|c|}\hline\cr\\
\displaystyle S_{\rm grav}[g,\widehat{g}]=-\frac{1}{4}\log\,{\rm Det}\,^{\prime}D^{2}[g]+\frac{1}{4}\log\,{\rm Det}\,^{\prime}D^{2}[\widehat{g}]\ .\\
\\
\hline\cr\end{array}

(3.11)

3.2
Zeta function regularization

All these determinants and products of eigenvalues are, of course, ill-defined since the infinite products are divergent, and have to be regularized. We will use the standard tool of regularization via the corresponding zeta-functions
[
20
]
.

3.2.1
Zeta-functions of positive operators

The zeta-function of an operator
{\cal O}
with eigenvalues
\Lambda_{n}>0
is defined as

\zeta_{\cal O}(s)=\sum_{n}\Lambda_{n}^{-s}\ .

(3.12)

Proceeding formally, i.e. disregarding whether or not the series converges, the determinant of
{\cal O}
is related to the derivative of this zeta function at
s=0
as follows

\zeta^{\prime}_{\cal O}(s)\equiv\frac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}s}\zeta_{\cal O}(s)=-\sum_{n}(\log\Lambda_{n})\Lambda_{n}^{-s}\quad\Rightarrow\quad\zeta^{\prime}_{\cal O}(0)=-\sum_{n}(\log\Lambda_{n})=-\log\,{\rm Det}\,{\cal O}\ .

(3.13)

For the operator
D^{2}
we are interested in, the corresponding zeta-function is

\zeta_{D^{2}}(s)=2\sum_{n}(\lambda_{n}^{2})^{-s}\ ,

(3.14)

since each eigenvalue
\lambda_{n}^{2}>0
occurs twice, once with eigenfunction
\chi_{n}
and once with
\phi_{n}
. Again, also, the sum obviously does not include any zero eigenvalue in case there are zero-modes.

The zeta-function regularization is a standard technique to regulate and possibly assign finite values to otherwise diverging sums. The zeta-functions of operators are generalisations of Riemann’s zeta function
\zeta_{\rm R}
, defined as

\zeta_{\rm R}(s)=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}n^{-s}\ .

(3.15)

3.2.2
A short discussion of Riemann’s zeta function

It will be useful to briefly discuss some of the well-known properties of Riemann’s zeta-function, in view of the generalisation of these properties to the zeta-functions of operators. Riemann’s zeta function as defined by the sum (
3.15
) should be
considered as a function of a complex argument
s\in{\bf C}
.
Then the sum is absolutely convergent for
\Re s>1
(
\Re s
designates the real part of
s
). Indeed, for complex
s
write
s=s_{1}+is_{2}
so that
n^{-s}=n^{-s_{1}}n^{-is_{2}}=n^{-s_{1}}e^{-is_{2}\log n}
and
|n^{-s}|=n^{-s_{1}}
, which shows that the sum indeed converges absolutely for
s_{1}>1
, i.e.
\Re s>1
.
This then defines an analytic function
\zeta_{\rm R}(s)
for all
\Re s>1
. This function can be analytically continued to
\Re s\leq 1
.
The function that results from analytically continuing
\zeta_{\rm R}
will still be still called
\zeta_{\rm R}
. Riemann has shown that this analytic continuation defines a meromorphic function on the whole complex plane with a single simple pole at
s=1
with unit residue. This means that
\zeta_{\rm R}(s)
is an analytic function on all of
{\bf C}\backslash\{1\}
and that
\lim_{s\to 1}(s-1)\zeta_{\rm R}(s)=1
. In particular,
\zeta_{\rm R}(0)=-\frac{1}{2}
is finite.

An important ingredient in this analytic continuation is Euler’s Gamma function, defined as

\Gamma(s)=\int_{0}^{\infty}{\rm d}t\,t^{s-1}e^{-t}\quad,\quad\Re s>0\ .

(3.16)

Integrating by parts, it is trivial to show that, for
\Re s>0
,
\Gamma(s)
satisfies the functional relation

\Gamma(s+1)=s\Gamma(s)
.
This relation can then be used to define
\Gamma(s)
for
\Re s\leq 0
, except at the isolated points
s=0,-1,-2,\ldots
. Indeed, the functional relation shows that
\Gamma(s)
has simple poles at all negative integers and 0, and is otherwise analytic.
Similarly, for
\Re s>1
, the sum (
3.15
) admits a simple integral representation

\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}n^{-s}=\frac{1}{\Gamma(s)}\int_{0}^{\infty}{\rm d}t\,t^{s-1}\frac{1}{e^{t}-1}\quad,\quad\Re s>1\ ,

(3.17)

as one sees by expanding
(e^{t}-1)^{-1}=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}e^{-nt}
,
and interchanging the sum and the integral, which is justified when both sums converge absolutely, i.e. for
\Re s>1
.
For later reference let us denote

K_{\rm R}(t)=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}e^{-tn}=\frac{1}{e^{t}-1}\ ,

(3.18)

and refer to it as the “Riemann heat kernel”. Then the previous equation can be summarised as

\zeta_{\rm R}(s)=\frac{1}{\Gamma(s)}\int_{0}^{\infty}{\rm d}t\,t^{s-1}K_{\rm R}(t)\ .

(3.19)

We will heavily use a generalisation of this relation later-on.

The basic functional relation used to perform the analytic continuation of
\zeta_{\rm R}
is the identity proven by Riemann: the function
\xi(s)=\pi^{-s/2}\Gamma(s/2)\zeta_{\rm R}(s)
satisfies
\xi(s)=\xi(1-s)
. The proof of this identity uses various standard techniques including Poisson resummation and Mellin transforms of theta functions (similar to the integral representation (
3.17
)). We will not go into details here. Instead, we will give a heuristic argument why the integral representation (
3.17
) or (
3.19
) of
\zeta_{\rm R}
can be analytically continued to yield a meromorphic function on the whole complex plane with a single simple pole at
s=1
with residue 1.
Again, this argument will be generalised below to study the zeta-function associated with our operator
D^{2}
.

First, observe that any singularity of the integral
\int_{0}^{\infty}{\rm d}t\,t^{s-1}\frac{1}{e^{t}-1}
can only come from the region
t\to 0
where the integrand behaves as
t^{s-2}
, while for
t\to\infty
the exponential decrease of the integrand ensures convergence. So let us isolate this small-
t
region by choosing some small (but finite) positive real
\epsilon>0
and split the integral (
3.17
) into 2 parts:

\frac{1}{\Gamma(s)}\Big(\int_{0}^{\epsilon}{\rm d}t\,t^{s-1}\frac{1}{e^{t}-1}+\int_{\epsilon}^{\infty}{\rm d}t\,t^{s-1}\frac{1}{e^{t}-1}\Big)\ .

(3.20)

In the first integral we can replace
\frac{1}{e^{t}-1}=\frac{1}{t+t^{2}/2+\ldots}
by
\frac{1}{t}
up to terms that contribute at most an order
\epsilon
correction. Then the first integral is elementary and yields

\int_{0}^{\epsilon}{\rm d}t\,t^{s-2}=\frac{t^{s-1}}{s-1}\Big|^{\epsilon}_{0}=\frac{\epsilon^{s-1}-0}{s-1}\quad\text{since}\ \Re s>1\ .

(3.21)

This term exhibits the pole at
s=1
. Indeed, letting
s\to 1
(for fixed
\epsilon
) one has
\epsilon^{s-1}=e^{(s-1)\log\epsilon}=1+(s-1)\log\epsilon+\ldots
and we get for the first integral
\frac{1}{s-1}+\log\epsilon+{\cal O}(s-1)
. On the other hand, the second integral in (
3.20
) is regular for all
s\in{\bf C}
and defines some analytic function for all
s\in{\bf C}
.
We see that this allows to define
\zeta_{\rm R}
as a meromorphic function on
{\bf C}
with a single simple pole at
s=1
with residue 1. One might still worry about the order
\epsilon
-corrections to the first integral as they seem to lead to
\int_{0}^{\epsilon}{\rm d}t\,t^{s-1}=\frac{t^{s}}{s}\Big|^{\epsilon}_{0}=\frac{\epsilon^{s}-0}{s}
, i.e. to a pole at
s=0
. However, this pole is cancelled by the zero of
\frac{1}{\Gamma(s)}
at
s=0
. The same cancellation occurs for all would-be poles at all negative integers. Hence there is only a single pole at
s=1
.

3.2.3
Expressing the gravitational action in terms of
\zeta_{D^{2}}

Let us now come back to the zeta-function (
3.14
) of our operator
D^{2}
.
In the next section, we will discuss the convergence properties of the sum, as well as the properties of its analytic continuation, and see that just as
\zeta_{\rm R}
, our
\zeta_{D^{2}}(s)
is analytic, except for a single simple pole at
s=1
. Hence, formally taking the derivative of the sum with respect to
s
and setting
s=0
gives as before

\zeta^{\prime}_{D^{2}}(0)=-2\sum_{n}(\log\lambda_{n}^{2})=-2\log\prod_{n}\lambda_{n}^{2}\ .

(3.22)

Of course, the expression on the left-hand side is computed from the analytic continuation and thus provides a way to make sense of the otherwise diverging sum on the right-hand side.
Similarly,
(\log\mu^{2})\,\zeta_{D^{2}}(0)=2\sum_{n}\log\mu^{2}=-2\log\prod_{n}\frac{1}{\mu^{2}}
.
These
\zeta_{D^{2}}
depend on the metric
g
via the dependence of
D^{2}
on
g
. Thus, to indicate the dependence on
g
we should denote them as
\zeta_{D^{2}_{g}}
but for notational convenience we will simple denote them as
\zeta_{g}
.
Then

-2\log\prod_{n}\frac{\lambda_{n}^{2}}{\mu^{2}}=\zeta^{\prime}_{D^{2}_{g}}(0)+(\log\mu^{2})\,\zeta_{D^{2}_{g}}(0)\equiv\zeta^{\prime}_{g}(0)+(\log\mu^{2})\,\zeta_{g}(0)\ .

(3.23)

It follows that the (regularized) gravitational action is

\begin{array}[]{|c|}\hline\cr\\
\displaystyle S_{\rm grav}[g,\widehat{g}]=\frac{1}{4}\Big(\zeta_{g}^{\prime}(0)+(\log\mu^{2})\,\zeta_{g}(0)\Big)-\frac{1}{4}\Big(\zeta_{\widehat{g}}^{\prime}(0)+(\log\mu^{2})\,\zeta_{\widehat{g}}^{\prime}(0)\Big)\ .\\
\\
\hline\cr\end{array}

(3.24)

We want to determine this gravitational action for
g_{\mu\nu}(x)=e^{2\sigma(x)}\widehat{g}_{\mu\nu}(x)
. Our strategy will be to first determine
\delta S_{\rm grav}
for infinitesimal
\delta\sigma
(around an arbitrary metric
g
) and then “integrate” this variation to obtain
S_{\rm grav}[g,\widehat{g}]
. Obviously, the variation of the gravitational action is given in terms of the variation of the zeta-function
\zeta_{g}(s)
(and of its derivative
\zeta^{\prime}_{g}(s)
) around
s=0
. To obtain this variation, we need to study the variations of the eigenvalues
\lambda_{n}
under a corresponding variation of the Dirac operator
D
, which we will do next. To fully exploit these results we will need to study in some detail Green’s functions, local zeta functions and local heat kernels and their variations - which will be the subject of section 4.

3.3
Variation of the eigenvalues : perturbation theory

We want to study how the eigenvalues
\lambda_{n}
(or
\lambda_{n}^{2}
) change under local conformal rescalings of the metric with some
\delta\sigma(x)
. The variation
\delta D=i\delta{\nabla\hskip-8.53581pt/}
of the Dirac operator
D
has been obtained in sect.
2.4
, see eqs (
2.80
) and (
2.84
).
Recall that
D\chi_{n}=i\lambda_{n}\phi_{n}
and
D\phi_{n}=-i\lambda_{n}\chi_{n}
. Then
under
D\to D+\delta D
we have
\lambda_{n}\to\lambda_{n}+\delta\lambda_{n}
, as well as
\chi_{n}\to\chi_{n}+\delta\chi_{n}
and
\phi_{n}\to\phi_{n}+\delta\phi_{n}
:

(D+\delta D)(\chi_{n}+\delta\chi_{n})=i(\lambda_{n}+\delta\lambda_{n})(\phi_{n}+\delta\phi_{n})\quad\Rightarrow\quad\ \delta D\chi_{n}+D\delta\chi_{n}=i\delta\lambda_{n}\phi_{n}+i\lambda_{n}\delta\phi_{n}\ .

(3.25)

Taking the inner product with
\phi_{n}
and using the hermiticity of
D
one gets

i\delta\lambda_{n}(\phi_{n},\phi_{n})=(\phi_{n},\delta D\chi_{n})+(\phi_{n},D\delta\chi_{n})-i\lambda_{n}(\phi_{n},\delta\phi_{n})=(\phi_{n},\delta D\chi_{n})+i\lambda_{n}(\chi_{n},\delta\chi_{n})-i\lambda_{n}(\phi_{n},\delta\phi_{n})

(3.26)

so that

\delta\lambda_{n}=-i(\phi_{n},\delta D\chi_{n})+\lambda_{n}\Big((\chi_{n},\delta\chi_{n})-(\phi_{n},\delta\phi_{n})\Big)\ .

(3.27)

In “ordinary” first order perturbation theory in quantum mechanics one has a relation analogous to
(\chi_{n},\delta\chi_{n})=(\phi_{n},\delta\phi_{n})=0
that translates that both
\chi_{n}
and
\chi_{n}+\delta\chi_{n}
are normalised to one, and similarly for the
\phi_{n}
. Here, however, one must take into account that the
\chi_{n}
and
\phi_{n}
are normalised with the metric
g
, while
\chi_{n}+\delta\chi_{n}
and
\phi_{n}+\delta\phi_{n}
are normalised with the metric
e^{2\delta\sigma}g
. Let us write out what this implies (to first order in the perturbation) :

1=\int{\rm d}^{2}x\,\sqrt{g}\,e^{2\delta\sigma}\,(\chi_{n}+\delta\chi_{n})^{\dagger}(\chi_{n}+\delta\chi_{n})=\int{\rm d}^{2}x\,\sqrt{g}\,\big(\chi_{n}^{\dagger}\chi_{n}+2\delta\sigma\chi_{n}^{\dagger}\chi_{n}+\delta\chi_{n}^{\dagger}\chi_{n}+\chi_{n}^{\dagger}\delta\chi_{n}\big)

(3.28)

The first term
\int{\rm d}^{2}x\,\sqrt{g}\,\chi_{n}^{\dagger}\chi_{n}
is just
(\chi_{n},\chi_{n})=1
, while
\delta\chi_{n}^{\dagger}\chi_{n}=\delta\chi_{n}^{T}\chi_{n}=\chi_{n}^{T}\delta\chi_{n}=\chi_{n}^{\dagger}\delta\chi_{n}
and we get

(\chi_{n},\delta\chi_{n})=-\int{\rm d}^{2}x\sqrt{g}\,\delta\sigma(x)\chi_{n}^{\dagger}(x)\chi_{n}(x)\equiv-\int\delta\sigma\,\chi_{n}^{\dagger}\chi_{n}\ ,

(3.29)

where
\int\ldots
is short-hand for
\int{\rm d}^{2}x\sqrt{g}\ldots
.
Similarly,
(\phi_{n},\delta\phi_{n})=-\int\delta\sigma\phi_{n}^{\dagger}\phi_{n}
. Replacing
\delta D=i\delta{\nabla\hskip-8.53581pt/}
, we can then rewrite (
3.27
) as

\delta\lambda_{n}=(\phi_{n},\delta{\nabla\hskip-8.53581pt/}\,\chi_{n})+\lambda_{n}\int\delta\sigma\big(\phi_{n}^{\dagger}\phi_{n}-\chi_{n}^{\dagger}\chi_{n}\big)\ .

(3.30)

Next, we insert
\delta{\nabla\hskip-8.53581pt/}=-\delta\sigma{\nabla\hskip-8.53581pt/}+\frac{1}{2}{\partial\hskip-6.25958pt/}\delta\sigma
, cf. (
2.84
), and integrate by parts (cf (
2.29
)), so that

\displaystyle(\phi_{n},\delta{\nabla\hskip-8.53581pt/}\,\chi_{n})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\int\delta\sigma\phi_{n}^{\dagger}{\nabla\hskip-8.53581pt/}\,\chi_{n}+\frac{1}{2}\int\partial_{\lambda}\delta\sigma\phi_{n}^{\dagger}\gamma^{\lambda}\chi_{n}=-\int\delta\sigma\Big(\phi_{n}^{\dagger}{\nabla\hskip-8.53581pt/}\,\chi_{n}+\frac{1}{2}({\nabla\hskip-8.53581pt/}\,\phi_{n})^{\dagger}\chi_{n}+\frac{1}{2}\phi_{n}{\nabla\hskip-8.53581pt/}\,\chi_{n}\Big)

(3.31)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\int\delta\sigma\Big(-i\phi_{n}^{\dagger}(D-m\gamma_{*})\chi_{n}+\frac{i}{2}\big((D-m\gamma_{*})\phi_{n}\big)^{\dagger}\chi_{n}-\frac{i}{2}\phi_{n}^{\dagger}(D-m\gamma_{*})\chi_{n}\Big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\int\delta\sigma\Big(\frac{3}{2}\lambda_{n}\phi_{n}^{\dagger}\phi_{n}-\frac{1}{2}\lambda_{n}\chi_{n}^{\dagger}\chi_{n}+im\phi_{n}^{\dagger}\gamma_{*}\chi_{n}\Big)\ ,

and then

\delta\lambda_{n}=-\int\delta\sigma\Big(\frac{\lambda_{n}}{2}(\phi_{n}^{\dagger}\phi_{n}+\chi_{n}^{\dagger}\chi_{n})+im\phi_{n}^{\dagger}\gamma_{*}\chi_{n}\Big)\ .

(3.32)

Since
\phi_{n}
,
\chi_{n}
and
i\gamma_{*}
are real, we have
\phi_{n}^{\dagger}i\gamma_{*}\chi_{n}=(\phi_{n}^{\dagger}i\gamma_{*}\chi_{n})^{\dagger}=\chi_{n}^{\dagger}(-i)\gamma_{*}\phi_{n}
, and we can also rewrite this as

\delta\lambda_{n}=-\frac{1}{2}\int\delta\sigma\Big(\lambda_{n}(\phi_{n}^{\dagger}\phi_{n}+\chi_{n}^{\dagger}\chi_{n})+im(\phi_{n}^{\dagger}\gamma_{*}\chi_{n}-\chi_{n}^{\dagger}\gamma_{*}\phi_{n})\Big)\ .

(3.33)

This allows us to express the variation of
\zeta(s)
as

\delta\zeta(s)=2\sum_{n}\delta\big(\lambda_{n}^{-2s}\big)=-4s\sum_{n}\frac{\delta\lambda_{n}}{\lambda_{n}^{2s+1}}=2s\hskip-2.84526pt\int\delta\sigma\sum_{n}\Big(\frac{1}{\lambda_{n}^{2s}}(\phi_{n}^{\dagger}\phi_{n}+\chi_{n}^{\dagger}\chi_{n})+im\frac{1}{\lambda_{n}^{2s+1}}(\phi_{n}^{\dagger}\gamma_{*}\chi_{n}-\chi_{n}^{\dagger}\gamma_{*}\phi_{n})\Big).

(3.34)

Let us make a remark about zero-modes if they are present. By zero-modes we here mean solutions
\psi_{0,i}
of
i{\nabla\hskip-8.53581pt/}\,\psi_{0,i}=0
, or in terms of the real and imaginary parts
i{\nabla\hskip-8.53581pt/}\,\phi_{0,i}=i{\nabla\hskip-8.53581pt/}\,\chi_{0,i}=0
. Then
i\lambda_{0}\phi_{0,i}=D\chi_{0,i}=m\gamma_{*}\chi_{0,i}
so that necessarily
\lambda_{0}=m
and
\gamma_{*}\chi_{0,i}=i\phi_{0,i}
. Similarly
\gamma_{*}\phi_{0,i}=-i\chi_{0,i}
. We have seen that under a conformal transformation the zero-modes change in a rather simple way so as to remain zero-modes of the conformally transformed
i{\nabla\hskip-8.53581pt/}
. In particular then the new eigenvalue is still
\lambda_{0}=m
, so that

\delta\lambda_{0}=0\ .

(3.35)

Let us check this from (
3.32
). One has
\phi_{0,i}^{\dagger}\phi_{0,i}=-i\phi_{0,i}^{\dagger}\gamma_{*}\chi_{0,i}
, and
\chi_{0,i}^{\dagger}\chi_{0,i}=i\chi_{0,i}^{\dagger}\gamma_{*}\phi_{0,i}=\big(i\chi_{0,i}^{\dagger}\gamma_{*}\phi_{0,i}\big)^{\dagger}

=-i\phi_{0,i}^{\dagger}\gamma_{*}\chi_{0,i}
. It follows that the bracket on the right-hand side of (
3.32
) vanishes for
n=0
, confirming
\delta\lambda_{0}=0
. It follows that in (
3.34
) we can just as well exclude the contributions of the zero-modes and replace
\sum_{n}\to\sum_{n\neq 0}
.

4
The tool box : heat kernels, zeta-function and Green’s functions

In this section we set up and discuss the technical tools we need to obtain the gravitational action. This includes in particular local heat kernels, local zeta functions and Green’s functions. As we will see, they are all related through various relations. An important detailed computation about one of the two the heat kernels we introduce, namely
K_{+}
, is deferred to appendix A. Some partial results about the other heat kernel (
K_{-}
) are presented in appendix B. General references on the heat kernel are
[
21
,
16
,
22
]
.

4.1
The basic tools : definitions, relations and properties

Throughout this section we assume the mass
m
is non-vanishing, so that
D=i{\nabla\hskip-8.53581pt/}+m\gamma_{*}
has no zero-modes. As discussed above,
i{\nabla\hskip-8.53581pt/}
has zero-modes for manifolds of genus
{\tt g}\geq 1
, but the non-vanishing mass implies that
\lambda_{n}\geq m>0
. The absence of zero-modes of
D
is an important assumption. It means in particular that all zeta-functions
\zeta
, Green’s functions
G
, determinants
\,{\rm Det}\,
, etc can be straightforwardly defined in terms of sums or products of
all
eigenvalues / eigenfunctions. On the other hand, in the zero-mass limit the zero-modes of
i{\nabla\hskip-8.53581pt/}
become zero-modes of
D
. One can then still define corresponding quantities
\widetilde{\zeta}
,
\widetilde{G}
,
\,{\rm Det}\,^{\prime}
etc by excluding the zero-modes from the sum / product.
However, these modified quantities satisfy different relations from those satisfied by
\zeta
,
G
and
\,{\rm Det}\,
. For example, the Green’s function
G(x,y)
of
D^{2}
satisfies
D^{2}_{x}G(x,y)=\delta^{(2)}(x-y){\bf 1}_{2\times 2}
while in the presence of zero-modes,
\widetilde{G}(x,y)
satisfies a similar relation,
D^{2}_{x}\widetilde{G}(x,y)=\delta^{(2)}(x-y){\bf 1}_{2\times 2}-P_{0}(x,y)
where on the right-hand side one must subtract
P_{0}(x,y)
, the projector on the zero-modes, which was discussed in subsection
2.5
. Since we are interested in the effective gravitational action for massive matter, one might think that this complication just does not occur. However, for massive bosonic (scalar) matter a fruitful method was to do a small mass expansion around zero-mass, and the same will be true here. One must then express everything in terms of quantities that have a smooth limit ass
m\to 0
, and these are the
\widetilde{G}
,
\widetilde{\zeta}
, etc. On the torus we could compute the zero-mode projector exactly, and on higher genus Riemann surfaces we could still sufficiently characterise its structure. This will allow us in the next section to obtain the variation of the gravitational action in an expansion in
m^{2}
to all orders, and on arbitrary genus Riemann surfaces. This is quite some progress with respect to
[
1
]
!

Having said this, we emphasize again that throughout this section we assume that
D
has no zero-modes (i.e. that either
m\neq 0
or that we deal with spherical topology). Recall that we had defined the complex eigenfunctions
\psi_{n}
and
\psi_{n}^{*}
(and hence also the real eigenfunctions
\chi_{n}
and
\phi_{n}
) such that
\lambda_{n}>0
and

D\psi_{n}=\lambda_{n}\psi_{n}\quad,\quad D\psi_{n}^{*}=-\lambda_{n}\psi_{n}^{*}\quad,\quad D\chi_{n}=i\lambda_{n}\phi_{n}\quad,\quad D\phi_{n}=-i\lambda_{n}\chi_{n}\ .

(4.1)

In terms of these eigenfunctions
the completeness relation reads

\sum_{n}\big(\chi_{n}(x)\chi_{n}^{\dagger}(y)+\phi_{n}(x)\phi_{n}^{\dagger}(y)\big)=\sum_{n}\big(\psi_{n}(x)\psi_{n}^{\dagger}(y)+\psi^{*}_{n}(x)(\psi_{n}^{*}(y))^{\dagger}\big)=\frac{\delta^{(2)}(x-y)}{\sqrt{g}}\ {\bf 1}_{2\times 2}\ .

(4.2)

4.1.1
Local zeta-functions

We define two local zeta-functions
\zeta_{+}(s,x,y)
and
\zeta_{-}(s,x,y)
as

\displaystyle\zeta_{+}(s,x,y)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{n}\lambda_{n}^{-2s}\big(\chi_{n}(x)\chi_{n}^{\dagger}(y)+\phi_{n}(x)\phi_{n}^{\dagger}(y)\big)\ ,

\displaystyle\zeta_{-}(s,x,y)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{n}\lambda_{n}^{-2s}\big(\chi_{n}(x)\phi_{n}^{\dagger}(y)-\phi_{n}(x)\chi_{n}^{\dagger}(y)\big)\ ,

(4.3)

where
x=(x^{1},x^{2})
and
y=(y^{1},y^{2})
denote points on the manifold. Note that these local zeta-functions are real
2\times 2
-matrices.
They can also be rewritten in terms of the
\psi_{n}
and
\psi_{n}^{*}
as

\displaystyle\zeta_{+}(s,x,y)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{n}\lambda_{n}^{-2s}\big(\psi_{n}(x)\psi_{n}^{\dagger}(y)+\psi^{*}_{n}(x)(\psi_{n}^{*})^{\dagger}(y)\big)\ ,

\displaystyle\zeta_{-}(s,x,y)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle i\ \sum_{n}\lambda_{n}^{-2s}\big(\psi_{n}(x)\psi_{n}^{\dagger}(y)-\psi^{*}_{n}(x)(\psi_{n}^{*})^{\dagger}(y)\big)\ .

(4.4)

As we will see below,
\zeta_{+}(1,x,y)
is the Green’s function of
D^{2}
, while
-i\zeta_{-}(\frac{1}{2},x,y)
is the Green’s function of
D
. It is for this reason that we need to introduce the “strange-looking”
\zeta_{-}(s,x,y)
.

As mentioned above, we will also encounter the local zeta-functions
\widetilde{\zeta}_{\pm}(s,x,y)
with the zero-modes of
i{\nabla\hskip-8.53581pt/}\,
(if present) excluded from the sums. The contributions of these zero-modes is precisely given in terms of the zero-mode projectors
P_{0}
defined in (
2.102
) and
Q_{0}
defined in (
2.106
). Hence we obviously have

\widetilde{\zeta}_{+}(s,x,y)=\zeta_{+}(s,x,y)-\frac{P_{0}(x,y)}{m^{2s}}\quad,\quad\widetilde{\zeta}_{-}(s,x,y)=\zeta_{+}(s,x,y)-i\,\frac{Q_{0}(x,y)}{m^{2s}}\ .

(4.5)

There is an important relation between
\zeta_{+}
and
\zeta_{-}
which generalises the relation between the corresponding Green’s functions.
It follows immediately from (
4.1
) and the definitions (
4.1.1
) that

\displaystyle D_{x}\,\zeta_{+}(s,x,y)=-i\,\zeta_{-}(s-\frac{1}{2},x,y)\quad

,

\displaystyle\quad D_{x}\,\zeta_{-}(s,x,y)=i\,\zeta_{+}(s-\frac{1}{2},x,y)\ ,

\displaystyle D_{x}^{2}\,\zeta_{\pm}(s,x,y)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\zeta_{\pm}(s-1,x,y)\ ,

(4.6)

where the subscript
x
on
D
indicates that the derivatives are with respect to
x
. Since
D_{x}P_{0}(x,y)=m\gamma_{*}P_{0}(x,y)=mQ_{0}(x,y)
and
D_{x}Q_{0}(x,y)=m\gamma_{*}Q_{0}(x,y)=mP_{0}(x,y)
, these relations (
4.1.1
) immediately also carry over to the
\widetilde{\zeta}_{\pm}(s,x,y)
.

Denoting the trace over the
2\times 2
matrices by
\,{\rm tr}\,
, and using the orthonormality of the
\phi_{n}
and
\chi_{n}
we have

\int{\rm d}^{2}x\sqrt{g}\,\,{\rm tr}\,\,\zeta_{+}(s,x,x)=2\sum_{n}\lambda_{n}^{-2s}\equiv\zeta(s)\quad,\quad\int{\rm d}^{2}x\sqrt{g}\,\,{\rm tr}\,\zeta_{-}(s,x,x)=0\ ,

(4.7)

where
\zeta(s)
is the zeta function of
D^{2}
.

The following (anti-) hermiticity relations follow directly from the definitions (
4.1.1
) :

\displaystyle\big(\zeta_{+}(s,x,y)\big)^{\dagger}=\zeta_{+}(s,y,x)\quad

\displaystyle\Rightarrow

\displaystyle\quad\text{$\zeta_{+}(s,x,x)$ is real and hermitian}\ ,

\displaystyle\big(\zeta_{-}(s,x,y)\big)^{\dagger}=-\zeta_{-}(s,y,x)\quad

\displaystyle\Rightarrow

\displaystyle\quad\text{$\zeta_{-}(s,x,x)$ is real and anti-hermitian}\ .

(4.8)

There are 3 possible hermitian and real matrices, namely
\sigma_{x},\ \sigma_{z}
and
{\bf 1}_{2\times 2}
, while there is only a single real and anti-hermitian matrix, namely
i\gamma_{*}
. Hence, we can already assert that

\zeta_{-}(s,x,x)\sim i\gamma_{*}\ .

(4.9)

On the other hand, for
\zeta_{+}(s,x,x)
the present argument would allow 3 possible matrix structures, while later-on we will find that
\zeta_{+}(s,x,x)\sim{\bf 1}_{2\times 2}
.

The convergence properties of these local zeta-functions depend essentially on the large-
n
behaviour of the eigenvalues
\lambda_{n}^{2}
. Indeed, equation (
4.7
) more or less suggests that multiplying the
\lambda_{n}^{-2s}
by
\chi_{n}^{\dagger}(x)\chi_{n}(y)
and
\phi_{n}^{\dagger}(x)\phi_{n}(y)
does not change the convergence properties.
17
17

17

This argument is certainly true for the
convergence
properties of the sum. For large eigenvalues, we expect the corresponding
\psi_{n}(x)\psi_{n}^{\dagger}(y)
to be rapidly oscillating functions of
x
and
y
, leading to cancellations between adjacent terms in the sum, and thus to a possible improvement of the convergence of the sum. But this rapidly oscillating behaviour and the corresponding cancellations also mean that one has to examine more carefully the usual argument made for the heat kernel, see below, that for small
t
the sum is dominated by the large eigenvalues.

Of course, discussing the convergence of a sum of functions (or even functions of 2 arguments) needs somewhat more care and requires specifying an appropriate norm in which the convergence is to be appreciated, but without going into these details it is clear that we get a good deal of information about the convergence of the local zeta-functions by studying the behaviour of the large eigenvalues, which amounts to studying the convergence of the (integrated) zeta-function
\zeta(s)
.

The behaviour of the large eigenvalues of
D^{2}
is dictated by the leading 2-derivative term in
D^{2}
which, by (
2.48
), is the same as the one of the scalar Laplacian, and which is the same as in flat space (with periodic boundary conditions). This amounts to saying that the large eigenvalue behaviour (“UV-behaviour”) reflects the short-distance properties of the manifold, and at short distances all manifolds are locally flat. Thus the leading large
n
behaviour of the
\lambda_{n}
must be the same as in the case of the flat torus where the eigenvalues depend on two integers
n_{1}
and
n_{2}
.
We have seen before that for general periods of the torus the eigenvalues are
\lambda_{\vec{n}}^{2}=n_{1}^{2}+\big(\frac{n_{2}+\tau_{1}n_{1}}{\tau_{2}}\big)^{2}+m^{2}
.
Thus, for large eigenvalues we will always have
\lambda_{n}^{2}\equiv\lambda^{2}_{\vec{n}}\simeq a^{2}\big[n_{1}^{2}+\big(\frac{n_{2}+\tau_{1}n_{1}}{\tau_{2}}\big)^{2}\big]
for some
a
which sets the scale. This is essentially the statement of Weyl’s law
[
23
]
about the large eigenvalues of the Laplace operator.
18
18

18

We see that the behaviour of the large eigenvalues does not depend much on the details of the geometry. It depends to some extend on the global structure like boundary conditions. And it does, of course, crucially depend on the dimensionality of the manifold.

Then for large
|n_{1}|
and large
|n_{2}|
we approximately have

\sum_{(n_{1},n_{2})\neq(0,0)}\lambda_{n}^{-2s}\simeq a^{-2s}\sum_{(n_{1},n_{2})\neq(0,0)}\big[n_{1}^{2}+\big(\frac{n_{2}+\tau_{1}n_{1}}{\tau_{2}}\big)^{2}\big]^{-s}\simeq a^{-2s}\int_{\vec{k}\notin{\cal D}}{\rm d}^{2}k\,\big[k_{1}^{2}+\big(\frac{k_{2}+\tau_{1}k_{1}}{\tau_{2}}\big)^{2}\big]^{-s}\ ,

(4.10)

where
{\cal D}
is some domain “of unit radius” around the origin. Assuming convergence, we change variables to
p_{1}=k_{1},\ p_{2}=\frac{k_{2}+\tau_{1}k_{1}}{\tau_{2}}
and go to polar coordinates so that

\sum_{n_{1},n_{2}}\lambda_{n}^{-2s}\simeq 2\pi a^{-2s}\tau_{2}\int_{1}^{\infty}{\rm d}p\,p\,(p^{2})^{-s}=\pi a^{-2s}\tau_{2}\int_{1}^{\infty}{\rm d}\xi\,\xi^{-s}\simeq\pi a^{-2s}\tau_{2}\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}n^{-s}=\pi a^{-2s}\tau_{2}\,\zeta_{\rm R}(s)\ .

(4.11)

We see that the convergence properties of the zeta-functions of Laplace-like operators in two dimensions are exactly the same as for Riemann’s zeta-function !
It follows that, just as for the sum defining Riemann’s zeta function
\zeta_{\rm R}(s)
, the zeta function
\zeta(s)\equiv\zeta_{D^{2}}(s)
is defined by a convergent sum for
\Re s>1
. As explained before, we then also expect that the sums defining the local zeta-functions
\zeta_{\pm}(s,x,y)
are convergent expressions for
\Re s>1
and are otherwise defined by analytical continuation. Below, we will indeed confirm this expectation and show that, for
x\neq y,

\zeta_{\pm}(s,x,y)
can be defined as analytic functions for all
s\in{\bf C}
, and that
\zeta_{+}(s,x,x)
has a single pole at
s=1
while
\zeta_{-}(s,x,x)
has a single pole at
s=\frac{1}{2}
.

4.1.2
Local heat kernels

We similarly define local heat kernels that are again
2\times 2
-matrices :

\displaystyle K_{+}(t,x,y)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{n}e^{-\lambda_{n}^{2}t}\big(\chi_{n}(x)\chi_{n}^{\dagger}(y)+\phi_{n}(x)\phi_{n}^{\dagger}(y)\big)=\sum_{n}e^{-\lambda_{n}^{2}t}\big(\psi_{n}(x)\psi_{n}^{\dagger}(y)+\psi^{*}_{n}(x)(\psi_{n}^{*})^{\dagger}(y)\big)\ ,

\displaystyle K_{-}(t,x,y)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{n}e^{-\lambda_{n}^{2}t}\big(\chi_{n}(x)\phi_{n}^{\dagger}(y)-\phi_{n}(x)\chi_{n}^{\dagger}(y)\big)=i\ \sum_{n}e^{-\lambda_{n}^{2}t}\big(\psi_{n}(x)\psi_{n}^{\dagger}(y)-\psi^{*}_{n}(x)(\psi_{n}^{*})^{\dagger}(y)\big)\ .

Again, if necessary, one can also define the corresponding local heat kernels with the zero-modes of
i{\nabla\hskip-8.53581pt/}\,
excluded as

\widetilde{K}_{+}(t,x,y)=K_{+}(t,x,y)-e^{-m^{2}t}P_{0}(x,y)\quad,\quad\widetilde{K}_{-}(t,x,y)=K_{-}(t,x,y)-ie^{-m^{2}t}Q_{0}(x,y)\ .

(4.13)

One could also define

{\cal K}(t,x,y)=\frac{1}{2}K_{+}(t,x,y)-\frac{i}{2}K_{-}(t,x,y)=\sum_{n}e^{-\lambda_{n}^{2}t}\psi_{n}(x)\psi_{n}^{\dagger}(y)\ .

(4.14)

Then the real functions
K_{\pm}
constitute (2 times) the real and (
-2
times) the imaginary parts of
2{\cal K}
.

It follows from (
4.1.2
) that the large-
t
behaviour of the heat kernels is controlled by the smallest eigenvalue. Since we assumed that there is no zero-mode, we have
\lambda_{n}^{2}>0
and for
t\to\infty
all our heat kernels vanish (at least) as
e^{-\lambda_{\rm min}^{2}t}
, where
\lambda_{\rm min}
denotes the smallest eigenvalue :

K_{\pm}(t,x,y)\sim_{t\to\infty}{\cal O}(e^{-\lambda_{\rm min}^{2}t})\ .

(4.15)

From the definitions (
4.1.2
) one sees that
K_{+}
and
K_{-}
(and hence also
{\cal K}
) satisfy the “generalised heat equation”

\left(\frac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}t}+D_{x}^{2}\right)K_{\pm}(t,x,y)=0\ ,

(4.16)

where the subscript
x
on
D^{2}
indicates that the derivatives are with respect to
x
.
In appendix A we explain how one can obtain the asymptotic small-
t
expansion of
K_{+}(t,x,y)
from this differential equation and an appropriate “initial” condition for
K_{+}
. Indeed, one sees from the definition (
4.1.2
) and the closure relation (
4.2
) that for
t\to 0
we have

K_{+}(t,x,y)\sim\frac{\delta^{(2)}(x-y)}{\sqrt{g}}\,{\bf 1}_{2\times 2}\ ,

(4.17)

which is the “usual” initial condition for a heat kernel, as also assumed in the appendix. However, no such simple initial condition holds for
K_{-}
. This is why the method of appendix A yields
K_{+}
and not
K_{-}
.
In particular, in the appendix we work out the relevant first few terms of the small-
t
expansion of
K_{+}
which we will need below.

Another way to see the differences between
K_{+}
and
K_{-}
is to note that
K_{+}
contains the full sum of all eigenfunctions of
D
, namely the
\psi_{n}
and the
\psi_{n}^{*}
. Thus if one uses a different basis for these eigenfunctions one still gets the same
K_{+}
. Moreover, the
\psi_{n}
and the
\psi_{n}^{*}
appear symmetrically. We can write
K_{+}(t,x,y)=\left\langle x\right|\sum_{\lambda_{n}^{2}}e^{-\lambda_{n}^{2}t}P(\lambda_{n}^{2})\left|y\right\rangle
where
P(\lambda_{n}^{2})
is the projector on the eigenspace of
D^{2}
with eigenvalue
\lambda_{n}^{2}
. This makes clear that one could use any basis of eigenfunctions of
D^{2}
. This also explains why one is able (in principle) to obtain
K_{+}
uniquely by solving the heat equation (
4.16
) for the operator
D^{2}
with the appropriately prescribed initial condition. However, this is not true for the imaginary part
K_{-}
of
{\cal K}
. We see from (
4.1.2
) that the definition of
K_{-}
is not simply a sum over all eigenfunctions of
D^{2}
, but that we made a certain distinction between the eigenfunctions of
D
with eigenvalues
\lambda_{n}>0
and those with eigenvalues
-\lambda_{n}<0
. (Recall that we always take
\lambda_{n}>0
.) Clearly, the operator
D^{2}
does not make this distinction, and hence, one cannot simply get the
K_{-}
by solving (
4.16
).

What can be said about the matrix structure of the heat kernel
K_{+}(t,x,y)
? As just explained,
K_{+}
can be obtained by solving the heat equation (
4.16
) with initial condition (
4.17
). Now
it is clear from (
2.43
) and (
2.48
), that the matrix structure of
D^{2}
is
{\bf 1}(\ldots)+\gamma_{*}(\ldots)
. It then follows that
K_{+}(t,x,y)
must have the same matrix structure:

K_{+}(t,x,y)=K_{+}^{0}(t,x,y)\,{\bf 1}+K_{+}^{*}(t,x,y)\,\gamma_{*}\ .

(4.18)

What can be said about
K_{-}
? Define an auxiliary quantity
L(t,x,y)
as

L(t,x,y)=i\sum_{n}\frac{e^{-\lambda_{n}^{2}t}}{\lambda_{n}}\big(\chi_{n}(x)\chi_{n}^{\dagger}(y)+\phi_{n}(x)\phi_{n}^{\dagger}(y)\big)=i\sum_{n}\frac{e^{-\lambda^{2}_{n}t}}{\lambda_{n}}\big(\psi_{n}(x)\psi_{n}^{\dagger}(y)+\psi^{*}_{n}(x)(\psi_{n}^{*})^{\dagger}(y)\big)\ ,

(4.19)

which is now constructed from the eigenfunctions and eigenvalues of
D^{2}
only, without any distinction between positive and negative eigenvalues. We then have

K_{-}(t,x,y)=D_{x}L(t,x,y)\ ,

(4.20)

Obviously,
L(t,x,y)
also satisfies the heat equation (
4.16
). Its initial condition is

L(t,x,y)\sim_{t\to 0}\ i\sum_{n}\frac{1}{\lambda_{n}}\big(\chi_{n}(x)\chi_{n}^{\dagger}(y)+\phi_{n}(x)\phi_{n}^{\dagger}(y)\big)\ .

(4.21)

However, contrary to the completeness relation, we do not recognise here any known function (or distribution). This prevents us from obtaining
L
in analogy to what is done for
K_{+}
in appendix A.

Let us nevertheless see what we can say about
K_{-}
and
K_{+}
on general grounds.
In analogy with the discussion for the local heat kernels, it follows directly from the definitions (
4.1.2
) of
K_{+}
and
K_{-}
in terms of the eigenfunctions
\psi_{n}
and
\psi_{n}^{*}
that

(K_{+}(t,x,y))^{\dagger}=K_{+}(t,y,x)\quad,\quad(K_{-}(t,x,y))^{\dagger}=-K_{-}(t,y,x)\ ,

(4.22)

so that for
x=y
we simply have

(K_{+}(t,x,x))^{\dagger}=K_{+}(t,x,x)\quad,\quad(K_{-}(t,x,x))^{\dagger}=-K_{-}(t,x,x)\ .

(4.23)

This means that
K_{+}(t,x,x)
is hermitian and
K_{-}(t,x,x)
is anti-hermitian. Moreover, they are both real. The only real and anti-hermitian matrix is
i\gamma_{*}
and it follows that :

\begin{array}[]{|c|}\hline\cr\\
\displaystyle K_{+}(t,x,x)\sim{\bf 1}_{2\times 2}\quad,\quad K_{-}(t,x,x)\sim\gamma_{*}\ .\\
\\
\hline\cr\end{array}

(4.24)

4.1.3
Relating zeta functions and heat kernels

There is a well-known relation between zeta-functions and heat kernels which generalises the relation (
3.19
) for Riemann’s zeta function. This relation also generalises to the local zeta-functions and local heat kernels we have defined :

\begin{array}[]{|c|}\hline\cr\\
\displaystyle\zeta_{\pm}(s,x,y)=\frac{1}{\Gamma(s)}\int_{0}^{\infty}{\rm d}t\,t^{s-1}\,K_{\pm}(t,x,y)\ .\\
\\
\hline\cr\end{array}

(4.25)

Indeed, these relations (
4.25
) follow trivially (for
\Re s>1
) from inserting the definitions of
K_{\pm}
as a sum over the eigenvalues and eigenfunction, interchanging sum and integration and doing the integrations term per term as
(\Gamma(s))^{-1}\int_{0}^{\infty}{\rm d}t\,t^{s-1}\,e^{-t\lambda^{2}_{n}}=\lambda_{n}^{-2s}(\Gamma(s))^{-1}\int_{0}^{\infty}{\rm d}t^{\prime}\,{t^{\prime}}^{s-1}\,e^{-t^{\prime}}=\lambda_{n}^{-2s}
. Then, the relation (
4.25
) can be obtained on all of
{\bf C}
as an identity between meromorphic functions by analytic continuation.

The relation (
4.25
) is a special case of the Mellin transform. The Mellin transform
{\cal M}(f)
of a (continuous) function
f
is

\varphi(s)\equiv({\cal M}(f))(s)=\int_{0}^{\infty}{\rm d}t\,f(t)\,t^{s-1}\ .

(4.26)

If this integral is absolutely convergent in a strip
a<\Re s<b
, then
\varphi(s)
is analytic in this strip, and
f
can be recovered by the inverse Mellin transform defined as

f(t)=({\cal M}^{-1}(\varphi))(t)=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{c-i\infty}^{c+i\infty}{\rm d}s\,\varphi(s)\,t^{-s}\ ,\quad a<c<b\ .

(4.27)

We can then interpret equation (
4.25
) by saying that the
\Gamma(s)\,\zeta_{\pm}(s,x,y)
are the Mellin transforms of the heat kernels
K_{\pm}(t,x,y)
. The inverse Mellin transform then allows us to get the heat kernels back from the zeta functions as

K_{\pm}(t,x,y)=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{c-i\infty}^{c+i\infty}{\rm d}s\,t^{-s}\,\Gamma(s)\,\zeta_{\pm}(s,x,y)\ ,

(4.28)

where the real
c
must be chosen such that
\Gamma(c+i\sigma)\,\zeta_{\pm}(c+i\sigma,x,y)
is analytic for all real
\sigma
and tends uniformly to zero as
\sigma\to\pm\infty
. One can show that any
c>1
is a convenient choice. This is discussed in more detail below.

The relation (
4.25
) translates (
4.24
) into the corresponding relations for the zeta-functions at coinciding points, confirming the structure of
\zeta_{-}(s,x,x)
already obtained earlier, and providing the corresponding result for
\zeta_{+}(s,x,x)
:

\begin{array}[]{|c|}\hline\cr\\
\displaystyle\zeta_{+}(s,x,x)\sim{\bf 1}_{2\times 2}\quad,\quad\zeta_{-}(s,x,x)\sim\gamma_{*}\ .\\
\\
\hline\cr\end{array}

(4.29)

As already discussed, one can get quite some information about
K_{+}
from the differential equation it satisfies, which is not the case of
K_{-}
. However, much of the information about
K_{+}
translates via (
4.25
) into corresponding information about
\zeta_{+}
. We can then use the relations
(
4.1.1
) to obtain
\zeta_{-}
from
\zeta_{+}
and obtain the corresponding information about
\zeta_{-}
. Finally one can use the inverse Mellin transformation to deduce statements about
K_{-}
.

To begin with note that, since
\zeta_{+}
can be obtained from
K_{+}
by (
4.25
), its matrix structure must be the same (cf (
4.18
)) :

\zeta_{+}(s,x,y)=\zeta_{+}^{0}(s,x,y){\bf 1}_{2\times 2}+\zeta_{+}^{*}(s,x,y)\gamma_{*}\ .

(4.30)

Next, recall from (
2.50
) that

i{\nabla\hskip-8.53581pt/}=i\sigma_{x}{\cal D}_{1}+i\sigma_{z}{\cal D}_{2}
, where
{\cal D}_{1}
and
{\cal D}_{2}
are some differential operators not involving any matrix. It follows that
i{\nabla\hskip-8.53581pt/}\,\gamma_{*}=-i\gamma_{*}\,{\nabla\hskip-8.53581pt/}=-\sigma_{z}{\cal D}_{1}+\sigma_{x}{\cal D}_{2}
and

\,{\rm tr}\,i{\nabla\hskip-8.53581pt/}=\,{\rm tr}\,\gamma_{*}i{\nabla\hskip-8.53581pt/}=\,{\rm tr}\,i{\nabla\hskip-8.53581pt/}\,\gamma_{*}=0\ .

(4.31)

Recall the first relation (
4.1.1
) which we rewrite as
\gamma_{*}\zeta_{-}(s,x,y)=i\gamma_{*}(i{\nabla\hskip-8.53581pt/}_{x}+m\gamma_{*})\zeta_{+}(s+\frac{1}{2},x,y)
. Then taking the Dirac trace of this relation, only the part
\sim m
survives :

\,{\rm tr}\,\gamma_{*}\zeta_{-}(s,x,y)=im\,{\rm tr}\,\zeta_{+}(s+\frac{1}{2},x,y)\ .

(4.32)

As we will see in the next subsection, the variation of the gravitational action involves only
\,{\rm tr}\,\zeta_{+}(s,x,x)
and
\,{\rm tr}\,\gamma_{*}\zeta_{-}(s,x,x)
, and the previous relation shows that the knowledge of
\,{\rm tr}\,\zeta_{+}(s,x,x)
should be all we need to know. But we can also easily establish this same relation without the traces : we have seen in (
4.29
) that
\zeta_{-}(s,x,x)\sim\gamma_{*}
and
\zeta_{+}(s,x,x)\sim{\bf 1}_{2\times 2}
, so that indeed

\begin{array}[]{|c|}\hline\cr\\
\displaystyle\zeta_{-}(s,x,x)=im\gamma_{*}\ \zeta_{+}(s+\frac{1}{2},x,x)\ .\\
\\
\hline\cr\end{array}

(4.33)

We will soon show that
\zeta_{+}(s,x,x)
is a meromorphic function with a single pole at
s=1
. The previous relation should be understood as an identity between meromorphic functions and, in particular,
\zeta_{-}(s,x,x)
then has a single pole at
s=\frac{1}{2}
.
Let us insist that one should not conclude from this relation that
\zeta_{-}(s,x,x)
vanishes for
m=0
, since our whole discussion assumed that there are no zero-modes of the Dirac operator. It is only for spherical topology where this conclusion would be correct, while for genus larger or equal to one, we expect
\zeta_{+}(s,x,x)
to contain a contribution
\sim\frac{1}{m^{s}}
, so there is a possible zero-mode contribution to
\zeta_{-}(s,x,x)
even as
m\to 0
.

4.1.4
Small-
t
asymptotics and poles of zeta functions at coinciding points

Let us now study the possible singularities of the integrals (
4.25
) in order to establish the analyticity properties of
\zeta_{\pm}(s,x,y)
.
Since
K_{\pm}
vanish exponentially for large
t
, cf (
4.15
), the integral always converges at
t\to\infty
. Hence any divergences of the integral must come from the region
t\to 0
where the
K_{\pm}
are singular. Thus any singularities (poles) of the zeta-functions are related to the small-
t
behaviour of the heat kernel. Furthermore, since
\Gamma(s)
has poles at
s=0,-1,-2,\ldots
, one sees that
\frac{1}{\Gamma(s)}
vanishes at
s=0,-1,-2,\ldots
, resulting in finite values of the zeta-functions at
s=0,-1,-2,\ldots
. In particular, these finite values are also determined by the divergences of the integral due to the small-
t
behaviour of
K
. We will explain this in further detail below. There is quite some abundant literature on the small-
t
asymptotics of heat kernels associated to various differential operators, in particular at coinciding points
x=y
.

The general expectation is that, due to the presence of
e^{-\lambda^{2}_{n}t}
, the small-
t
behaviour of the heat kernel is related to the behaviour of the large eigenvalues of order
\lambda^{2}_{n}\sim\frac{1}{t}
, and as discussed above, this is related to the leading-derivative terms of the differential operator
D^{2}
and the small-scale structure of the manifold. However, while this expectation turns out to be correct for our heat kernels
K_{\pm}(t,x,x)
at coinciding points, as well as for
K_{+}(t,x,y)
at
x\neq y
, it will
not
be true for
K_{-}(t,x,y)
at
x\neq y
. Indeed, for
x\neq y
, the local heat kernels not only involve the
e^{-\lambda^{2}_{n}t}
but also the
\psi_{n}(x)\psi_{n}^{\dagger}(y)
and the latter typically oscillate rapidly for large
n
(large
\lambda^{2}_{n}
). This can potentially lead to important cancellations and correspondingly the sum no longer is dominated by the eigenvalues of order
\lambda^{2}_{n}\sim\frac{1}{t}
but by much smaller eigenvalues already. Obviously, this will change the small-
t
aymptotics of
K_{-}(t,x,y)
. We will see this very explicitly in appendix B for the
K_{-}(t,x,y)
on the flat torus.

As explained above, our heat kernel
K_{+}
can, in principle, be entirely determined by solving the heat equation. In particular, its small-
t
behaviour can be determined in an asymptotic expansion from the differential equation (
4.16
), with the leading behaviour being the same as in flat space, and the subleading terms being given in terms of the local curvature and derivatives of the curvature. This is studied in some detail in appendix A. Of particular interest will be the expansion at coinciding points
x=y
. It is shown in appendix A eq (
A.49
) that
19
19

19

In the appendix, we have determined the first few coefficients of the heat kernel
K(t,x,y)
as follow from the differential equation. As argued above, this actually only determines
K_{+}(t,x,y)
, so eq (
A.49
) translates into an equation for
K_{+}(t,y,y)
.
Note that, solely from the differential equation, we could also have expected a contributions
\sim\gamma_{*}
. Indeed, such contributions do appear in the intermediate steps of the computation, in particular for
K_{+}(t,x,y)
with
x\neq y
, but they drop out when setting
x=y
to obtain
K_{+}(t,x,x)
, in agreement with the general result (
4.24
).

,

K_{+}(t,x,x)\sim_{t\to 0}\frac{1}{4\pi t}\Big(1-\big(\frac{{\cal R}(x)}{12}+m^{2}\big)\,t+{\cal O}(t^{2})\Big)\,{\bf 1}_{2\times 2}\ .

(4.34)

A heuristic way to understand this result is to note that the heat kernel, at coinciding points, for the two-dimensional scalar Laplacian
\Delta_{\rm scalar}
is well-known (cf (
1.41
)) to be
K_{\Delta_{\rm scalar}}(t,x,x)

=\frac{1}{4\pi t}\big(1+\frac{{\cal R}}{6}t+\ldots\big)
. Now
K_{+}
is the heat kernel for
D^{2}
, which involves the spinorial Laplacian
\Delta_{\rm sp}
which, of course, differs from
\Delta_{\rm scalar}
but has the same leading derivative terms. One might then expect that it also gives the same result to this order
20
20

20

This assumption is not entirely true as one sees from the detailed computations in the appendix. Indeed, the subleading single derivative term gives important contributions to the first subleading term in the small-
t
expansion. Since this first-derivative terms differs between
\Delta_{\rm scalar}
and
\Delta_{\rm sp}
by
-\frac{i}{2}\gamma_{*}\omega^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}
one might have expected a different contribution to the first subleading terms in the small-
t
expansion of
K_{+}
. However, at this order, these additional contributions cancel.

.
Moreover, one expects that the additional
\frac{{\cal R}}{4}+m^{2}
in
D^{2}
simply gives an extra
e^{-({\cal R}/4+m^{2})t}\simeq 1-(\frac{{\cal R}}{4}+m^{2})t
, to this order, resulting in
\frac{1}{4\pi t}\big(1+\frac{{\cal R}}{6}t+\ldots\big)\times\big(1-\frac{{\cal R}}{4}t-m^{2}t+\ldots\big)=\frac{1}{4\pi t}\big(1-\frac{{\cal R}}{12}t-m^{2}t+\ldots\big)
, in agreement with (
4.34
).

Inserting the result (
4.34
) for
K_{+}
into (
4.25
) allows us to get much information about
\zeta_{+}(s,x,x)
as we will explain next. More generally, the small-
t
expansion is (cf appendix A)

K_{+}(t,x,x)\sim_{t\to 0}\frac{{\bf 1}_{2\times 2}}{4\pi t}\ \sum_{r=0}^{\infty}F_{r}(x,x)t^{r}\ \quad,\quad F_{0}(x,x)=1\ .

(4.35)

This is an asymptotic expansion for small
t
which means it disregards any exponentially small terms
{\cal O}(e^{-a/t})
that can be present but are invisible in such an expansion in powers of
t
. As discussed above, we also know that
K_{+}(t,x,x)
vanishes exponentially as
e^{-\lambda^{2}_{\rm min}t}
as
t\to\infty
. It follows that upon
evaluating the integral (
4.25
) any possible divergences can only come from the small-
t
region
21
21

21

We have introduced an arbitrary large mass scale
\mu
so that
\mu^{-2}
is small enough for the asymptotic expansion to be applicable. This is similar to the small but finite
\epsilon
we had introduced when discussing Riemann’s zeta function.

\int_{0}^{\mu^{-2}}\ldots
where the asymptotic expansion (
4.35
) is valid. Inserting this expansion into this integral, and interchanging the integral and the sum, the
r^{\rm th}
term in the sum is (this is to be understood as evaluated for
\Re s>1
and then analytically continued)

\frac{F_{r}(x,x)}{4\pi}\frac{1}{\Gamma(s)}\int_{0}^{\mu^{-2}}{\rm d}t\,t^{s+r-2}=\frac{F_{r}(x,x)}{4\pi}\frac{1}{\Gamma(s)}\ \frac{(\mu^{-2})^{s+r-1}}{s+r-1}\ ,\quad r=0,1,2,\ldots

(4.36)

Let us insist that, since the integral is evaluated for
\Re s>1
, the contribution from the lower boundary is
0^{s+r-1}=0
.
The expression on the right-hand side of (
4.36
) can then be analytically continued : it is regular, except possibly as
s\to 1-r
. Indeed, Euler’s
\Gamma
-function has no zeros so that
\frac{1}{\Gamma(s)}
is regular. On the other hand,
\Gamma(s)
has poles whenever
s\to 0,-1,-2,\ldots
which corresponds to
r=1,2,\ldots
, but not
r=0
. Hence, we must distinguish the cases
r=0
and
r=1,2,\ldots
.
For
r=0
