, i.e.
s\to 1
, we have
\Gamma(s)\sim 1-\gamma(s-1)
, where
\gamma\simeq 0.57
is Euler’s constant, and then

\frac{F_{0}(x,x)}{4\pi}\frac{1}{\Gamma(s)}\int_{0}^{\mu^{-2}}{\rm d}t\,t^{s-2}\sim_{s\to 1}\frac{F_{0}(x,x)}{4\pi}\Big(\frac{1}{s-1}+\gamma+\log\mu^{-2}+{\cal O}(s-1)\Big)\ .

(4.37)

For
r=1,2,\ldots
, we have
\frac{1}{\Gamma(s)}\sim_{s\to 1-r}(-)^{r-1}(r-1)!\,(s-1+r)
so that the “would-be pole” of the integral is cancelled by this zero and one gets

\frac{F_{r}(x,x)}{4\pi}\frac{1}{\Gamma(s)}\int_{0}^{\mu^{-2}}{\rm d}t\,t^{s+r-2}\sim_{s\to 1-r}\frac{F_{r}(x,x)}{4\pi}\Big((-)^{r-1}(r-1)!\,+{\cal O}(s+r-1)\Big)\ ,\quad r=1,2,\ldots

(4.38)

We conclude that

\begin{array}[]{|c|}\hline\cr\\
\displaystyle\zeta_{+}(s,x,x)\sim_{s\to 1}\Big(\frac{1}{4\pi(s-1)}+\text{regular}\Big)\ {\bf 1}_{2\times 2}\ ,\quad\zeta_{+}(k,x,x)=\frac{(-)^{k}}{4\pi}k!\,F_{1-k}(x,x)\ {\bf 1}_{2\times 2}\ ,\ k=0,-1,\ldots\\
\\
\hline\cr\end{array}

(4.39)

In particular for
k=0
, we read from (
4.34
) that
F_{1}(x,x)=-\big(\frac{{\cal R}(x)}{12}+m^{2}\big)\,{\bf 1}_{2\times 2}
which yields

\zeta_{+}(0,x,x)=-\frac{1}{4\pi}\big(\frac{{\cal R}(x)}{12}+m^{2}\big)\,{\bf 1}_{2\times 2}\ .

(4.40)

What about
\zeta_{-}(s,x,x)
? We know from (
4.33
) that

\zeta_{-}(s,x,x)=im\gamma_{*}\ \zeta_{+}(s+\frac{1}{2},x,x)
.
This means that
\zeta_{-}(s,x,x)
must be analytic in
s
except for a simple pole at
s=\frac{1}{2}
. More precisely, (
4.39
) translates into

\zeta_{-}(s,x,x)\sim_{s\to 1/2}\Big(\frac{im}{4\pi(s-\frac{1}{2})}\,+\text{regular}\Big)\gamma_{*}\ ,\ \zeta_{-}(k-\frac{1}{2},x,x)=(-)^{k}\frac{im}{4\pi}F_{1-k}(x,x)\,\gamma_{*}\ ,\ k=0,-1,\ldots

(4.41)

We can now use this information, together with the inverse Mellin transform (
4.28
) to obtain the small-
t
expansion of
K_{-}(t,x,x)
as follows. Choosing e.g.
c>1
and assuming that
22
22

22

This should follow from the fact that
\Gamma(s)
vanishes exponentially for
s=c\pm i\sigma
and
\sigma\to\infty
, and, more generally on the dotted part of the integration contour. To estimate the
\Gamma
-function for large arguments we use Stirling’s formula

\Gamma(z+1)\sim_{|z|\to\infty}z^{z}e^{-z}=e^{z(\log z-1)}\ ,

(4.42)

which remains valid even if the argument is complex as long as
\arg z\neq\pm\pi
, since one must avoid the poles on the negative real axis. If we let
z=a\pm i\sigma
with
\sigma\to\infty
it is straightforward to see that

\Gamma(a+1\pm i\sigma)\sim_{\sigma\to\infty}e^{\pm i\big(\frac{\pi a}{2}+\sigma\log\sigma-\sigma\big)}e^{-\frac{\pi}{2}\sigma+a\log\sigma+{\cal O}(\frac{1}{\sigma})}\quad\Rightarrow\quad\big|\Gamma(a+1\pm i\sigma)\big|\sim_{\sigma\to\infty}\sigma^{a}e^{-\frac{\pi}{2}\sigma}\ .

(4.43)

One can show a similar result if also
a\to-\infty
, so that the
\Gamma
-function vanishes exponentially on the dotted part of the contour in the above figure as this contour is taken to infinity. Note that the left vertical part of the contour must, of course, avoid the poles at the negative integers, but it remains true, even on the negative real axis between any two negative integers
-k-1
and
-k
, that
|\Gamma|
vanishes exponentially as
k\to\infty
.

\lim_{\sigma\to\infty}|\Gamma(c^{\prime}\pm i\sigma)\zeta_{-}(c^{\prime}\pm i\sigma,x,x)|=0\ ,\forall c^{\prime}\leq c\ ,

(4.44)

we can close the integration contour by an infinite rectangle to the left, cf the figure, so that the integral is given by the sum of the residues of all the poles enclosed.

There is the pole at
s=\frac{1}{2}
from
\zeta_{-}(s,x,x)
and there are the poles from
\Gamma(s)
at
s=-n,\ n=0,1,2,\ldots
with residues
\frac{(-)^{n}}{n!}
. We get

\displaystyle K_{-}(t,x,x)

\displaystyle\sim_{t\to 0}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{\rm inf.rect.}{\rm d}s\,t^{-s}\,\Gamma(s)\zeta_{-}(s,x,x)=t^{-1/2}\Gamma(\frac{1}{2})\frac{im}{4\pi}\,\gamma_{*}\ +\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}t^{n}\frac{(-)^{n}}{n!}\zeta_{-}(-n,x,x)

(4.45)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle im\,\gamma_{*}\Big(\frac{1}{4\sqrt{\pi\,t}}\,\gamma_{*}+\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}t^{n}\frac{(-)^{n}}{n!}\zeta_{+}(-n+\frac{1}{2},x,x)\Big)\ .

Just as for
\zeta_{-}(s,x,x)
, the explicit factor of
m
on the right-hand side does not mean that
K_{-}(t,x,x)
vanishes for
m=0
since, in general, the
\zeta_{+}(-n+\frac{1}{2},x,x)
are expected to contain contributions
\sim m^{2n-1}
. However, we see that for
m=0
the only term involving a half-integer power of
t
, namely
\frac{1}{\sqrt{t}}
is absent (as are probably also the terms with
n\geq 1
).

Below, we will estimate the small-
t
behaviour of
K_{-}
on the flat torus where the eigenvalues and eigenfunctions are explicitly known, and we will find that

K_{-}(t,x,x)\sim_{t\to 0}\Big(\frac{m}{4\sqrt{\pi t}}+\frac{1}{4\pi^{2}}\Big)i\gamma_{*}+\ldots\ ,

(4.46)

in agreement with the general formula (
4.45
) !

4.1.5
Green’s functions

The Green’s function
S(x,y)
of the Dirac operator
D
is a
2\times 2
-matrix solution of

D_{x}S(x,y)=\frac{\delta^{(2)}(x-y)}{\sqrt{g}}\ {\bf 1}_{2\times 2}\ ,

(4.47)

while we denote
G
the (also
2\times 2
-matrix) Green’s function of
D^{2}
:

D^{2}_{x}G(x,y)=\frac{\delta^{(2)}(x-y)}{\sqrt{g}}\ {\bf 1}_{2\times 2}\ .

(4.48)

In terms of the eigenfunctions and eigenvalues we have

\displaystyle S(x,y)

\displaystyle=-i

\displaystyle\sum_{n}\frac{1}{\lambda_{n}}\big(\chi_{n}(x)\phi_{n}^{\dagger}(y)-\phi_{n}(x)\chi_{n}^{\dagger}(y)\big)=\sum_{n}\frac{1}{\lambda_{n}}\big(\psi_{n}(x)\psi_{n}^{\dagger}(x)-\psi_{n}^{*}(x){\psi_{n}^{*}}^{\dagger}(y)\big)\ ,

\displaystyle G(x,y)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{n}\frac{1}{\lambda_{n}^{2}}\big(\chi_{n}(x)\chi_{n}^{\dagger}(y)+\phi_{n}(x)\phi_{n}^{\dagger}(y)\big)=\sum_{n}\frac{1}{\lambda_{n}^{2}}\big(\psi_{n}(x)\psi_{n}^{\dagger}(x)+\psi_{n}^{*}(x){\psi_{n}^{*}}^{\dagger}(y)\big)\ .

(4.49)

They are indeed solutions of (
4.47
), resp. (
4.48
) as one sees by applying
D
, resp.
D^{2}
, and using the completeness relation (
4.2
).
It trivially follows from either (
4.47
) and (
4.48
), or from (
4.1.5
), that

S(x,y)=D_{x}G(x,y)\ .

(4.50)

Comparing with (
4.1.1
), one sees that

S(x,y)=-i\,\zeta_{-}(\frac{1}{2},x,y)\quad,\quad G(x,y)=\zeta_{+}(1,x,y)\ .

(4.51)

Note that this is consistent with (
4.1.1
) and (
4.50
).
Recall that
\zeta_{-}(s,x,x)
has a pole at
s=\frac{1}{2}
and
\zeta_{+}(s,x,x)
at
s=1
, consistent with the fact that the Green’s functions are singular as
x\to y
.

It is also obvious from the definitions that
G(x,y)
is real and
S(x,y)
purely imaginary. Furthermore,

(G(x,y))^{\dagger}=G(y,x)\quad,\quad(S(x,y))^{\dagger}=S(y,x)\ .

(4.52)

It follows from the orthonormality of the
\chi_{n}
and
\phi_{n}
that

\int{\rm d}^{2}z\sqrt{g(z)}\,S(x,z)S(z,y)=G(x,y)\ .

(4.53)

Note that this is also consistent with the differential equations obeyed by
S
and
G
. Indeed, we have

\displaystyle D_{x}^{2}G(x,y)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle D_{x}\int{\rm d}^{2}z\sqrt{g(z)}\ (D_{x}S(x,z))S(z,y)=D_{x}\int{\rm d}^{2}z\sqrt{g(z)}\ \frac{\delta^{(2)}(x-z)}{\sqrt{g(z)}}S(z,y)

(4.54)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle D_{x}\,S(x,y)=\frac{\delta^{(2)}(x-y)}{\sqrt{g}}{\bf 1}_{2\times 2}\ .

What can be said about the matrix structure of these Green’s functions ?
As before, the matrix structure of
D^{2}
is
{\bf 1}(\ldots)+\gamma_{*}(\ldots)
(cf (
2.43
) and (
2.48
)), implying that
G
must have the same structure:

G(x,y)=G_{0}(x,y)\,{\bf 1}+G_{*}(x,y)\,\gamma_{*}\ ,

(4.55)

which also follows from (
4.51
) and (
4.30
).
Combining this with the reality of
G
and the property (
4.52
) we see that

G_{0}(x,y)
is real and symmetric and
G_{*}(x,y)
is imaginary and antisymmetric.
The matrix structure of
S
is somewhat less trivial, but follows from
S(x,y)=(i{\nabla\hskip-8.53581pt/}_{x}+m\gamma_{*})G(x,y)
along the same lines as discussed above for the zeta-functions.
We find

\,{\rm tr}\,S(x,y)=m\,{\rm tr}\,\gamma_{*}G(x,y)\quad,\quad\,{\rm tr}\,\gamma_{*}S(x,y)=m\,{\rm tr}\,G(x,y)\ ,

(4.56)

which translates the corresponding relation (
4.32
) between the traces of
\zeta_{+}
and
\zeta_{-}
.
For vanishing mass, one must exclude the zero-mode contributions from
S
and from
G
(we then call them
\widetilde{S}
and
\widetilde{G}
), but one still has the corresponding relation
\widetilde{S}=i{\nabla\hskip-8.53581pt/}\,\widetilde{G}
and it then follows that, for vanishing mass,

\,{\rm tr}\,\widetilde{S}(x,y)=\,{\rm tr}\,\gamma_{*}\widetilde{S}(x,y)=0\quad\text{for $m=0$}\ .

(4.57)

4.2
Variation of
\zeta(s)

Now we have the tools and definitions to express the variation of the zeta-function
\zeta(s)
that appeared in the variation of the effective gravitational action. In the previous section we did the perturbation theory of the eigenvalues and had obtained the formula (
3.34
). It can now be nicely rewritten in terms of the local zeta functions
\zeta_{\pm}(s,x,x)
as (recall that
\int f(x)
is meant to be
\int{\rm d}^{2}x\,\sqrt{g(x)}\,f(x)
)

\delta\zeta(s)=2s\int\delta\sigma\Big(\,{\rm tr}\,\zeta_{+}(s,x,x)+im\,{\rm tr}\,\gamma_{*}\zeta_{-}(s+\frac{1}{2},x,x)\Big)\ .

(4.58)

We may use (
4.1.1
) to trade
\zeta_{-}(s+\frac{1}{2},x,x)
for
im\gamma_{*}\zeta_{+}(s+1,x,x)
so that

\delta\zeta(s)=2s\int\delta\sigma\Big(\,{\rm tr}\,\zeta_{+}(s,x,x)-m^{2}\,{\rm tr}\,\zeta_{+}(s+1,x,x)\Big)\ .

(4.59)

As we have discussed above, if zero-modes of
i{\nabla\hskip-8.53581pt/}
are present, we have
\lambda_{0}=m
and
\delta\lambda_{0}=0
so that there is no contribution from these zero-modes to the above formula. We can then equivalently rewrite it in terms of the
\widetilde{\zeta}_{+}(s,x,x)
which are the local zeta functions with the zero-modes of
i{\nabla\hskip-8.53581pt/}
excluded from the sum :

\delta\zeta(s)=2s\int\delta\sigma\Big(\,{\rm tr}\,\widetilde{\zeta}_{+}(s,x,x)-m^{2}\,{\rm tr}\,\widetilde{\zeta}_{+}(s+1,x,x)\Big)\ .

(4.60)

Note that the zero-modes alone cannot contribute to the singularities and thus
\widetilde{\zeta}_{+}(s,x,x)
has the same pole and residue at
s=1
as
\zeta_{+}(s,x,x)
, only the regular parts differ by the zero-mode contribution.
For the derivative of (
4.59
) we obviously get

\displaystyle\delta\zeta^{\prime}(s)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2\int\delta\sigma\Big(\,{\rm tr}\,\zeta_{+}(s,x,x)-m^{2}\,{\rm tr}\,\zeta_{+}(s+1,x,x)\Big)

(4.61)

\displaystyle+2s\int\delta\sigma\Big(\,{\rm tr}\,\zeta^{\prime}_{+}(s,x,x)-m^{2}\,{\rm tr}\,\zeta^{\prime}_{+}(s+1,x,x)\Big)\ .

We want to evaluate both
\delta\zeta
and
\delta\zeta^{\prime}
at
s=0
. Now
\zeta_{+}(0,x,x)
is regular (cf e.g. (
4.40
), and
23
23

23

If a meromorphic function is regular at a given point, then its derivative necessarily is also regular at this point.
so is
\zeta^{\prime}_{+}(0,x,x)
.
It follows that

\displaystyle\hskip-28.45274pt\delta\zeta(0)=-2m^{2}\int\delta\sigma\,\lim_{s\to 0}s\,\,{\rm tr}\,\zeta_{+}(s+1,x,x)\ ,

\displaystyle\hskip-28.45274pt\delta\zeta^{\prime}(0)=2\int\delta\sigma\,{\rm tr}\,\zeta_{+}(0,x,x)-2m^{2}\int\delta\sigma\,\lim_{s\to 0}\,{\rm tr}\,\Big(\zeta_{+}(s+1,x,x)+s\,\zeta^{\prime}_{+}(s+1,x,x)\Big)\ .

(4.62)

Now
\zeta_{+}(\widetilde{s},x,x)
has a pole at

\widetilde{s}=1
:
\zeta_{+}(\widetilde{s},x,x)\sim\big(\frac{1}{4\pi}\frac{1}{\widetilde{s}-1}+C_{+}\big){\bf 1}_{2\times 2}+\zeta_{+}^{\rm reg}(\widetilde{s},x,x)
, where the (finite) value of the constant
C_{+}
depends on the exact definition of
\zeta_{+}^{\rm reg}
, cf eqs (
4.92
) and (
4.93
) below. It follows that
\zeta_{+}(s+1,x,x)
has a pole at
s=0
and then

\lim_{s\to 0}s\,\zeta_{+}(s+1,x,x)=\lim_{s\to 0}s\,\widetilde{\zeta}_{+}(s+1,x,x)=\frac{1}{4\pi}\,{\bf 1}_{2\times 2}\ .

(4.63)

Note that we get the same expression whether we use
\zeta_{+}
or
\widetilde{\zeta}_{+}
. In any case

\delta\zeta(0)=-2m^{2}\int\delta\sigma\,{\rm tr}\,\frac{1}{4\pi}\,{\bf 1}_{2\times 2}=-\frac{m^{2}}{\pi}\int\delta\sigma=-\frac{m^{2}}{2\pi}\delta A\ ,

(4.64)

where we used that

\int\delta\sigma\equiv\int{\rm d}^{2}x\,\sqrt{g}\,\delta\sigma=\int{\rm d}^{2}x\sqrt{\widehat{g}}\,e^{2\sigma}\,\delta\sigma=\frac{1}{2}\,\delta\int{\rm d}^{2}x\,\sqrt{\widehat{g}}\,e^{2\sigma}=\frac{1}{2}\,\delta A\ .

(4.65)

Similarly, for the terms appearing in
\delta\zeta^{\prime}(0)
we have as
s\to 0

\displaystyle\hskip-56.9055pt\lim_{s\to 0}\big(\zeta_{+}(s+1,x,x)+s\,\zeta^{\prime}_{+}(s+1,x,x)\big)

\displaystyle=\lim_{s\to 0}\Big[\big(\frac{1}{4\pi\,s}+C_{+}\big){\bf 1}_{2\times 2}+\zeta_{+}^{\rm reg}(s+1,x,x)+s\frac{(-1)}{4\pi\,s^{2}}{\bf 1}_{2\times 2}+s(\zeta_{+}^{\rm reg})^{\prime}(s+1,x,x)\Big]

\displaystyle=\zeta_{+}^{\rm reg}(1,x,x)+C_{+}\,{\bf 1}_{2\times 2}\ .

(4.66)

(We could have argued similarly about
\zeta_{-}(s+\frac{1}{2},x,x)+s\,\zeta^{\prime}_{-}(s+\frac{1}{2},x,x)
as
s\to 0
.)
Thus

\displaystyle\delta\zeta^{\prime}(0)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2\int\delta\sigma\,{\rm tr}\,\zeta_{+}(0,x,x)-2m^{2}\int\delta\sigma\,{\rm tr}\,\zeta_{+}^{\rm reg}(1,x,x)-2m^{2}C_{+}\delta A\ ,

(4.67)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2\int\delta\sigma\,{\rm tr}\,\widetilde{\zeta}_{+}(0,x,x)-2m^{2}\int\delta\sigma\,{\rm tr}\,\widetilde{\zeta}_{+}^{\rm reg}(1,x,x)-2m^{2}\widetilde{C}_{+}\delta A\ ,

In the second line we have written
\widetilde{C}_{+}
, but our definitions of
\zeta_{+}^{\rm reg}
and
\widetilde{\zeta}_{+}^{\rm reg}
below will be such that
C_{+}=\widetilde{C}_{+}=\frac{\gamma-\log\mu^{2}}{4\pi}
.
Thus we can express
\delta\zeta^{\prime}(0)
either in terms of
\zeta_{+}(0,x,x)
and
\zeta_{+}^{\rm reg}(1,x,x)
or in terms of
\widetilde{\zeta}_{+}(0,x,x)
and
\widetilde{\zeta}_{+}^{\rm reg}(1,x,x)
.

4.3
Small-
t
asymptotics of the heat kernels and singularity structure of the local zeta-functions for the flat torus

We have seen that the singularities (poles) of the local zeta-functions are determined by the small-
t
asymptotics of the heat kernels.
We have also seen that we could relate
\zeta_{+}
and
\zeta_{-}
and, in particular, at coinciding points we could deduce from this relation the small-
t
expansion of
K_{-}(t,x,x)
, see (
4.45
), from the one of
K_{+}(t,x,x)
, which is established in appendix A. However, we find it useful (and pedagogical) to try to explicitly compute these quantities for the example of the flat torus and see that we have indeed a perfect agreement with the results from the general statements. In this subsection we will thus establish the results for the flat “square” torus with further detailed computations for
K_{-}
presented in appendix B.

From the explicit form of the normalised eigenfunctions
\psi_{\vec{n}}
of
D
on the flat square torus (with periods
2\pi
in both directions) given in (
2.61
) we find

\displaystyle{\cal K}(t,x,y)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{n_{1},n_{2}}e^{-\lambda^{2}_{\vec{n}}t}\psi_{\vec{n}}(x)\psi_{\vec{n}}^{\dagger}(y)

(4.68)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{8\pi^{2}}\sum_{n_{1},n_{2}}e^{-(n_{1}^{2}+n_{2}^{2}+m^{2})t}e^{in_{1}z^{1}+in_{2}z^{2}}\frac{1}{\lambda_{\vec{n}}}\begin{pmatrix}\lambda_{\vec{n}}-n_{2}&-n_{1}-im\\
-n_{1}+im&\lambda_{\vec{n}}+n_{2}\end{pmatrix}\ ,

where here, and throughout this subsection

z^{1}=x^{1}-y^{1},\ z^{2}=x^{2}-y^{2}
.
Note that the terms odd under
n_{1}\to-n_{1}
or
n_{2}\to-n_{2}
drop out of the sum, so that (2 times) the real part is

K_{+}(t,x,y)={\cal K}(t,x,y)+{\cal K}^{*}(t,x,y)=\frac{1}{4\pi^{2}}\sum_{n_{1},n_{2}}e^{-(n_{1}^{2}+n_{2}^{2}+m^{2})t}e^{in_{1}z^{1}+in_{2}z^{2}}\ {\bf 1}_{2\times 2}\ ,

(4.69)

while (-2 times) the imaginary part of
{\cal K}
is

\displaystyle K_{-}(t,x,y)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle i({\cal K}(t,x,y)-{\cal K}^{*}(t,x,y))

(4.70)

\displaystyle\hskip-71.13188pt=-\frac{1}{4\pi^{2}}\sum_{n_{1},n_{2}}e^{-(n_{1}^{2}+n_{2}^{2}+m^{2})t}\Big(\cos\big(n_{1}z^{1}+n_{2}z^{2}\big)\frac{1}{\lambda_{\vec{n}}}\begin{pmatrix}0&-m\\
m&0\end{pmatrix}+\sin\big(n_{1}z^{1}+n_{2}z^{2}\big)\frac{1}{\lambda_{\vec{n}}}\begin{pmatrix}-n_{2}&-n_{1}\\
-n_{1}&n_{2}\end{pmatrix}\Big)

\displaystyle\hskip-71.13188pt=\frac{i}{4\pi^{2}}\sum_{n_{1},n_{2}}e^{-(n_{1}^{2}+n_{2}^{2}+m^{2})t}e^{in_{1}z^{1}+in_{2}z^{2}}\frac{1}{\lambda_{\vec{n}}}\,\big(-n_{1}\sigma_{x}-n_{2}\sigma_{z}+m\gamma_{*}\big)\ .

Note that for
t=0
one correctly finds that
K_{+}(0,x,y)=\delta^{(2)}(x-y){\bf 1}_{2\times 2}
, while nothing like this is true for
K_{-}
.
Also note that the a priori possible
\gamma_{*}
-terms are absent in (
4.69
) and, correspondingly, there are no terms
\sim{\bf 1}_{2\times 2}
in (
4.70
).

Obviously, at coinciding points
x=y
, i.e.
z=0
, these heat kernels simplify:

\displaystyle K_{+}(t,x,x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{4\pi^{2}}\sum_{n_{1},n_{2}}e^{-(n_{1}^{2}+n_{2}^{2}+m^{2})t}\ {\bf 1}_{2\times 2}\ ,

\displaystyle K_{-}(t,x,x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{i\,m}{4\pi^{2}}\sum_{n_{1},n_{2}}\frac{1}{\lambda_{\vec{n}}}\,e^{-(n_{1}^{2}+n_{2}^{2}+m^{2})t}\ \gamma_{*}\ \ .

(4.71)

We see that
K_{+}(t,x,x)\sim\ {\bf 1}_{2\times 2}
and
K_{-}(t,x,x)\sim\gamma_{*}
, in agreement with the general statements above, cf. (
4.24
).
Note that for
K_{+}(t,x,y)
the double sum factorises into a sum over
n_{1}
and another one over
n_{2}
, while for
K_{-}(t,x,y)
or
K_{-}(t,x,x)
there is no such factorisation since
\lambda_{\vec{n}}=\sqrt{n_{1}^{2}+n_{2}^{2}+m^{2}}
.
In fact,
K_{+}(t,x,y)
can be expressed in terms of the theta function
\theta_{3}
, (cf
[
24
]
) as

K_{+}(t,x,y)=\frac{1}{4\pi^{2}}\,e^{-m^{2}t}\ \theta_{3}\big(\frac{z^{1}}{2\pi}\big|i\frac{t}{\pi}\big)\ \theta_{3}\big(\frac{z^{2}}{2\pi}\big|i\frac{t}{\pi}\big)\ {\bf 1}_{2\times 2}\quad,\quad\theta_{3}(\nu|\tau)=\sum_{n=-\infty}^{\infty}e^{i\pi\tau n^{2}+2\pi i\nu n}\quad,\quad\mathop{\rm Im}\tau>0\ .

(4.72)

On the other hand,
K_{-}(t,x,y)
can be expressed in terms of the auxiliary sum
L
already defined in (
4.19
), or equivalently another related sum
{\cal L}
, as follows :

K_{-}(t,x,y)=D_{x}L(t,x,y)\equiv(i\sigma_{x}\partial_{1}+i\sigma_{z}\partial_{2}+m\gamma_{*})L(t,x,y)\ ,

(4.73)

where

L(t,x,y)=\frac{i}{4\pi^{2}}\,{\cal L}(t,z){\bf 1}_{2\times 2}\quad,\quad{\cal L}(t,z)=\sum_{n_{1},n_{2}}\frac{e^{-\lambda_{\vec{n}}^{2}\,t}}{\lambda_{\vec{n}}}\ e^{i\vec{n}\cdot\vec{z}}\equiv\sum_{n_{1},n_{2}}\frac{e^{-(n_{1}^{2}+n_{2}^{2}+m^{2})t}e^{in_{1}z^{1}+in_{2}z^{2}}}{\sqrt{n_{1}^{2}+n_{2}^{2}+m^{2}}}\ .

(4.74)

Note that
K_{-}
at coinciding points is directly given in terms of this
{\cal L}
as (cf (
4.3
))

K_{-}(t,x,x)=\frac{i}{4\pi^{2}}\,m\gamma_{*}\ {\cal L}(t,0)\ .

(4.75)

For the present case of the torus, we find even for
x\neq y
that
L(t,x,y)\sim{\bf 1}_{2\times 2}
, a fact that is not necessarily true in general.

The explicit expression of
K_{+}
in terms of the theta function
\theta_{3}
makes it easy to study its properties. The periodicity property
\theta_{3}(\nu+1|\tau)=\theta_{3}(\nu|\tau)
reflects the periodicity of the eigenfunctions on the torus.

\theta_{3}
has a well-known modular transformation property
[
24
]
which can be written as

\theta_{3}(\frac{z}{2\pi}|i\frac{t}{\pi})=\sqrt{\frac{\pi}{t}}e^{-z^{2}/(4t)}\ \theta_{3}(-i\frac{z}{2t},i\frac{\pi}{t})\ ,

(4.76)

giving immediately the small-
t
asymptotics as follows :

\theta_{3}(\frac{z}{2\pi}|i\frac{t}{\pi})=\sqrt{\frac{\pi}{t}}\,e^{-z^{2}/(4t)}\ \sum_{n}e^{-\pi^{2}n^{2}/t+\pi zn/t}=\sqrt{\frac{\pi}{t}}\,\sum_{n}e^{-\frac{1}{4t}(z-2\pi n)^{2}}\ .

(4.77)

This clearly exhibits again the
2\pi
-periodicity in
z
. Let us then assume that
z\in(-\pi,\pi]
and consider
t\to 0
. For
z=\pi
, both
n=0
and
n=1
contribute equally to the sum so that one gets
\sqrt{\frac{\pi}{t}}\,2\,e^{-\pi^{2}/(4t)}(1+{\cal O}(e^{-2\pi^{2}/t}))
. For all other values of
z\in(-\pi,\pi)
there is a single dominant term that contributes
\sqrt{\frac{\pi}{t}}\,e^{-z^{2}/(4t)}
and all other terms are exponentially smaller by some factor
e^{-a/t}
for some
a>0
.
Hence the small-
t
asymptotics is

\theta_{3}(\frac{z}{2\pi}|i\frac{t}{\pi})\sim_{t\to 0}\sqrt{\frac{\pi}{t}}\,e^{-z^{2}/(4t)}\,\Big(1+{\cal O}\big(e^{-a/t}\big)\Big)\quad,\quad a>0\ ,\quad\text{for}|z|<\pi\ .

(4.78)

One
immediately deduces the small-
t
behaviour of
K_{+}(t,x,y)
(for
z^{i}\neq\pm\pi
) as

K_{+}(t,x,y)=\frac{1}{4\pi t}\ \exp\Big(-\frac{(z^{1})^{2}+(z^{2})^{2}}{4t}\Big)\ e^{-m^{2}t}\ {\bf 1}_{2\times 2}\ \Big(1+{\cal O}\big(e^{-a/t}\big)\Big)\ .

(4.79)

The leading piece coincides, of course, with the well-known answer on
{\bf R}^{2}
.
For
x=y
one simply has

K_{+}(t,x,x)=\frac{1}{4\pi t}\,e^{-m^{2}t}\ {\bf 1}_{2\times 2}\ \Big(1+{\cal O}\big(e^{-a/t}\big)\Big)\ .

(4.80)

On the other hand, the small
t
asymptotics of
K_{-}(t,x,y)
, or equivalently the small-
t
asymptotics of
L(t,x,y)
or
{\cal L}(t,z)
is much more difficult to obtain directly. We have devoted some effort to try to estimate their asymptotic behaviour. These results are presented in appendix B.
At coinciding points we have been able to estimate rather easily (cf (
B.4
)) that

{\cal L}(t,0)\simeq\sqrt{\frac{\pi^{3}}{t}}+\frac{1}{m}+{\cal O}(t^{0},m^{0})\ .

(4.81)

This implies in turn that
K_{-}(t,x,x)
is given by (cf (
4.75
))

K_{-}(t,x,x)\sim_{t\to 0}\Big(\frac{m}{4\sqrt{\pi t}}+\frac{1}{4\pi^{2}}\Big)\ i\gamma_{*}\ +\ldots\ .

(4.82)

where the unwritten terms
+\ldots
vanish as
t\to 0
, a result already cited above.
To estimate
{\cal L}(t,z)
for
z\neq 0
turned out to be much more difficult. The reader can find more details in the appendix B. But we will not need them here to proceed further.

As discussed above, the small-
t
behaviours of
K_{\pm}(t,x,x)
translate into possible poles of the corresponding local zeta-functions
\zeta_{\pm}(s,x,x)
. Using the above torus results for coinciding points one immeadiately finds that

\displaystyle\zeta_{+}(s,x,x)\sim_{s\to 1}\ \frac{{\bf 1}}{4\pi(s-1)}+{\rm finite}\ \ ,\quad\zeta_{+}(0,x,x)={\rm finite}\times{\bf 1}\ ,

\displaystyle\zeta_{-}(s,x,x)\sim_{s\to 1/2}\ \frac{mi\gamma_{*}}{4\pi(s-\frac{1}{2})}+{\rm finite}\ ,\quad\zeta_{-}(0,x,x)={\rm finite}\times i\gamma_{*}\ ,

(4.83)

again in agreement with our general results.

4.4
General statements about the singularity structure for
x\neq y

In subsection
4.1
we already discussed many general properties of the local heat kernels and local zeta functions, in particular at coinciding point. Now, we will make some further general statements for
x\neq y
. These statements will be based on the small-
t
behaviour of
K_{+}(t,x,y)
as follows from the differential (heat) equation, and worked out in appendix
A
. For
K_{-}(t,x,y)
we can deduce the corresponding
statements by using the fundamental relation (
4.1.1
) between the corresponding
\zeta_{+}(s,x,y)
and
\zeta_{-}(s,x,y)
.

As already discussed above, since
D^{2}
only contains the matrix structures
{\bf 1}_{2\times 2}
and
\gamma_{*}
, the same is true for
G(x,y)
and
K_{+}(t,x,y)
. However, the leading small-
t
singularity of
K_{+}(t,x,y)
is only
\sim{\bf 1}_{2\times 2}
and is universal, cf (
A.21
) of the appendix
24
24

24

Actually, in
n=2
dimensions, equation (
A.21
) gives

K(t,x,y)=\frac{1}{4\pi t}\exp\left(-{\ell^{2}(x,y)\over 4t}\right)\big(F_{0}(x,y)+{\cal O}(t)\big)
. Now,
F_{0}(x,y)=1+{\cal O}(\ell^{2})
and the exponential forces
\ell^{2}
to be of order
t
so that
{\cal O}(\ell^{2})\simeq{\cal O}(t)
and we indeed have
K(t,x,y)=\frac{1}{4\pi t}\exp\left(-{\ell^{2}(x,y)\over 4t}\right)\big(1+{\cal O}(t)\big)
.

:

K_{+}(t,x,y)\sim_{t\to 0}\frac{1}{4\pi t}\,e^{-\ell^{2}(x,y)/(4t)}\,{\bf 1}_{2\times 2}\ ,

(4.84)

where,
\ell(x,y)
is the geodesic distance between
x
and
y
. This generalizes the corresponding formula (
4.79
) for the torus. We can then deduce the singularity structure of
\zeta_{+}(s,x,y)
solely form this universal leading behaviour of
K_{+}
.
This universality is due to the (leading) 2-derivative part of
D^{2}
being always
-{\bf 1}_{2\times 2}\,g^{\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}\partial_{\nu}
which is the same as for the scalar Laplacian
-{\bf 1}_{2\times 2}\Delta_{\rm scalar}
. Indeed, as discussed above, we may obtain
K_{+}(t,x,y)
solely from the differential equation (
4.16
).

For
K_{-}(t,x,y)=D_{x}L(t,x,y)
we expect that the
leading
small-
t
singularity of
L
is again generic and hence given by the obvious generalization of the torus result, cf (
B.14
).
However, instead of relying on this “expected” formula, we will instead use the proven relation (
4.1.1
) between
\zeta_{+}
and
\zeta_{-}
, together with (
4.84
), to deduce the singularity structure of
\zeta_{-}(s,x,y)
.

Let us then establish the singularity structures of both,
\zeta_{\pm}(s,x,y)
. To begin with, (
4.84
) defines what we call the singular part of the heat-kernel :

K^{\rm sing}_{+}(t,x,y)=\frac{1}{4\pi t}\,e^{-\ell^{2}(x,y)/(4t)}\,{\bf 1}_{2\times 2}\ .

(4.85)

From this we define the singular part of the zeta-function as

\zeta^{\rm sing}_{+}(s,x,y)=\frac{1}{\Gamma(s)}\int_{0}^{1/\mu^{2}}{\rm d}t\,t^{s-1}K^{\rm sing}_{\pm}(t,x,y)\ .

(4.86)

Indeed, any singularity of the zeta-function must come from the small-
t
part of the integral, and as we have seen before, only the
\frac{1}{t}
-part of the heat kernel
K_{+}
can lead to a pole of the
\zeta_{+}(s,\cdot,\cdot)
, while the “would-be” poles due to the sub-leading terms in
t
are cancelled by the zeros of
\frac{1}{\Gamma(s)}
.
For general
x,y
we explicitly have (setting
\frac{1}{t}=\mu^{2}u
)

\zeta^{\rm sing}_{+}(s,x,y)=\frac{1}{\Gamma(s)}\int_{0}^{1/\mu^{2}}{\rm d}t\,t^{s-1}\frac{e^{-\ell^{2}(x,y)/(4t)}}{4\pi t}\,{\bf 1}_{2\times 2}=\frac{(\mu^{2})^{1-s}}{4\pi\Gamma(s)}\,E_{s}\big(\frac{\mu^{2}\ell^{2}(x,y)}{4}\big)\,{\bf 1}_{2\times 2}\ ,

(4.87)

where
E_{s}
is the exponential integral function defined as

E_{s}(z)=\int_{1}^{\infty}{\rm d}u\,u^{-s}e^{-zu}\ .

(4.88)

Its asymptotic expansions are well known and, in particular, if we first set
s=1
and then let
x\to y
, i.e.
\ell(x,y)\to 0
, we have

E_{1}\Big(\frac{\mu^{2}\ell^{2}}{4}\Big)\sim_{\ell^{2}\to 0}\ \ -\gamma-\log\frac{\mu^{2}\ell^{2}}{4}+{\cal O}\big(\mu^{2}\ell^{2}\big)\ ,

(4.89)

(where
\gamma\simeq 0.57
is Euler’s constant, not to be confused with the matrix
\gamma_{*}
)
so that

\zeta^{\rm sing}_{+}(1,x,y)\sim_{\ell\to 0}\ \frac{1}{4\pi}\Big(-\log\frac{\mu^{2}\ell^{2}(x,y)}{4}-\gamma+{\cal O}\big(\mu^{2}\ell^{2}\big)\Big)\,{\bf 1}_{2\times 2}\ ,

(4.90)

Of course,
\zeta_{+}(1,x,y)
is the Green’s function
G(x,y)
and the term
-\frac{1}{4\pi}\log\frac{\mu^{2}\ell^{2}}{4}\,{\bf 1}_{2\times 2}
is just the (leading) short-distance singularity of
G(x,y)
which is identical to the well-known short-distance singulartity of the Green’s function of the scalar Laplace operator in two dimensions.
On the other hand, if we first set
x=y
so that
\ell(x,y)=0
we have, assuming
\Re s>1
,
E_{s}(0)=\int_{0}^{1}{\rm d}v\,v^{s-2}=\frac{1}{s-1}
:

E_{s}(0)=\frac{1}{s-1}\ .

(4.91)

Expanding
\Gamma(s)
and
(\mu^{2})^{1-s}
we have

\zeta^{\rm sing}_{+}(s,x,x)=\frac{1}{4\pi}\Big(\frac{1}{s-1}-\log\mu^{2}+\gamma+{\cal O}(s-1)\Big)\,{\bf 1}_{2\times 2}\ .

(4.92)

It follows that if we define

\zeta_{+}^{\rm reg}(s,x,y)=\zeta_{+}(s,x,y)-\zeta^{\rm sing}_{+}(s,x,y)

(4.93)

we have subtracted all the potential singularities of this zeta-function, namely the pole at
s=1
that is present for
x=y
and the short-distance singularity of the Green’s function which is present for
s=1
.
In particular, we see that the following limit exists :

\displaystyle\lim_{s\to 0}\Big(\zeta_{+}(s+1,x,x)+s\,\zeta_{+}^{\prime}(s+1,x,x)\Big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\zeta_{+}^{\rm reg}(1,x,x)+\frac{1}{4\pi}\big(\gamma-\log\mu^{2}\big)\,{\bf 1}_{2\times 2}

(4.94)

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle\zeta_{+}^{\rm reg}(1,x,x)+C_{+}\,{\bf 1}_{2\times 2}\ .

The existence of this limit was already anticipated above in (
4.2
), but now we have also determined the value of finite constant
C_{+}
.

It is straightforward to repeat this reasoning for the
\widetilde{\zeta}_{\pm}(s,x,y)
where the zero-modes are excluded. Since the finitely many zero-modes cannot contribute to the singular parts the latter are unchanged,
\widetilde{\zeta}^{\rm sing}_{\pm}(s,x,y)=\zeta^{\rm sing}_{\pm}(s,x,y
. Then whole discussion can be repeated with the result

\displaystyle\lim_{s\to 0}\Big(\widetilde{\zeta}_{+}(s+1,x,x)+s\,\widetilde{\zeta}_{+}^{\prime}(s+1,x,x)\Big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\widetilde{\zeta}_{+}^{\rm reg}(1,x,x)+\frac{1}{4\pi}\big(\gamma-\log\mu^{2}\big)\,{\bf 1}_{2\times 2}

(4.95)

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle\widetilde{\zeta}_{+}^{\rm reg}(1,x,x)+C_{+}\,{\bf 1}_{2\times 2}\ ,

where, in particular, the constant
C_{+}
is the same as before.

We can also translate all these statements to
\zeta_{-}(s,x,y)
by using (
4.1.1
) :

\zeta_{-}^{\rm sing}(s,x,y)=iD_{x}\zeta_{+}^{\rm sing}(s+\frac{1}{2},x,y)=i\frac{(\mu^{2})^{\frac{1}{2}-s}}{4\pi\Gamma(s+\frac{1}{2})}\,(i{\nabla\hskip-8.53581pt/}_{x}+m\gamma_{*})\,E_{s+\frac{1}{2}}\big(\frac{\mu^{2}\ell^{2}(x,y)}{4}\big)\ .\ \

(4.96)

In particular, for
s=\frac{1}{2}
we have again
E_{1}\big(\frac{\mu^{2}\ell^{2}(x,y)}{4}\big)
with its small-
\ell
behaviour given in (
4.89
) and, for
x=y
, obviously (
4.91
) translates into
E_{s+\frac{1}{2}}(0)=\frac{1}{s-\frac{1}{2}}
. However, to evaluate
\zeta_{-}^{\rm sing}(s,x,y)
at
x=y
we must first act with
{\nabla\hskip-8.53581pt/}_{x}
and then set
x=y
:
{\nabla\hskip-8.53581pt/}_{x}\,E_{s+\frac{1}{2}}\big(\frac{\mu^{2}\ell^{2}(x,y)}{4}\big)\big|_{x=y}=\frac{\mu^{2}}{4}\big({\nabla\hskip-8.53581pt/}_{x}\,\ell^{2}(x,y)\big)\big|_{x=y}E^{\prime}_{s+\frac{1}{2}}(0)
.
What does it mean to have a spinorial covariant derivative
{\nabla\hskip-8.53581pt/}=\gamma^{\mu}\nabla_{\mu}^{\rm sp}=\gamma^{\mu}(\partial_{\mu}-\frac{i}{4}\omega_{\mu}\gamma_{*})
acting on
\ell^{2}(x,y)
? One has
\nabla_{\mu}^{\rm sp}\ell^{2}(x,y)=\partial_{\mu}\ell^{2}(x,y)-\frac{i}{4}\omega_{\mu}\gamma_{*}\ell^{2}(x,y)
. This can be evaluated more explicitly, for example, in Riemann normal coordinates around
y
, see the appendix A.1. One then sees that
\partial_{\mu}\ell^{2}(x,y)=2(x-y)^{\mu}
and
\omega_{\mu}=-\frac{{\cal R}}{2}\epsilon_{\mu\nu}(x-y)^{\nu}
(
\epsilon_{12}=-\epsilon_{21}=1
). In particular, we see that for
x\to y
this quantity vanishes. The “invariant” statement we can make is that

{\nabla\hskip-8.53581pt/}_{x}\ell^{2}(x,y)\big|_{x=y}=0\ .

(4.97)

It follows that

\zeta_{-}^{\rm sing}(s,x,x)=im\gamma_{*}\ \frac{(\mu^{2})^{\frac{1}{2}-s}}{4\pi\Gamma(s+\frac{1}{2})}\,E_{s+\frac{1}{2}}(0)\ .

(4.98)

Again, just as for
\zeta_{-}(s,x,x)
, only the
\gamma_{*}
-matrix part remains at coinciding points.
In particular, for
s\to\frac{1}{2}
one finds, much as above,

\zeta^{\rm sing}_{-}(s,x,x)=\frac{im}{4\pi}\,\gamma_{*}\,\Big(\frac{1}{s-\frac{1}{2}}-\log\mu^{2}+\gamma+{\cal O}(s-\frac{1}{2})\Big)\ .

(4.99)

On the other hand, if we first set
s=\frac{1}{2}
and then let
x\to y
, using (
4.89
), we have

\displaystyle\zeta_{-}^{\rm sing}(\frac{1}{2},x,y)\hskip-8.53581pt

\displaystyle\hskip-8.53581pt=

\displaystyle\hskip-8.53581pt\frac{i}{4\pi}\,(i{\nabla\hskip-8.53581pt/}_{x}+m\gamma_{*})\,E_{1}\big(\frac{\mu^{2}\ell^{2}(x,y)}{4}\big)\sim_{x\to y}\frac{i}{4\pi}\,(i{\nabla\hskip-8.53581pt/}_{x}+m\gamma_{*})\,\Big(-\gamma-\log\frac{\mu^{2}\ell^{2}}{4}+{\cal O}\big(\mu^{2}\ell^{2}\big)\Big)

(4.100)

\displaystyle\sim_{x\to y}

\displaystyle-\frac{i}{4\pi}\,\Big(\frac{i{\nabla\hskip-8.53581pt/}\,\ell^{2}}{\ell^{2}}+m\gamma_{*}\,\big(\log\frac{\mu^{2}\ell^{2}}{4}+\gamma\big)\Big)\ .

Of course, this is the short-distance behaviour of the fermionic Green’s function
iS(x,y)
and
we recognise the usual leading singularity
\simeq i{\nabla\hskip-8.53581pt/}\,\ell^{2}/\ell^{2}
of the Dirac Green’s function, as well as a sub-leading logarithmic singularity. Just as for
\zeta_{+}
, It follows that if we define

\zeta_{-}^{\rm reg}(s,x,y)=\zeta_{-}(s,x,y)-\zeta^{\rm sing}_{-}(s,x,y)

(4.101)

we have subtracted all the potential singularities of this zeta-function, namely the pole at
s=\frac{1}{2}
that is present for
x=y
and the short-distance singularity of the Green’s function which is present for
s=\frac{1}{2}
.
In particular, we see that the following limit exists :

\lim_{s\to 0}\Big(\zeta_{-}(s+\frac{1}{2},x,x)+s\,\zeta_{-}^{\prime}(s+\frac{1}{2},x,x)\Big)=\zeta_{-}^{\rm reg}(\frac{1}{2},x,x)+\frac{m}{4\pi}\big(\gamma-\log\mu^{2}\big)\,i\gamma_{*}\ .

(4.102)

Using
\zeta_{-}(s,x,x)=im\gamma_{*}\zeta_{+}(s+\frac{1}{2},x,x)
, cf (
4.33
), we conclude that also

\zeta_{-}^{\rm reg}(\frac{1}{2},x,x)=im\gamma_{*}\,\zeta_{+}^{\rm reg}(1,x,x)\ .

(4.103)

Note that, contrary to the singular parts, to evaluate the regular parts
\zeta_{\pm}^{\rm reg}
the knowledge of the small-
t
asymptotics of the heat kernels is
not
enough, but requires some knowledge about all eigenvalues and eigenfunctions. One way this information is coded is in the regularized and so-called renormalized Green’s functions which we will discuss next.

4.5
Renormalized Green’s functions

4.5.1
G_{\rm R}
,
G_{\zeta}
,
S_{\rm R}
and
S_{\zeta}

For the Green’s function
G(x,y)=\zeta_{+}(1,x,y)
of
D^{2}
, we may define a regularized Green’s function
G^{\rm reg}(x,y)
by subtracting the short-distance singularity, cf (
4.90
)

G^{\rm reg}(x,y)=G(x,y)-G^{\rm sing}(x,y)=G(x,y)+\frac{1}{4\pi}\log\frac{\mu^{2}\ell^{2}(x,y)}{4}\ {\bf 1}_{2\times 2}\ .

(4.104)

Note that this is similar to, but different from
\zeta_{+}^{\rm reg}(1,x,y)=G(x,y)-\frac{1}{4\pi}E_{1}\big(\frac{\mu^{2}\ell^{2}(x,y)}{4}\big)\,{\bf 1}_{2\times 2}
.
But just as
\zeta_{+}^{\rm reg}(1,x,y)
, the regulartized Green’s function
G^{\rm reg}(x,y)
has a well-defined limit as
x\to y
.
The so-called renormalized Green’s function at coinciding points
G_{\rm R}
then is simply defined as

G_{\rm R}(y)=\lim_{x\to y}G^{\rm reg}(x,y)\ .

(4.105)

In complete analogy, we define
S^{\rm reg}(x,y)
and
S_{\rm R}(y)
:

S^{\rm reg}(x,y)=S(x,y)-S^{\rm sing}(x,y)=S(x,y)+\frac{1}{4\pi}\,\big(i{\nabla\hskip-8.53581pt/}_{x}+m\gamma_{*}\big)\log\frac{\mu^{2}\ell^{2}(x,y)}{4}\ {\bf 1}_{2\times 2}\ .

(4.106)

Again, this is similar to, but different from
-i\zeta_{-}^{\rm reg}(\frac{1}{2},x,y)=S(x,y)-\frac{1}{4\pi}(i{\nabla\hskip-8.53581pt/}_{x}+m\gamma_{*})E_{1}\big(\frac{\mu^{2}\ell^{2}(x,y)}{4}\big)
.
The renormalized Green’s function at coinciding points
S_{\rm R}
then is defined as

S_{\rm R}(y)=\lim_{x\to y}S^{\rm reg}(x,y)\ .

(4.107)

Recall from (
4.56
) that
\,{\rm tr}\,\gamma_{*}S(x,y)=m\,{\rm tr}\,G(x,y)
. The same relation obviously also holds between
S^{\rm sing}
and
G^{\rm sing}
. Hence

\,{\rm tr}\,\gamma_{*}S^{\rm reg}(x,y)=m\,{\rm tr}\,G^{\rm reg}(x,y)\ .

(4.108)

If we take the limit
x\to y
of the last relation we get

\,{\rm tr}\,\gamma_{*}S_{\rm R}(y)=m\,{\rm tr}\,G_{\rm R}(y)
, but we will prove the more general relation without the traces below in (
4.114
).

It is also easy to express
G_{\rm R}
and
S_{\rm R}
in terms of
\zeta_{+}^{\rm reg}(1,x,x)

and
\zeta_{-}^{\rm reg}(\frac{1}{2},x,x)
. Indeed,

\displaystyle G^{\rm reg}(x,y)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\zeta_{+}^{\rm reg}(1,x,y)+\frac{1}{4\pi}\Big(E_{1}\big(\frac{\mu^{2}\ell^{2}(x,y)}{4}\big)+\log\frac{\mu^{2}\ell^{2}(x,y)}{4}\Big){\bf 1}_{2\times 2}

\displaystyle S^{\rm reg}(x,y)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-i\zeta_{-}^{\rm reg}(\frac{1}{2},x,y)+\frac{1}{4\pi}(i{\nabla\hskip-8.53581pt/}_{x}+m\gamma_{*})\Big(E_{1}\big(\frac{\mu^{2}\ell^{2}(x,y)}{4}\big)+\log\frac{\mu^{2}\ell^{2}(x,y)}{4}\Big)\ .

(4.109)

Taking the limit
x\to y
and using the asymptotics (
4.89
) of
E_{1}
we get

\displaystyle G_{\rm R}(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\zeta_{+}^{\rm reg}(1,x,x)-\frac{\gamma}{4\pi}{\bf 1}_{2\times 2}

\displaystyle S_{\rm R}(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-i\zeta_{-}^{\rm reg}(\frac{1}{2},x,x)-\frac{m\gamma}{4\pi}\gamma_{*}\ .

(4.110)

For completeness, let us mention that one sometimes defines different “renormalized” Green’s functions
G_{\zeta}(x)
and
S_{\zeta}(x)
from the
\zeta_{\pm}(s,x,x)
by subtracting simply the poles and then letting
s\to 1
or
s\to\frac{1}{2}
:

\displaystyle G_{\zeta}(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lim_{s\to 1}\Big((\mu^{2})^{s-1}\zeta_{+}(s,x,x)-\frac{{\bf 1}_{2\times 2}}{(4\pi)\,(s-1)}\Big)\ ,

\displaystyle S_{\zeta}(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lim_{s\to\frac{1}{2}}\Big(-i\,(\mu^{2})^{s-1/2}\zeta_{-}(s,x,x)-\frac{m\gamma_{*}}{4\pi(s-\frac{1}{2})}\Big)\ .

(4.111)

It follows, using (
4.92
) and (
4.93
)
that

\displaystyle G_{\zeta}(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\zeta_{+}^{\rm reg}(1,x,x)+\frac{\gamma}{4\pi}\,{\bf 1}_{2\times 2}\quad\Rightarrow\quad G_{\zeta}(x)=G_{\rm R}(x)+\frac{\gamma}{2\pi}\,{\bf 1}_{2\times 2}\ ,

\displaystyle S_{\zeta}(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-i\zeta_{-}^{\rm reg}(\frac{1}{2},x,x)+\frac{\gamma}{4\pi}\,m\,\gamma_{*}\quad\Rightarrow\quad S_{\zeta}(x)=S_{\rm R}(x)+\frac{\gamma}{2\pi}\,m\,\gamma_{*}\ .

(4.112)

We can now re-express the relation (
4.94
) in terms of
G_{\rm R}
or
G_{\zeta}
and equivalently the relation (
4.102
) in terms of
S_{\rm R}
or
S_{\zeta}
as follows

\displaystyle\lim_{s\to 0}\Big(\zeta_{+}(s+1,x,x)+s\,\zeta_{+}^{\prime}(s+1,x,x)\Big)=\zeta_{+}^{\rm reg}(1,x,x)+\frac{1}{4\pi}\big(\gamma-\log\mu^{2}\big)\,{\bf 1}_{2\times 2}

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt=G_{\rm R}(x)+\frac{1}{4\pi}\,\big(2\gamma-\log\mu^{2}\big)\ {\bf 1}_{2\times 2}=G_{\zeta}(x)-\frac{1}{4\pi}\,\log\mu^{2}\ {\bf 1}_{2\times 2}\ ,

\displaystyle\lim_{s\to 0}\Big(\zeta_{-}(s+\frac{1}{2},x,x)+s\,\zeta_{-}^{\prime}(s+\frac{1}{2},x,x)\Big)=\zeta_{-}^{\rm reg}(\frac{1}{2},x,x)+\frac{m}{4\pi}\big(\gamma-\log\mu^{2}\big)\,i\gamma_{*}

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt=iS_{\rm R}(x)+\frac{im}{4\pi}\,\big(2\gamma-\log\mu^{2}\big)\ \gamma_{*}=iS_{\zeta}(x)-\frac{im}{4\pi}\,\log\mu^{2}\ \gamma_{*}\ .

(4.113)

Moreover, it follows from (
4.33
) and (
4.5.1
) that
S_{\zeta}(x)=m\gamma_{*}G_{\zeta}(x)
and then by (
4.5.1
) also that
S_{\rm R}(x)=m\gamma_{*}G_{\rm R}(x)
(which also follows from (
4.5.1
) and (
4.103
)) :

\begin{array}[]{|c|}\hline\cr\\
\displaystyle S_{\zeta}(x)=m\gamma_{*}G_{\zeta}(x)\quad,\quad S_{\rm R}(x)=m\gamma_{*}G_{\rm R}(x)\ .\\
\\
\hline\cr\end{array}

(4.114)

Finally note that if the metric has sufficiently many isometries these renormalized Green’s function must be constants. This is the case for the round sphere and for the flat torus. Of course, they still depend on the geometric data of these surfaces, like the radius of the round sphere, or the periods of the flat torus, but not on the point
x
. Their explicit form can be quite non-trivial even for such simple geometries. Let us show this for our preferred example of the flat torus, now with periods
2a
and
2b
.

4.5.2
Green’s function
\widetilde{G}(x,y)
and
\widetilde{G}_{\rm R}(x)
on the flat torus for
m=0

Let us first show how one can obtain the Green’s function of the squared Dirac operator in the massless case on a “rectangular” flat torus with periods
2a
and
2b
, i.e. with
\tau=i\frac{b}{a}
. Since we look at the massless case, we will determine the Green’s function with the zero-mode part excluded, i.e.
\widetilde{G}(x,y)
. Being flat, there is no spin-connection and no curvature and we have, as above, that
D^{2}={\bf 1}_{2\times 2}(-\partial_{\nu}\partial^{\nu})=-{\bf 1}_{2\times 2}\Delta_{\rm scalar}
. Hence, the problem is identical to finding the Green’s function of the scalar Laplace operator on the flat torus. The non-trivial part is to reconcile the appropriate short-distance singularity and the periodicity of the torus. But this problem has a well-known solution in terms of theta functions and the corresponding computation can e.g. be found in
[
11
]
. Using complex coordinates
z=z^{1}+iz^{2}
, where
z^{i}=x^{i}-y^{i}
one finds
\widetilde{G}(x,y)=g(z,\bar{z}){\bf 1}_{2\times 2}
with

\displaystyle g(z,\bar{z})=\frac{(\mathop{\rm Im}z)^{2}}{8ab}-\frac{1}{4\pi}\log\Big|\theta_{1}\big(\frac{z}{2a}\big|i\frac{b}{a}\big)\Big|^{2}\equiv\frac{(\mathop{\rm Im}z)^{2}}{8ab}-\frac{1}{4\pi}\log\Big[\theta_{1}\big(\frac{z}{2a}\big|i\frac{b}{a}\big)\theta_{1}\big(\frac{\bar{z}}{2a}\big|i\frac{b}{a}\big)\Big]\ ,

(4.115)

where the theta function
\theta_{1}
is defined by
[
24
]

\displaystyle\theta_{1}(\nu|\tau)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2q^{1/4}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}(-)^{n}q^{n(n+1)}\sin(2n+1)\pi\nu\ ,\quad q=e^{i\pi\tau}\ .

(4.116)

Indeed, the scalar Laplace operator on flat space in the complex coordinates is simply
\Delta_{\rm scalar}=4\partial_{z}\partial_{\overline{z}}
and then, for
z,\bar{z}\neq 0
, we have
-4\partial_{z}\partial_{\overline{z}}\,g(z,\bar{z})=-4\partial_{z}\partial_{\overline{z}}\,\frac{(\mathop{\rm Im}z)^{2}}{8ab}=\frac{1}{4ab}=\frac{1}{A}
. On the other hand,
g(z,\bar{z})\sim_{|z|\to 0}\,-\frac{1}{4\pi}\log|z|^{2}
which is the required short-distance singularity. Hence, one has correctly
-4\partial_{z}\partial_{\overline{z}}\,g(z,\bar{z})=\delta^{(2)}(z)-\frac{1}{A}
.

As familiar by now, to get the renormalized Green’s function one must subtract the short-distance singularity
-\frac{1}{4\pi}\log\frac{\mu^{2}|z|^{2}}{4}\,{\bf 1}_{2\times 2}
and take the limit
z\to 0
with the result

\widetilde{G}^{m=0}_{\rm R}(x)=-\frac{1}{2\pi}\log\Big(\frac{2\pi}{a\mu}\,q^{\frac{1}{4}}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}(-)^{n}(2n+1)q^{n(n+1)}\Big)\ {\bf 1}_{2\times 2}\quad,\quad q=e^{-\pi b/a}\quad,\quad\text{(flat torus)}\ .

(4.117)

Obviously, this does not depend on the point
x
on the torus, but it depends quite non-trivially on the shape of the torus (i.e. on
b/a
), as well as on its volume (
(2\pi)^{2}ab
).

What about
\widetilde{S}_{\rm R}^{m=0}(x)
? Equation (
4.114
) states that in the massive theory
S_{\rm R}=m\gamma_{*}G_{\rm R}
. Now
from our explicit study of the flat torus we know that the zero-mode parts of
G
and
S
are related as
S^{(0)}=\frac{1}{m}\big(\psi_{\vec{0}}\psi_{\vec{0}}^{\dagger}-\psi^{*}_{\vec{0}}(\psi^{*}_{\vec{0}})^{\dagger}\big)=\frac{1}{m}\gamma_{*}\big(\psi_{\vec{0}}\psi_{\vec{0}}^{\dagger}+\psi^{*}_{\vec{0}}(\psi^{*}_{\vec{0}})^{\dagger}\big)=m\gamma^{*}G^{(0)}
, a relation we will also prove in more generality below. Subtracting this relation from

S_{\rm R}=m\gamma_{*}G_{\rm R}
we get
\widetilde{S}_{\rm R}=m\gamma_{*}\widetilde{G}_{\rm R}
. Now both,
\widetilde{S}_{\rm R}
and
\widetilde{G}_{\rm R}
have a smooth limit as
m\to 0
, and we can conclude that
\widetilde{S}_{\rm R}^{m=0}(x)=0
. Below, in subsection
5.3
, we will study the zero-mode parts of the Green’s functions more generally and show that
\widetilde{S}_{\rm R}^{m=0}(x)=0
always holds.

5
Determining the gravitational action

We now want to determine the variation of the effective gravitational action under infinitesimal conformal variations and write this variation in such a form that it can be integrated to obtain the effective action
S_{\rm grav}[g,\widehat{g}]
. We will do this in an expansion in powers of
m^{2}
. For this expansion to make sense, all quantities entering this expansion must have a well-defined limit as
m\to 0
, i.e. the expansion is done in terms of quantities defined in the massless theory. Thus, for genus one and larger, one must carefully subtract the zero-mode parts from all Green’s functions and zeta-functions.

We will first rewrite the variation of the gravitational action in terms of the renormalized Green’s function
G_{\rm R}
and its variation
\delta G_{\rm R}
. The latter can be re-expressed in an expansion to all orders in
m^{2}
involving higher Green’s functions and zeta functions, still of the massive theory. Proceeding this way first, provides a more streamlined presentation while being already a useful result for spherical topology where the
m\to 0
limit exists for all Green’s functions and zeta-functions without the need to subtract any zero-mode parts. Then, however, we repeat the analogous computation on arbitrary Riemann surfaces, taking into account the presence of zero-modes that must first be subtracted from these quantities. This uses the properties of the zero-mode projectors worked out in subsection
2.5
. The corresponding computations is a bit more cumbersome, but the final result will be only slightly more complicated. Finally, we obtain the gravitational action as an infinite expansion in powers of
m^{2}
, with a finite radius of convergence, valid on a manifold of arbitrary genus. In particular, at order
m^{2}
, we obtain a Mabuchi-like term, as well as terms that are multi-local in
\sigma
and involve the Green’s functions for the reference metric
\widehat{g}
, as well as a finite number of area-like parameters.

5.1
Expressing the variation of
S_{\rm grav}
in terms of
G_{\rm R}

Recall our formula (
3.24
) for the gravitational action in terms of
\zeta(0)
and
\zeta^{\prime}(0)
which gives

\delta S_{\rm grav}=\frac{1}{4}\delta\zeta^{\prime}(0)+\frac{1}{4}\log\mu^{2}\ \delta\zeta(0)\ .

(5.1)

The variations of the zeta functions under infinitesimal conformal rescalings with
\delta\sigma(x)
have been worked out above and are given in (
4.64
) and (
4.67
) or (
4.2
).

\delta\zeta(0)
was shown to be simply
-\frac{m^{2}}{\pi}\int\sqrt{g}\,\delta\sigma=-\frac{m^{2}}{2\pi}\delta A
.
Combining (
4.2
) with (
4.40
)
and (
4.5.1
) we get

\delta\zeta^{\prime}(0)=-\int\sqrt{g}\delta\sigma\Big(\frac{{\cal R}}{12\pi}+\frac{m^{2}}{\pi}\Big)-2m^{2}\int\sqrt{g}\,\delta\sigma\Big(\,{\rm tr}\,G_{\rm R}+\frac{1}{2\pi}(2\gamma-\log\mu^{2})\Big)\ .

(5.2)

Thus

\delta S_{\rm grav}=-\frac{2\gamma+1}{8\pi}\,m^{2}\delta A-\frac{1}{48\pi}\int\sqrt{g}\,\delta\sigma\,{\cal R}-\frac{m^{2}}{2}\int\sqrt{g}\,\delta\sigma\,{\rm tr}\,G_{\rm R}\ .

(5.3)

The term
\sim\delta A
is usually referred to as the variation of a cosmological constant term, while the term
\sim\int\sqrt{g}\,\delta\sigma\,{\cal R}
is the variation of the Liouville action (
1.4
), see (
1.17
) :

\int\sqrt{g}\,\delta\sigma\,{\cal R}=\delta S_{\rm Liouville}\ .

(5.4)

Note that the coefficient of
\delta S_{\rm Liouville}
in
\delta S_{\rm grav}
is exactly
\frac{1}{2}
the one that occurs in the gravitational action of a bosonic scalar matter field. It is interesting to trace back how this coefficient occurs.
The term
\sim{\cal R}
originates from
\zeta_{+}(0,x,x)
which was given in terms of the heat kernel coefficient
a_{1}\equiv F_{1}(x,x)
.
For the bosonic scalar field and scalar Laplacian
a_{1}=\frac{{\cal R}}{6}
. At present,
a_{1}
is
\frac{{\cal R}}{6}-\frac{{\cal R}}{4}=-\frac{{\cal R}}{12}
. But because we are dealing with fermions, the gravitational action has an overall minus sign, so that in the end one gets a
+\frac{{\cal R}}{12}
, i.e. exactly one half the bosonic result.
25
25

25

Of course, we also had the
\frac{1}{4}
multiplying the
\delta\zeta^{\prime}(0)
and
\delta\zeta(0)
instead of a
\frac{1}{2}
in the bosonic case. But this extra
\frac{1}{2}
is offset by a factor
2
coming from the Dirac traces.

Of course, this is consistent with the well-known central charges 1 and
\frac{1}{2}
of the conformal algebra for a single massless scalar and massless Majorana fermion. We conclude that

\begin{array}[]{|c|}\hline\cr\\
\displaystyle\delta\Big[S_{\rm grav}+\frac{1}{48\pi}S_{\rm Liouville}+\frac{m^{2}}{8\pi}\big(2\gamma+1\big)A\Big]=-\frac{m^{2}}{2}\int\sqrt{g}\,\delta\sigma\,{\rm tr}\,G_{\rm R}\ .\\
\\
\hline\cr\end{array}

(5.5)

It remains to characterise the right-hand side of this equations and express it as a total variation of some appropriate quantity. Obviously, we have (using
\delta\sqrt{g}=2\sqrt{g}\,\delta\sigma
)

\int\sqrt{g}\,\delta\sigma\,{\rm tr}\,G_{\rm R}=\frac{1}{2}\ \delta\int\sqrt{g}\,\,{\rm tr}\,G_{\rm R}-\frac{1}{2}\int\sqrt{g}\,\delta\,{\rm tr}\,G_{\rm R}\ ,

(5.6)

so that we can rewrite (
5.5
) as

\delta\Big[S_{\rm grav}+\frac{1}{48\pi}S_{\rm Liouville}+\frac{m^{2}}{8\pi}\big(2\gamma+1\big)A+\frac{m^{2}}{4}\int\sqrt{g}\,\,{\rm tr}\,G_{\rm R}\Big]=\frac{m^{2}}{4}\int\sqrt{g}\,\delta\,{\rm tr}\,G_{\rm R}\ .

(5.7)

5.2
Conformal variations of the Green’s functions

We will need to study the variations of the Green’s functions
S(x,y)
and
G(x,y)
under conformal transformations. Since
G(x,y)=\int d^{2}z\sqrt{g(z)}\,S(x,z)S(z,y)
it will be enough in the first place to obtain the variation of
S(x,y)
, from which the variation of
G(x,y)
can then be deduced. In this subsection we assume again that
m\neq 0
so that there are no zero-modes of the Dirac operator
D=i{\nabla\hskip-8.53581pt/}+m\gamma_{*}
. Generically there are, of course, zero-modes of
i{\nabla\hskip-8.53581pt/}
as discussed in subsection
2.5
. In the next subsection, when we want to do a small mass expansion, we will explicitly separate the contributions of these zero-modes of
i{\nabla\hskip-8.53581pt/}
, that contribute terms of order
\frac{1}{m}
to the Green’s function
S(x,y)
and terms of order
\frac{1}{m^{2}}
to the Green’s function
G(x,y)
. But at present, we will not need to do this separation, as long as
m\neq 0
.

5.2.1
Conformal variations of
S(x,y)
,
G(x,y)
and
G_{\rm R}(y)

Recall that
S(x,y)
is the solution of
D_{x}S(x,y)\equiv(i{\nabla\hskip-8.53581pt/}_{x}+m\gamma_{*})S(x,y)=\delta^{(2)}(x-y)/\sqrt{g}
, see eq. (
4.47
). We want to determine the variation of
S
under a conformal rescaling. Consider two metrics
g
and
g^{\prime}
related by an infinitesimal conformal rescaling
g^{\prime}=e^{2\delta\sigma}g
. Then, of course
\sqrt{g^{\prime}}=e^{2\delta\sigma}\sqrt{g}
and from (
2.79
),
{{\nabla\hskip-8.53581pt/}}{\ {}^{\prime}}=e^{-3\delta\sigma/2}\,{\nabla\hskip-8.53581pt/}\ e^{\delta\sigma/2}
. Thus the corresponding Green’s functions
S
and
S^{\prime}
satisfy

\displaystyle(i{\nabla\hskip-8.53581pt/}_{x}+m\gamma_{*})S(x,y)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\delta^{(2)}(x-y)}{\sqrt{g}}\ ,

\displaystyle\big(ie^{-3\delta\sigma(x)/2}\,{\nabla\hskip-8.53581pt/}\ e^{\delta\sigma(x)/2}+m\gamma_{*}\big)S^{\prime}(x,y)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\delta^{(2)}(x-y)}{\sqrt{g}}\,e^{-3\delta\sigma(x)/2}e^{-\delta\sigma(y)/2}\ .

(5.8)

It looks as if for
m=0
one would simply have
S^{\prime}(x,y)=e^{-\delta\sigma(x)/2}S(x,y)e^{-\delta\sigma(y)/2}
. But for
m=0
we must precisely subtract the zero-mode contribution and deal with
\widetilde{S}
and
\widetilde{S}^{\prime}
instead and then the relation becomes again more complicated due to the conformal transformation of the zero-mode projector. This will be dealt with in the next two subsections. But this remark motivates the following definition of
\widehat{\delta}S
:

\displaystyle S^{\prime}(x,y)\equiv S(x,y)+\delta S(x,y)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle e^{-\delta\sigma(x)/2}S(x,y)e^{-\delta\sigma(y)/2}+\widehat{\delta}S(x,y)

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\quad\delta S(x,y)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}\big(\delta\sigma(x)+\delta\sigma(y)\big)S(x,y)+\widehat{\delta}S(x,y)\ .

(5.9)

We then insert this
S^{\prime}(x,y)
into the second equation (
5.2.1
) and develop to first order in the variation. This yields

D_{x}\,\widehat{\delta}S(x,y)\equiv(i{\nabla\hskip-8.53581pt/}_{x}+m\gamma_{*})\widehat{\delta}S(x,y)=-m\delta\sigma(x)\,\gamma_{*}S(x,y)\ ,

(5.10)

which is solved by multiplying with the Green’s function of
D
and integrating :

\int{\rm d}^{2}z\,\sqrt{g(z)}\,S(x,z)\,D_{z}\,\widehat{\delta}S(z,y)=-m\int{\rm d}^{2}z\,\sqrt{g(z)}\,\delta\sigma(z)\,S(x,z)\,\gamma_{*}\,S(z,y)\ .

(5.11)

Using the hermiticity of
D_{z}
the terms on the left-hand side is
\int{\rm d}^{2}z\sqrt{g(z)}\,(D_{z}S(z,x))^{\dagger}\,\widehat{\delta}S(z,y)

=\widehat{\delta}S(x,y)
, so that

\widehat{\delta}S(x,y)=-m\int{\rm d}^{2}z\sqrt{g}\,\delta\sigma(z)\,S(x,z)\gamma_{*}S(z,y)\ ,

(5.12)

and

\delta S(x,y)=-\frac{1}{2}\big(\delta\sigma(x)+\delta\sigma(y)\big)S(x,y)-m\int{\rm d}^{2}z\sqrt{g}\,\delta\sigma(z)\,S(x,z)\gamma_{*}S(z,y)\ .

(5.13)

One can now continue from this to obtain the variation of
S_{\rm R}(x)
by subtracting the variation of the singular part of
S(x,y)
, and then deduce the variation of
\,{\rm tr}\,\gamma_{*}S_{\rm R}=\,{\rm tr}\,G_{R}
. We have tried this avenue, but it turns out not to be the most straightforward one. Instead, we will determine the variation of
G(x,y)
from the variation of
S(x,y)
and then deduce the variation of
G_{\rm R}(x)
by subtracting the variation of the singular part of
G(x,y)
. Recall that

G(x,y)=\int{\rm d}z\,S(x,z)S(z,y)
,
where here and in the following, to simplify the notations, we abbreviate the integration measure
{\rm d}^{2}z\sqrt{g(z)}
by writing simply
{\rm d}z
. Of course, one should not forget then that
{\rm d}z
changes under a conformal rescaling :

\int{\rm d}z\simeq\int{\rm d}^{2}z\sqrt{g(z)}\quad,\quad\delta\,{\rm d}z={\rm d}z\ 2\delta\sigma(z)\ .

(5.14)

The variation of
G(x,y)
is then given by

\displaystyle\hskip-28.45274pt\delta G(x,y)=\delta\int dz\,S(x,z)S(z,y)=\int{\rm d}z\,\Big[2\delta\sigma(z)S(x,z)S(z,y)+\delta S(x,z)S(z,y)+S(x,z)\delta S(z,y)\Big]

\displaystyle=\int{\rm d}z\,\Big[\big(\delta\sigma(z)-\frac{\delta\sigma(x)}{2}-\frac{\delta\sigma(y)}{2}\big)S(x,z)S(z,y)+\widehat{\delta}S(x,z)S(z,y)+S(x,z)\widehat{\delta}S(z,y)\Big]\ .

Now the short-distance singularity of
G(x,y)
is
G^{\rm sing}(x,y)=-\frac{1}{4\pi}\log\frac{\mu^{2}\ell^{2}(x,y)}{4}{\bf 1}_{2\times 2}
and we need to compute its variation

\delta G^{\rm sing}(x,y)=-\frac{1}{4\pi}\frac{\delta\ell^{2}(x,y)}{\ell^{2}(x,y)}\ {\bf 1}_{2\times 2}\ .

(5.16)

Actually, we only need this in the limit
x\to y
, i.e. when
x
and
y
are only separated by an infinitesimal
{\rm d}x
. But then the (geodesic) distance between the two points is just given by
\ell^{2}(x,x+{\rm d}x)=g_{\mu\nu}{\rm d}x^{\mu}{\rm d}x^{\nu}=e^{2\sigma(x)}\widehat{g}_{\mu\nu}{\rm d}x^{\mu}{\rm d}x^{\nu}
. Obviously then
\delta\ell^{2}(x,x+{\rm d}x)=2\delta\sigma(x)\ell^{2}(x,x+{\rm d}x)
. Somewhat more generally, for
x
close to
y
, one can show that (see e.g. the appendix A1 of
[
25
]
) :

\frac{\delta\ell^{2}(x,y)}{\ell^{2}(x,y)}=\delta\sigma(x)+\delta\sigma(y)+{\cal O}(\ell^{2})\ .\quad\Rightarrow\quad\delta G^{\rm sing}(x,y)=-\frac{1}{4\pi}\big(\delta\sigma(x)+\delta\sigma(y)+{\cal O}(\ell^{2})\big)\ {\bf 1}_{2\times 2}\ .

(5.17)

We see that the variation of the singular part is non-singular. Hence it must be that
\delta G(x,y)
as given in (
5.2.1
) also is non-singular. Indeed, the terms involving
\widehat{\delta}S
are easily seen to be non-singular, so only the first term in (
5.2.1
) could potentially be singular as
x\to y
. Indeed
S(y,z)S(z,y)
is divergent as
\sim\frac{1}{\ell^{2}(z,y)}
when the integration variable
z
gets close to
y
. But then the factor
\delta\sigma(z)-\delta\sigma(y)
vanishes as
z-y\sim\ell(z,y)
so that the integrand behaves as
\frac{1}{\ell(z,y)}
which is an integrable singularity in 2 dimensions, leading to a finite integral over
{\rm d}^{2}z
. We may thus take the limit
x\to y
:

\lim_{x\to y}\delta G(x,y)=\int{\rm d}z\,\Big[\big(\delta\sigma(z)-\delta\sigma(y)\big)S(y,z)S(z,y)+\widehat{\delta}S(y,z)S(z,y)+S(y,z)\widehat{\delta}S(z,y)\Big]\ ,

(5.18)

and subtracting (
5.17
) with
x=y
we get

\delta G_{\rm R}(y)=\int{\rm d}z\,\Big[\big(\delta\sigma(z)-\delta\sigma(y)\big)S(y,z)S(z,y)+\widehat{\delta}S(y,z)S(z,y)+S(y,z)\widehat{\delta}S(z,y)\Big]+\frac{\delta\sigma(y)}{2\pi}\ {\bf 1}_{2\times 2}\ .

(5.19)

If we now take the trace and integrate over
y
, the first term in the integrand is odd under the exchange of
y
and
z
and thus does not contribute. Using the cyclicity of the trace and exchanging again
y
and
z
, the second and third terms are easily seen to be identical. We arrive at

\int{\rm d}y\,\,\delta\,{\rm tr}\,G_{\rm R}(y)=2\int{\rm d}y\,{\rm d}z\,\,{\rm tr}\,S(y,z)\,\widehat{\delta}S(z,y)+\frac{\delta A}{2\pi}\ .

(5.20)

We now insert the explicit expression (
5.12
) for
\widehat{\delta}S
to get

\displaystyle\int{\rm d}y\,\delta\,{\rm tr}\,G_{\rm R}(y)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\delta A}{2\pi}-2m\int{\rm d}y\,\int{\rm d}z\,\int{\rm d}u\,\delta\sigma(u)\,{\rm tr}\,S(y,z)S(z,u)\gamma_{*}S(u,y)

(5.21)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\delta A}{2\pi}-2m\int{\rm d}y\,{\rm d}u\,\delta\sigma(u)\,{\rm tr}\,G(y,u)\gamma_{*}S(u,y)\ .

In the previous section, we have shown in (
4.56
) that
\,{\rm tr}\,\gamma_{*}S(x,y)=m\,{\rm tr}\,G(x,y)
. The argument was based on the relation
S(x,y)=(i{\nabla\hskip-8.53581pt/}_{x}+m\gamma_{*})G(x,y)
and the fact that
G
only contains the matrices
{\bf 1}_{2\times 2}
and
\gamma_{*}
and that in the end only
{\bf 1}_{2\times 2}
has a non-vanishing trace. One can show in completely the same way that

\,{\rm tr}\,\gamma_{*}S(x,y)G(u_{1},v_{1})\ldots G(u_{n},v_{n})=m\,{\rm tr}\,G(x,y)G(u_{1},v_{1})\ldots G(u_{n},v_{n})\ .

(5.22)

This allows us to rewrite (
5.21
) as

\int{\rm d}y\,\delta\,{\rm tr}\,G_{\rm R}(y)=\frac{\delta A}{2\pi}-2m^{2}\int{\rm d}y\,{\rm d}u\,\delta\sigma(u)\,{\rm tr}\,G(u,y)G(y,u)=\frac{\delta A}{2\pi}-2m^{2}\int{\rm d}u\,\delta\sigma(u)\,{\rm tr}\,G_{2}(u,u)\ ,

(5.23)

where we used the orthonormality of the eigenfunctions to rewrite the right-hand side in terms of a higher Green’s function
G_{2}
:

\int{\rm d}y\,G(u,y)G(y,v)=\sum_{n}\frac{1}{\lambda_{n}^{4}}\big(\chi_{n}(u)\chi_{n}^{\dagger}(v)+\phi_{n}(u)\phi_{n}^{\dagger}(v)\big)\equiv G_{2}(u,v)\equiv\zeta_{+}(2,u,v)\ .

(5.24)

Let us note for later reference that one can equally easily show that

\int{\rm d}u\,{\rm d}v\,S(x,u)G(u,v)S(v,y)=G_{2}(x,y)\equiv\zeta_{+}(2,x,y)\ .

(5.25)

Finally, inserting (
5.23
) into (
5.7
), we get

\delta\Big[S_{\rm grav}+\frac{1}{48\pi}S_{\rm Liouville}+\frac{\gamma}{4\pi}m^{2}A+\frac{m^{2}}{4}\int{\rm d}y\,\,{\rm tr}\,G_{\rm R}(y)\Big]=-\frac{m^{4}}{2}\int{\rm d}u\,\delta\sigma(u)\,{\rm tr}\,G_{2}(u,u)\ ,

(5.26)

If there are no zero-modes of
i{\nabla\hskip-8.53581pt/}\,
, which is the case for spherical topology, then
S
and
G
have finite limits as
m\to 0
and one can assert that the term on the right-hand side of (
5.26
) is of order
m^{4}
. In this case, if we satisfy ourselves with an order
m^{2}
calculation, we may just drop the right-hand side, and moreover replace
G_{\rm R}
by the corresponding quantity in the massless theory. However, for genus one or larger, we have zero-modes of
i{\nabla\hskip-8.53581pt/}
and one must separate the zero-mode and the non zero-mode parts to isolate the contributions that are truly of order
m^{2}
. This separation will be the subject of subsection
5.3
.

5.2.2
Conformal variation of the higher Green’s function
G_{n},\ n\geq 2

But let us go on and determine
\int{\rm d}u\,\delta\,{\rm tr}\,G_{n}(u,u)
for
n\geq 2
. We have

G_{n}(u_{1},u_{1})=\int{\rm d}u_{2}\dots{\rm d}u_{n}\,G(u_{1},u_{2})G(u_{2},u_{3})\ldots G(u_{n},u_{1})\ ,

(5.27)

so that

\displaystyle\hskip-14.22636pt\int{\rm d}u_{1}\ \delta\,{\rm tr}\,G_{n}(u_{1},u_{1}))=\int{\rm d}u_{1}\ldots{\rm d}u_{n}\Big[2\sum_{i=2}^{n}\delta\sigma(u_{i})\,{\rm tr}\,G(u_{1},u_{2})\ldots G(u_{n},u_{1})

\displaystyle\hskip 170.71652pt+\sum_{i=1}^{n}\,{\rm tr}\,G(u_{1},u_{2})\ldots\delta G(u_{i},u_{i+1})\ldots G(u_{n},u_{1})\Big]

\displaystyle\hskip-14.22636pt=\int{\rm d}u_{1}\Big[2(n-1)\delta\sigma(u_{1})\,{\rm tr}\,G_{n}(u_{1},u_{1})+n\int{\rm d}u_{1}\,{\rm d}u_{2}\,\,{\rm tr}\,\delta G(u_{1},u_{2})G_{n-1}(u_{2},u_{1})\Big],

(5.28)

where in the last step we used the cyclicity of the trace.
The variation
\delta G(z,y)
was determined in (
5.2.1
) and it follows that

\displaystyle\int{\rm d}u_{1}\,{\rm d}u_{2}\,\,{\rm tr}\,\big(\delta G(u_{1},u_{2})\big)\,G_{n-1}(u_{2},u_{1})

\displaystyle=\int{\rm d}v\,du_{1}\,{\rm d}u_{2}\big(\delta\sigma(v)-\frac{\delta\sigma(u_{1})+\delta\sigma(u_{2})}{2}\big)\,{\rm tr}\,S(u_{1},v)S(v,u_{2})G_{n-1}(u_{2},u_{1})

\displaystyle+\int{\rm d}v\,du_{1}\,{\rm d}u_{2}\,{\rm tr}\,\Big(\widehat{\delta}S(u_{1},v)S(v,u_{2})+S(u_{1},v)\widehat{\delta}S(v,u_{2})\Big)G_{n-1}(u_{2},u_{1})\ .

(5.29)

The different pieces of the first term on the right-hand side can be rewritten, using the cyclicity of the trace and an identity analogous to (
5.25
), as

\int{\rm d}v{\rm d}u_{1}{\rm d}u_{2}\,\delta\sigma(v)\,{\rm tr}\,S(u_{1},v)S(v,u_{2})G_{n-1}(u_{2},u_{1})=\int{\rm d}v\,\delta\sigma(v)\,{\rm tr}\,G_{n}(v,v)
, and similarly for
\int{\rm d}v{\rm d}u_{1}{\rm d}u_{2}\,\delta\sigma(u_{1})\,{\rm tr}\,S(u_{1},v)S(v,u_{2})G_{n-1}(u_{2},u_{1})=
\int\,du_{1}\delta\sigma(u_{1})\,{\rm tr}\,G_{n}(u_{1},u_{1})
, etc, and we see that the terms on the right-hand side of (
5.2.2
) involving the explicit
\delta\sigma
cancel. Hence only the terms involving
\widehat{\delta}S
remain.
Thus

\displaystyle\hskip-19.91684pt\int{\rm d}u_{1}\,{\rm d}u_{2}\,{\rm tr}\,\big(\delta G(u_{1},u_{2})\big)\,G_{n-1}(u_{2},,u_{1})

\displaystyle\hskip-19.91684pt=-m\hskip-2.84526pt\int\hskip-2.84526pt{\rm d}v\,{\rm d}w\,du_{1}{\rm d}u_{2}\delta\sigma(w)\,{\rm tr}\,\Big(S(u_{1},w)\gamma_{*}S(w,v)S(v,u_{2})+S(u_{1},v)S(v,w)\gamma_{*}S(w,u_{2})\Big)G_{n-1}(u_{2},u_{1})

\displaystyle=-m\int{\rm d}w\,du_{1}\delta\sigma(w)\,\,{\rm tr}\,\Big(\gamma_{*}G_{n}(w,u_{1})S(u_{1},w)+\gamma_{*}S(w,u_{1})G_{n}(u_{1},w))\Big)

\displaystyle=-2m^{2}\int{\rm d}w\,\delta\sigma(w)\,{\rm tr}\,G_{n+1}(w,w)\ .

(5.30)

We see that we can rewrite (
5.2.2
) as

\int{\rm d}u\ \delta\,{\rm tr}\,G_{n}(u,u)-2(n-1)\int{\rm d}u\,\delta\sigma(u)\,{\rm tr}\,G_{n}(u,u)=-2n\,m^{2}\int{\rm d}u\,\delta\sigma(u)\,{\rm tr}\,G_{n+1}(u,u)\ .

(5.31)

Now, on the left-hand side we combine
\int{\rm d}u\ \delta\,{\rm tr}\,G_{n}+2\int{\rm d}u\,\delta\sigma(u)\,{\rm tr}\,G_{n}=\delta\int{\rm d}u\,\,{\rm tr}\,G_{n}
so that, after dividing by
2n
:

\int{\rm d}u\,\delta\sigma(u)\,{\rm tr}\,G_{n}=\delta\int{\rm d}u\,\frac{1}{2n}\,{\rm tr}\,G_{n}+m^{2}\int{\rm d}u\,\delta\sigma(u)\,{\rm tr}\,G_{n+1}\ ,

(5.32)

where we dropped the arguments, all Green’s functions being obviously evaluated at coinciding points
u
.
If we multiply this equation by
m^{2n}
and iterate it
N-n+1
times we get

m^{2n}\int{\rm d}u\,\delta\sigma(u)\,{\rm tr}\,G_{n}=\delta\int{\rm d}u\,\sum_{k=n}^{N}\frac{m^{2k}}{2k}\,{\rm tr}\,G_{k}+m^{2(N+1)}\int{\rm d}u\,\delta\sigma(u)\,{\rm tr}\,G_{N+1}\ .

(5.33)

We now use this relation for
n=2
to rewrite the right-hand side of (
5.26
) to get

\displaystyle\delta\Big[S_{\rm grav}+\frac{1}{48\pi}S_{\rm Liouville}+\frac{\gamma}{4\pi}m^{2}A+\frac{1}{4}\int{\rm d}^{2}y\sqrt{g}\,\Big(m^{2}\,{\rm tr}\,G_{\rm R}(y)+\sum_{k=2}^{N}\frac{m^{2k}}{k}\,{\rm tr}\,G_{k}(y,y)\Big)\Big]

\displaystyle=-\frac{m^{2(N+1)}}{2}\int{\rm d}^{2}y\,\sqrt{g}\,\delta\sigma(y)\,{\rm tr}\,G_{N+1}(y,y)\ ,

(5.34)

where on the left-hand side one can replace
\int{\rm d}^{2}y\sqrt{g}\,{\rm tr}\,G_{k}(y,y)=\zeta(k)
.

5.2.3
Variation of the gravitational action to all orders in
m^{2}
for spherical topology

Contrary to what this expansion (
5.2.2
) might suggest, it is important to realise that the quantities
G_{\rm R}
and
\zeta(k)
are the quantities computed in the massive theory. However, in the absence of zero-modes of
i{\nabla\hskip-8.53581pt/}\,
(i.e. for spherical topology), they all have a finite limit as
m
goes to zero. Below, in subsubsection
5.3.5
, we will give a precise bound on the remainder term on the right-hand side of (
5.2.2
). In particular,
for
\frac{m^{2}A}{a_{*}^{2}}<1
, where
a_{*}
is the smallest eigenvalue of
D
for zero mass and unit area
A=1
, this term goes to zero as
N\to\infty
. Hence

\delta\Big[S_{\rm grav}^{\rm sphere}+\frac{1}{48\pi}S_{\rm Liouville}+\frac{\gamma}{4\pi}m^{2}A+\frac{m^{2}}{4}\int\sqrt{g}\,\,{\rm tr}\,G_{\rm R}(y)+\sum_{k=2}^{\infty}\frac{m^{2k}}{4k}\zeta(k)\Big]=0

(5.35)

It is relatively straightforward to re-express
G_{\rm R}
and
\zeta(k)
in terms of the same set of quantities in the massless theory. We will do this below for the case of arbitrary genus.

5.3
Variation of the gravitational action for arbitrary genus

5.3.1
Zero-mode and non-zero-mode parts of the Green’s functions

As discussed in subsection
2.5
, for genus one or larger, there are zero-modes
\psi_{0,i}
of
i{\nabla\hskip-8.53581pt/}\,
. They may be chosen as having definite chirality, i.e. be eigenstates of
\gamma_{*}
. There are as many positive as negative chirality zero-modes. For the arbitrary torus there is exactly one zero-mode of each chirality and we have obtained them explicitly. In general, since
i{\nabla\hskip-8.53581pt/}\,\psi_{0,i}=0
we have
D\psi_{0,i}=m\gamma_{*}\psi_{0,i}=\pm m\psi_{0,i}
, i.e
\lambda_{0}=m
. Recall the spectral decomposition (
4.1.5
) of the Green’s functions in terms of the eigenfunctions and eigenvalues. It follows that the zero-mode parts of the Green’s functions
S
and
G
behave as
\frac{1}{m}
and
\frac{1}{m^{2}}
and do not have a limit as
m\to 0
. Since we want to do a small-
m
expansion, we have to separate the Green’s functions into their zero-mode and their non-zero-mode parts. In terms of the projectors
P_{0}
and
Q_{0}
on the zero-modes defined in (
2.102
) and (
2.106
), the zero-mode contribution to the Green’s functions
G
and
S
simply are
\frac{P_{0}}{\lambda_{0}^{2}}=\frac{P_{0}}{m^{2}}
and
\frac{Q_{0}}{\lambda_{0}}=\frac{Q_{0}}{m}
. Obviously then, one has the decomposition

\displaystyle G(x,y)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle G_{(0)}(x,y)+\widetilde{G}(x,y)\quad,\quad G_{(0)}(x,y)=\frac{P_{0}(x,y)}{m^{2}}\ ,

\displaystyle S(x,y)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle S_{(0)}(x,y)+\widetilde{S}(x,y)\quad,\quad S_{(0)}(x,y)=\frac{Q_{0}(x,y)}{m}\ ,

(5.36)

where
\widetilde{G}(x,y)
and
\widetilde{S}(x,y)
are the contributions of the non-zero-modes for which the
\lambda_{n}
have finite limits as
m\to 0
. Hence,
\widetilde{G}(x,y)
and
\widetilde{S}(x,y)
have finite limits as
m\to 0
, which obviously is not the case for
G^{(0)}
and
S^{(0)}
.

As already noted, the zero-modes are orthogonal to the non-zero modes so that

\displaystyle\int{\rm d}z\,S_{(0)}(x,z)\widetilde{S}(z,y)=0\quad,\quad\int{\rm d}z\,G_{(0)}(x,z)\widetilde{G}(z,y)=0\ ,

\displaystyle\int{\rm d}z\,S_{(0)}(x,z)\widetilde{G}(z,y)=0\quad,\quad\int{\rm d}z\,G_{(0)}(x,z)\widetilde{S}(z,y)=0\ .

(5.37)

Furthermore (cf (
2.107
))

\int{\rm d}z\,S_{(0)}(x,z)S_{(0)}(z,y)=\frac{1}{m^{2}}\int{\rm d}z\,Q_{0}(x,z)Q_{0}(z,y)=\frac{1}{m^{2}}P_{0}(x,y)=G_{(0)}(z,y)\ .

(5.38)

We also have
G(x,y)=\int{\rm d}z\,S(x,z)S(z,y)
. If we substitute
S=\widetilde{S}+S_{(0)}
and use the orthogonality between the zero and non-zeromodes, eq. (
5.3.1
), we get

\widetilde{G}(x,y)=\int{\rm d}z\,\widetilde{S}(x,z)\widetilde{S}(z,y)\ .

(5.39)

We can similarly separate all higher Green’s functions as
G_{n}(x,y)=G_{n}^{(0)}(x,y)+\widetilde{G}_{n}(x,y)
and we have separate relations for the zero-mode and the non-zero-mode parts :

\displaystyle\widetilde{G}_{n+1}(x,y)=\int{\rm d}z\,\widetilde{G}(x,z)\widetilde{G}_{n}(z,y)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int{\rm d}z\,{\rm d}u\,\widetilde{S}(x,z)\widetilde{G}_{n}(z,u)\widetilde{S}(u,y)\ ,

\displaystyle G_{n+1}^{(0)}(x,y)=\int{\rm d}z\,G_{(0)}(x,z)\widetilde{G}_{n}^{(0)}(z,y)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int{\rm d}z\,{\rm d}u\,S_{(0)}(x,z)G_{n}^{(0)}(z,u)S_{(0)}(u,y).\ \ \

(5.40)

Finally note that the zero-mode parts satisfy
i{\nabla\hskip-8.53581pt/}\,S_{(0)}=i{\nabla\hskip-8.53581pt/}\,G_{(0)}=0
and recall from (
2.107
) that
Q_{0}=\gamma_{*}P_{0}
so that

S_{(0)}=m\gamma_{*}G_{(0)}=(i{\nabla\hskip-8.53581pt/}+m\gamma_{*})G_{(0)}
. Subtracting this identity from

S=(i{\nabla\hskip-8.53581pt/}+m\gamma_{*})G
results in

\widetilde{S}(x,y)=(i{\nabla\hskip-8.53581pt/}_{x}+m\gamma_{*})\widetilde{G}(x,y)\equiv D_{x}\widetilde{G}(x,y)\ ,

(5.41)

so that the zero-mode parts and the non-zero-mode parts satisfy this same relation separately. Of course, all these relations also follow directly from the spectral representations of the Green’s functions.

We now want to rewrite the various relations of the previous subsection in terms of these
\widetilde{S}(x,y)
and
\widetilde{G}(x,y)
for which the
m\to 0
limits exist. In the end we will find that we obtain almost the same result as in (
5.2.2
) or (
5.35
) except that all quantities are replaced by the the corresponding quantities with tildes referring to the non-zero-modes. This result can be understood as being due to the fact that when varying the gravitational action the eigenvalue
\lambda_{0}
corresponding to the zero-modes does not change and that the zero-mode and non-zero-mode contributions are orthogonal.

But let us proceed.
First note that the zero-mode pieces do not contribute to the short-distance singularities. Hence
G(x,y)
and
\widetilde{G}(x,y)
have the same short-distance singularity
-\frac{1}{4\pi}\log\frac{\mu^{2}\ell^{2}(x,y)}{4}\,{\bf 1}_{2\times 2}
. Similarly
S(x,y)
and
\widetilde{S}(x,y)
have the same short-distance singularity
-\frac{1}{4\pi}(i{\nabla\hskip-8.53581pt/}_{x}+m\gamma_{*})\log\frac{\mu^{2}\ell^{2}(x,y)}{4}
. Thus

\displaystyle S^{\rm reg}(x,y)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle S_{(0)}(x,y)+\widetilde{S}(x,y)+\frac{1}{4\pi}(i{\nabla\hskip-8.53581pt/}_{x}+m\gamma_{*})\log\frac{\mu^{2}\ell^{2}(x,y)}{4}=\frac{Q_{0}(x,y)}{m}+\widetilde{S}^{\rm reg}(x,y)\ ,

\displaystyle G^{\rm reg}(x,y)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle G_{(0)}(x,y)+\widetilde{G}(x,y)+\frac{1}{4\pi}\log\frac{\mu^{2}\ell^{2}(x,y)}{4}\,{\bf 1}_{2\times 2}=\frac{P_{0}(x,y)}{m^{2}}+\widetilde{G}^{\rm reg}(x,y)\ .

(5.42)

By taking the coincidence limit
x\to y
we then get

S_{\rm R}(y)=\frac{Q_{0}(y,y)}{m}+\widetilde{S}_{\rm R}(y)\quad,\quad G_{\rm R}(y)=\frac{P_{0}(y,y)}{m^{2}}+\widetilde{G}_{\rm R}(y)\ .

(5.43)

Also, since
Q_{0}=\gamma_{*}P_{0}
, the relation (
4.114
) translates into

\widetilde{S}_{\rm R}=m\gamma_{*}\widetilde{G}_{\rm R}\ .

(5.44)

But both,
\widetilde{S}_{\rm R}
and
\widetilde{G}_{\rm R}
have a smooth limit as
m\to 0
, and we find that
\widetilde{S}_{\rm R}
vanishes in the massless theory :

\widetilde{S}^{m=0}_{\rm R}(x)=0\ .

(5.45)

We will need the variations of the zero-mode parts of the Green’s functions
G_{(0)}(x,y)
and
S_{(0)}(x,y)
under conformal transformations. As we have just seen, the latter are given in terms of the zero-mode projectors
P_{0}(x,y)
and
Q_{0}(x,y)=\gamma_{*}P_{0}(x,y)
. But the conformal variations of
P_{0}(x,y)
have been studied in some detail in subsection
2.5.4
, see in particular (
2.115
) and (
2.116
).

5.3.2
Expressing the variation of the gravitational action in terms of
\widetilde{G}_{\rm R}

The variation of the gravitational action, eq. (
5.5
) contains the term

-\frac{m^{2}}{2}\int\sqrt{g}\,\delta\sigma\,{\rm tr}\,G_{\rm R}
on its right-hand side. We now use (
5.43
) and (
2.116
) to rewrite this as

\displaystyle-\frac{m^{2}}{2}\int{\rm d}y\,\delta\sigma(y)\,{\rm tr}\,G_{\rm R}(y)=-\frac{m^{2}}{2}\int{\rm d}y\,\delta\sigma(y)\,{\rm tr}\,\Big(\frac{P_{0}(y,y)}{m^{2}}+\widetilde{G}_{\rm R}(y)\Big)

\displaystyle=-\delta\ \Big(\log\det{\cal P}_{0}+\frac{m^{2}}{4}\int{\rm d}y\,\,{\rm tr}\,\widetilde{G}_{\rm R}(y)\Big)+\frac{m^{2}}{4}\int{\rm d}y\,\delta\,{\rm tr}\,\widetilde{G}_{\rm R}(y)\ .

(5.46)

We can then rewrite our initial equation (
5.7
) as

\delta\Big(S_{\rm grav}+\frac{1}{48\pi}S_{\rm Liouville}+\log\det{\cal P}_{0}+\frac{m^{2}}{8\pi}(2\gamma+1)A+\frac{m^{2}}{4}\int\sqrt{g}\,\,{\rm tr}\,\widetilde{G}_{\rm R}\Big)=\frac{m^{2}}{4}\int\sqrt{g}\,\delta\,{\rm tr}\,\widetilde{G}_{\rm R}\ .

(5.47)

This is as (
5.7
) but with
G_{\rm R}
replaced by
\widetilde{G}_{\rm R}
and the extra term
\delta\,\log\det{\cal P}_{0}
on the left-hand-side.

At present, this term
\delta\,\log\det{\cal P}_{0}
has emerged from separating the zero-mode piece from
G_{\rm R}
. But we have seen in section 3 that, since
\delta\lambda_{0}=0
, we may write
\delta S_{\rm grav}
from the beginning in terms of quantities that do not involve the zero-modes.
Then in eq. (
5.5
) it is directly
\widetilde{G}_{\rm R}
that appears. So where does this term
\delta\,\log\det{\cal P}_{0}
then come from?
Recall that the variation of the gravitational action was
\delta S_{\rm grav}=\frac{1}{4}\delta\zeta^{\prime}(0)+\frac{1}{4}\log\mu^{2}\ \delta\zeta(0)
, cf (
5.1
). First,
\delta\zeta(0)
only involved the residue of the pole of

\zeta_{+}(s,x,x)
which is the same as the residue of the pole of
\widetilde{\zeta}_{+}(s,x,x)
. Second,
when expressing
\delta\zeta^{\prime}(0)
directly in terms of the quantities without zero-modes
as given in the second line of (
4.67
), namely

\frac{1}{4}\delta\zeta^{\prime}(0)=\frac{1}{2}\int\delta\sigma\,{\rm tr}\,\widetilde{\zeta}_{+}(0,x,x)-\frac{m^{2}}{2}\int\delta\sigma\,{\rm tr}\,\widetilde{\zeta}_{+}^{\rm reg}(1,x,x)-2m^{2}C_{+}\delta A\ ,

(5.48)

the last two terms on the right-hand side will be expressed in terms of
\int\delta\sigma\widetilde{G}_{\rm R}
with
no
zero-mode contribution subtracted.
On the other hand,
\zeta_{+}(0,x,x)
was given in terms of the heat kernel coefficient
\frac{1}{4\pi}F_{1}(x,x)
, cf (
4.40
), as
\zeta_{+}(0,x,x)=-\frac{1}{4\pi}\big(\frac{{\cal R}(x)}{12}+m^{2}\big)\,{\bf 1}_{2\times 2}
. Now the corresponding

\widetilde{\zeta}_{+}(0,x,x)
is obtained from the heat kernel
\widetilde{K}_{+}(t,x,x)
with its zero-mode part subtracted. But the latter is just
e^{-m^{2}t}P_{0}(x,x)=P_{0}(x,x)\big(1+{\cal O}(t)\big)
. It follows that
\frac{1}{4\pi}\widetilde{F}_{1}(x,x)=\frac{1}{4\pi}F_{1}(x,x)-P_{0}(x,x)
and, hence,

\widetilde{\zeta}_{+}(0,x,x)=-\frac{1}{4\pi}\big(\frac{{\cal R}(x)}{12}+m^{2}\big)\,{\bf 1}_{2\times 2}-P_{0}(x,x)\ ,

(5.49)

so that

\frac{1}{2}\int{\rm d}x\,\delta\sigma(x)\,{\rm tr}\,\widetilde{\zeta}_{+}(0,x,x)=-\frac{1}{48\pi}\delta S_{\rm Liouville}-\frac{m^{2}}{8\pi}\delta A-\delta\,\log\det{\cal P}_{0}\ ,

(5.50)

where we used (
2.116
). Thus one gets again all the terms of (
5.47
).
This latter computation also shows that this zero-mode related term
\log\det{\cal P}_{0}
is actually already present in the massless theory and should have appeared together with the Liouville action. But, as mentioned earlier, in the massless theory one may redefine the matter partition function by an appropriate factor to precisely cancel this term.

Starting from (
5.47
), we can now proceed as in the previous subsection and express
\int\sqrt{g}\,\delta\,{\rm tr}\,\widetilde{G}_{\rm R}
in terms of a total variation and
\int\sqrt{g}\,\delta\,{\rm tr}\,\widetilde{G}_{2}(y,y)
, which then is again expressed as a total variation and a term
\int\sqrt{g}\,\delta\,{\rm tr}\,\widetilde{G}_{3}(y,y)
, etc. We have to be careful to subtract any zero-mode contributions where they might appear, but the orthogonality (
5.3.1
) of the zero and non-zero mode parts of the Green’s functions essentially ensures that the variations of the non-zero-mode quantities only involve other non-zero-mode quantities.

To begin with, we have

\delta\widetilde{G}_{\rm R}(y)=\lim_{x\to y}\ \delta\,\Big(\widetilde{G}(x,y)+\frac{1}{4\pi}\log\frac{\mu^{2}\ell^{2}(x,y)}{4}\ {\bf 1}_{2\times 2}\Big)\ .

(5.51)

We already observed above that
\delta\big(\frac{1}{4\pi}\log\frac{\mu^{2}\ell^{2}(x,y)}{4}\big)\sim_{x\to y}\frac{\delta\sigma(y)}{2\pi}
is non-singular and, hence, the same must be true for
\delta\widetilde{G}(x,y)
. From (
5.39
) we know that

\widetilde{G}(x,y)=\int{\rm d}z\,\widetilde{S}(x,z)\widetilde{S}(z,y)
, and we can set
x=y

after
taking the variation, so that

\delta\widetilde{G}_{\rm R}(y)=\int{\rm d}z\Big(2\delta\sigma(z)\widetilde{S}(y,z)\widetilde{S}(z,y)+\delta\widetilde{S}(y,z)\widetilde{S}(z,y)+\widetilde{S}(y,z)\delta\widetilde{S}(z,y)\Big)+\ \frac{\delta\sigma(y)}{2\pi}\,{\bf 1}_{2\times 2}\ .\ \ \

(5.52)

5.3.3
Determining
\delta\widetilde{S}

We then need to determine
\delta\widetilde{S}(x,y)
. To do this, we just repeat the steps leading to (
5.12
) and (
5.13
), but now subtracting the zero-mode parts.
We start with
(i{\nabla\hskip-8.53581pt/}_{x}+m\gamma_{*})S(x,y)=\delta^{(2)}(x-y)/\sqrt{g}
and separate the non-zero and zero-mode parts :
S=\widetilde{S}+S_{(0)}
. The zero-mode piece
S_{(0)}=\frac{Q_{0}}{m}
satisfies

i{\nabla\hskip-8.53581pt/}_{x}\,S_{(0)}(x,y)=0
so that (recall
\gamma_{*}Q_{0}=P_{0}
)

(i{\nabla\hskip-8.53581pt/}_{x}+m\gamma_{*})\widetilde{S}(x,y)=\frac{\delta^{(2)}(x-y)}{\sqrt{g}}-m\gamma_{*}S_{(0)}(x,y)=\frac{\delta^{(2)}(x-y)}{\sqrt{g}}-P_{0}(x,y).

(5.53)

Of course, this just expresses that
D_{x}\widetilde{S}(x,y)
gives the completeness relation without the zero-modes. Consider now a
metric
g^{\prime}
related to
g
by an infinitesimal conformal rescaling
g^{\prime}=e^{2\delta\sigma}g
. Then, as before,
\sqrt{g^{\prime}}=e^{2\delta\sigma}\sqrt{g}
and
{{\nabla\hskip-8.53581pt/}}{\ {}^{\prime}}=e^{-3\delta\sigma/2}\,{\nabla\hskip-8.53581pt/}\ e^{\delta\sigma/2}
. We have, of course,
\widetilde{S}^{\prime}(x,y)=S^{\prime}(x,y)-S^{\prime}_{(0)}(x,y)
where
i{\nabla\hskip-8.53581pt/}{\ {}^{\prime}}S^{\prime}_{(0)}(x,y)=0
. Then (
5.53
) becomes

\big(ie^{-3\delta\sigma(x)/2}\,{\nabla\hskip-8.53581pt/}\ e^{\delta\sigma(x)/2}+m\gamma_{*}\big)\widetilde{S}^{\prime}(x,y)=\frac{\delta^{(2)}(x-y)}{\sqrt{g}}\,e^{-3\delta\sigma(x)/2}e^{-\delta\sigma(y)/2}-P_{0}^{\prime}(x,y)\ .

(5.54)

Much as in (
5.2.1
), we set

\displaystyle\widetilde{S}^{\prime}(x,y)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\widetilde{S}(x,y)+\delta\widetilde{S}(x,y)=e^{-\delta\sigma(x)/2}\Big(\widetilde{S}(x,y)+\widehat{\delta}\widetilde{S}(x,y)\Big)e^{-\delta\sigma(y)/2}

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\quad\delta\widetilde{S}(x,y)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}\big(\delta\sigma(x)+\delta\sigma(y)\big)\widetilde{S}(x,y)+\widehat{\delta}\widetilde{S}(x,y)\ ,

(5.55)

where the equivalence between the two equations holds up to terms of second order in the variations.
Developing (
5.54
) to first order in the variations and comparing with (
5.53
) we get

D_{x}\,\widehat{\delta}\widetilde{S}(x,y)\equiv(i{\nabla\hskip-8.53581pt/}_{x}+m\gamma_{*})\widehat{\delta}\widetilde{S}(x,y)=-m\delta\sigma(x)\,\gamma_{*}\widetilde{S}(x,y)-\big(\frac{3}{2}\delta\sigma(x)+\frac{1}{2}\delta\sigma(y)\big)P_{0}(x,y)-\delta P_{0}(x,y)\ ,

(5.56)

where
\delta P_{0}=P_{0}^{\prime}-P_{0}
, so that

\displaystyle\hskip-85.35826pt\int{\rm d}z\,\widetilde{S}(x,z)D_{z}\,\widehat{\delta}\widetilde{S}(z,y)=-m\int{\rm d}z\,\widetilde{S}(x,z)\delta\sigma(z)\,\gamma_{*}\widetilde{S}(z,y)

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt-\int{\rm d}z\,\widetilde{S}(x,z)\Big[\big(\frac{3}{2}\delta\sigma(z)+\frac{1}{2}\delta\sigma(y)\big)P_{0}(z,y)+\delta P_{0}(z,y)\Big]\ .

(5.57)

On the left-hand side, as before, we use the hermiticity of
D_{z}
and let it act on
\widetilde{S}(x,z)
, according to (
5.53
) :

\int{\rm d}z\,\widetilde{S}(x,z)D_{z}\,\widehat{\delta}\widetilde{S}(z,y)=\widehat{\delta}\widetilde{S}(x,y)-\int{\rm d}z\,P_{0}(x,z)\widehat{\delta}\widetilde{S}(z,y)\ .

(5.58)

Hence,

\displaystyle\hskip-56.9055pt\widehat{\delta}\widetilde{S}(x,y)=-m\int{\rm d}z\,\widetilde{S}(x,z)\delta\sigma(z)\,\gamma_{*}\widetilde{S}(z,y)

\displaystyle\hskip 0.0pt-\int{\rm d}z\,\widetilde{S}(x,z)\Big[\big(\frac{3}{2}\delta\sigma(z)+\frac{1}{2}\delta\sigma(y)\big)P_{0}(z,y)+\delta P_{0}(z,y)\Big]+\int{\rm d}z\,P_{0}(x,z)\,\widehat{\delta}\widetilde{S}(z,y)\ .

(5.59)

Recall the orthogonality relation between
\widetilde{S}
and
G_{(0)}\sim P_{0}
which we write for metric
g
and for the metric
g^{\prime}=e^{2\delta\sigma}g
(with
{\rm d}z
still standing for
{\rm d}^{2}z\sqrt{g(z)}
) :

\int{\rm d}z\,P_{0}(x,z)\widetilde{S}(z,y)=0\quad,\quad\int{\rm d}z\,e^{3\delta\sigma(z)/2}e^{-\delta\sigma(y)/2}P^{\prime}_{0}(x,z)\Big(\widetilde{S}(z,y)+\widehat{\delta}\widetilde{S}(z,y)\Big)=0\ .

(5.60)

First, this shows that the term
\sim\delta\sigma(y)
in (
5.3.3
) does not contribute.
Next, developing the second equation to first order and using the first one we get

\int{\rm d}z\,P_{0}(x,z)\,\widehat{\delta}\widetilde{S}(z,y)=-\int{\rm d}z\,\Big(\frac{3}{2}\delta\sigma(z)P_{0}(x,z)\widetilde{S}(z,y)+\delta P_{0}(x,z)\widetilde{S}(z,y)\Big)\ .

(5.61)

Hence,

\displaystyle\hskip-28.45274pt\widehat{\delta}\widetilde{S}(x,y)=-m\int{\rm d}z\,\delta\sigma(z)\,\widetilde{S}(x,z)\gamma_{*}\widetilde{S}(z,y)

\displaystyle\hskip-5.69046pt-\int{\rm d}z\,\Big[\frac{3}{2}\delta\sigma(z)\big(\widetilde{S}(x,z)P_{0}(z,y)+P_{0}(x,z)\widetilde{S}(z,y)\big)+\widetilde{S}(x,z)\delta P_{0}(z,y)+\delta P_{0}(x,z)\widetilde{S}(z,y)\Big].\ \ \

(5.62)

Now,
\delta P_{0}(x,z)
has been worked out in (
2.115
). It contains a piece
-\frac{1}{2}\big(\delta\sigma(x)+\delta\sigma(z)\big)P_{0}(x,z)
as well as a piece
\sum_{i,j}a_{ij}\,\psi_{0,i}(x)\psi_{0,j}^{\,{\dagger}}(z)
where the
a_{ij}
are some constants. Thus, when multiplied by
\widetilde{S}(z,y)
and integrated over
{\rm d}z
