the second piece does not contribute. Similarly for
\int{\rm d}z\,\widetilde{S}(x,z)\delta P_{0}(z,y)
. Thus we can rewrite (
5.3.3
) as

\widehat{\delta}\widetilde{S}(x,y)=-\int{\rm d}z\,\delta\sigma(z)\,\Big[m\,\widetilde{S}(x,z)\gamma_{*}\widetilde{S}(z,y)+\widetilde{S}(x,z)P_{0}(z,y)+P_{0}(x,z)\widetilde{S}(z,y)\Big]\ .

(5.63)

This, together with the second equation (
5.3.3
) determines
\delta\widetilde{S}
entirely in terms of
\widetilde{S}
,
\delta\sigma
and
P_{0}
. Note that we cannot (yet) use the “orthogonality” of
\widetilde{S}
and
P_{0}
since the integral over
{\rm d}z
also involves
\delta\sigma(z)
. However, we will see in the sequel that the
P_{0}
terms drop out from the relevant quantities we will compute here.

5.3.4
Determining
\delta\widetilde{G}_{\rm R}

We have already given
\delta\widetilde{G}_{\rm R}
in terms of
\delta\widetilde{S}
and
\delta\sigma
in (
5.52
). Using now the second equation (
5.3.3
) to express
\delta\widetilde{S}
in terms of
\delta\sigma\,\widetilde{S}
and
\widehat{\delta}\widetilde{S}
, this becomes

\delta\widetilde{G}_{\rm R}(y)=\int{\rm d}z\,\Big(\big(\delta\sigma(z)-\delta\sigma(y)\big)\widetilde{S}(y,z)\widetilde{S}(z,y)+\widehat{\delta}\widetilde{S}(x,z)\widetilde{S}(z,y)+\widetilde{S}(x,y)\widehat{\delta}\widetilde{S}(z,y)\big)+\frac{\delta\sigma(y)}{2\pi}\ {\bf 1}_{2\times 2}\ .\quad

(5.64)

Let us remark for further reference that if we do not take the
x\to y
limit and discard the last term we also get

\delta\widetilde{G}(x,y)=\int{\rm d}z\,\Big(\big(\delta\sigma(z)-\frac{\delta\sigma(x)}{2}-\frac{\delta\sigma(y)}{2}\big)\widetilde{S}(x,z)\widetilde{S}(z,y)+\widehat{\delta}\widetilde{S}(x,z)\widetilde{S}(z,y)+\widetilde{S}(x,z)\widehat{\delta}\widetilde{S}(z,y)\Big)\ .

(5.65)

If we take the trace of
\delta\widetilde{G}_{\rm R}
and integrate, as before, the term
\delta\sigma(z)-\delta\sigma(y)
vanishes by antisymmetry, and we are left with

\int{\rm d}y\,\delta\,\,{\rm tr}\,\widetilde{G}_{\rm R}(y)=2\int{\rm d}y\,{\rm d}z\,\,{\rm tr}\,\hat{\delta}\widetilde{S}(y,z)\widetilde{S}(z,y)+\frac{\delta A}{2\pi}\ .

(5.66)

Next, we insert
\widehat{\delta}\widetilde{S}
from (
5.63
) :

\displaystyle\hskip-28.45274pt\int{\rm d}y\,\delta\,\,{\rm tr}\,\widetilde{G}_{\rm R}(y)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-2\int{\rm d}y\,{\rm d}z\,{\rm d}u\,\delta\sigma(u)\,{\rm tr}\,\Big(m\,\widetilde{S}(y,u)\gamma_{*}\widetilde{S}(u,z)

(5.67)

\displaystyle\hskip 85.35826pt+\widetilde{S}(y,u)P_{0}(u,z)+P_{0}(y,u)\widetilde{S}(u,z)\Big)\widetilde{S}(z,y)+\frac{\delta A}{2\pi}\ .

We see that the terms involving
P_{0}
now vanish :
\int{\rm d}z\,P_{0}(u,z)\widetilde{S}(z,y)=0
and (using the cyclicity of the trace)

\int{\rm d}y\,\widetilde{S}(z,y)P_{0}(y,u)=0
. Furthermore, we can replace
\int{\rm d}y\,\widetilde{S}(z,y)\widetilde{S}(y,u)
by
\widetilde{G}(z,u)
. We are left with

\displaystyle\hskip-28.45274pt\int{\rm d}y\,\delta\,\,{\rm tr}\,\widetilde{G}_{\rm R}(y)=-2m\int{\rm d}z\,{\rm d}u\,\delta\sigma(u)\,{\rm tr}\,\gamma_{*}\widetilde{S}(u,z)\widetilde{G}(z,u)+\frac{\delta A}{2\pi}

\displaystyle\hskip-28.45274pt=-2m^{2}\int{\rm d}z\,{\rm d}u\,\delta\sigma(u)\,{\rm tr}\,\widetilde{G}(u,z)\widetilde{G}(z,u)+\frac{\delta A}{2\pi}=-2m^{2}\int{\rm d}u\,\delta\sigma(u)\,{\rm tr}\,\widetilde{G}_{2}(u,u)+\frac{\delta A}{2\pi}\ ,

(5.68)

where we used, much as for
S
and
G
, that

\widetilde{S}(u,z)=(i{\nabla\hskip-8.53581pt/}_{u}+m\gamma_{*})\widetilde{G}(u,z)
and the fact that only the
m\gamma_{*}\widetilde{G}
survives in the trace. Inserting this into (
5.47
) we get

\displaystyle\hskip-56.9055pt\delta\Big(S_{\rm grav}+\frac{1}{48\pi}S_{\rm Liouville}+\log\det{\cal P}_{0}+\frac{\gamma}{4\pi}\,m^{2}A+\frac{m^{2}}{4}\int{\rm d}u\,\,{\rm tr}\,\widetilde{G}_{\rm R}(u)\Big)

\displaystyle\hskip 184.9429pt=-\frac{m^{4}}{2}\int{\rm d}u\,\delta\sigma(u)\,\,{\rm tr}\,\widetilde{G}_{2}(u,u)\ .

(5.69)

Of course, this is very similar to (
5.26
), except that now all Green’s functions have their zero-modes excluded and, instead, we have the zero-mode related piece
\log\det{\cal P}_{0}
.

Since
\widetilde{G}_{2}
has a finite limit as
m\to 0
, the right-hand side is a genuine order
m^{4}
-term and one can read the complete variation of
S_{\rm grav}
up to and including the order
m^{2}
-terms from the left-hand side. But we also want to obtain the higher-order contributions which requires to study similarly the variation of the higher
\widetilde{G}_{n}
.

5.3.5
Conformal variation of the higher
\widetilde{G}_{n}(x,y),n\geq 2

As before, we now want to obtain the conformal variations of the non-zero-mode parts of the higher Green’s functions. It follows immediately from the orthonormality of the zero-mode and non-zero-mode parts of
G
that

\widetilde{G}_{n}(x,y)=G_{n}(x,y)-G_{n}^{(0)}(x,y)\equiv G_{n}(x,y)-\frac{P_{0}(x,y)}{m^{2n}}=\int{\rm d}u_{2}\ldots{\rm d}u_{n}\,\widetilde{G}(x,u_{2})\widetilde{G}(u_{2},u_{3})\ldots\widetilde{G}(u_{n},y)\ .

(5.70)

Then, as before for
G_{n}
in (
5.2.2
), we now have for
\widetilde{G}_{n}

\displaystyle\int{\rm d}u_{1}\,\delta\,\,{\rm tr}\,\widetilde{G}_{n}(u_{1},u_{1})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(2(n-1)\int{\rm d}u\,\delta\sigma(u)\,{\rm tr}\,\widetilde{G}_{(n)}(u,u)

(5.71)

\displaystyle+n\int{\rm d}u_{1}{\rm d}u_{2}\,{\rm tr}\,\delta\widetilde{G}(u_{1},u_{2})\widetilde{G}_{(n-1)}(u_{2},u_{1})\ .

Let us evaluate the second term on the right-hand side of this equation.
\delta\widetilde{G}
was given in (
5.65
) so that

\displaystyle\hskip-56.9055pt\int{\rm d}x\,{\rm d}y\,\,{\rm tr}\,\delta\widetilde{G}(x,y)\widetilde{G}_{(n-1)}(y,x)

\displaystyle=\int{\rm d}x\,{\rm d}y\,{\rm d}z\,\Big[\big(\delta\sigma(z)-\frac{\delta\sigma(x)}{2}-\frac{\delta\sigma(y)}{2}\big)\,{\rm tr}\,\widetilde{S}(x,z)\widetilde{S}(z,y)\widetilde{G}_{(n-1)}(y,x)

\displaystyle\hskip 85.35826pt+\,{\rm tr}\,\big(\widehat{\delta}\widetilde{S}(x,z)\widetilde{S}(z,y)+\widetilde{S}(x,z)\widehat{\delta}\widetilde{S}(z,y)\big)\widetilde{G}_{(n-1)}(y,x)\Big]\ .

(5.72)

Using the cyclicity of the trace we can show again that the term involving explicitly the
\delta\sigma
vanishes and only the terms involving
\widehat{\delta}\widetilde{S}
remain. Using (
5.63
) these terms are

\displaystyle\int{\rm d}x\,{\rm d}y\,{\rm d}z\,\,{\rm tr}\,\big(\widehat{\delta}\widetilde{S}(x,z)\widetilde{S}(z,y)+\widetilde{S}(x,z)\widehat{\delta}\widetilde{S}(z,y)\big)\widetilde{G}_{(n-1)}(y,x)

\displaystyle=-\int{\rm d}x\,{\rm d}y\,{\rm d}u\,{\rm d}z\,\delta\sigma(z)\,{\rm tr}\,\Big[m\,\widetilde{S}(x,z)\gamma_{*}\widetilde{S}(z,u)+\widetilde{S}(x,z)P_{0}(z,u)+P_{0}(x,z)\widetilde{S}(z,u)\Big]\times

\displaystyle\hskip 142.26378pt\times\big[\widetilde{S}(u,y)\widetilde{G}_{(n-1)}(y,x)+\widetilde{G}_{(n-1)}(u,y)\widetilde{S}(y,x)\big]\ .

(5.73)

Again, by the cyclicity of the trace, we see that the terms involving
P_{0}
always appear as
\int{\rm d}u\,P_{0}(z,u)\widetilde{S}(u,y)=0
or
\int{\rm d}x\,\widetilde{G}_{(n-1)}(y,x)P_{0}(x,z)=0
, etc. Hence they give vanishing contributions and (
5.3.5
) reduces to

\displaystyle(\ref{deltahattermsGn})

\displaystyle=-m\int{\rm d}x\,{\rm d}z\,\delta\sigma(z)\,\,{\rm tr}\,\big(\widetilde{S}(x,z)\gamma_{*}\widetilde{G}_{n}(z,x)+\gamma_{*}\widetilde{S}(z,x)\widetilde{G}_{n}(x,z)\Big)

(5.74)

\displaystyle=-m^{2}\int{\rm d}x\,{\rm d}z\,\delta\sigma(z)\,\,{\rm tr}\,\big(\widetilde{G}(x,z)\widetilde{G}_{n}(z,x)+\widetilde{G}(z,x)\widetilde{G}_{n}(x,z)\Big)

\displaystyle=-2m^{2}\int{\rm d}y\,\delta\sigma(y)\,\,{\rm tr}\,\widetilde{G}_{n+1}(y,y)\ ,

where in the next to last step we used the by now familiar argument to replace
\widetilde{S}\gamma_{*}
and
\gamma_{*}\widetilde{S}
by
m\widetilde{G}
inside the trace.
Putting the pieces together, using (
5.71
), (
5.3.5
) and (
5.74
), we get

\int{\rm d}y\,\delta\,{\rm tr}\,\widetilde{G}_{n}(y,y)=2(n-1)\int{\rm d}y\,\delta\sigma(y)\,\,{\rm tr}\,\widetilde{G}_{n}(y,y)-2n\,m^{2}\int{\rm d}y\,\delta\sigma(y)\,\,{\rm tr}\,\widetilde{G}_{n+1}(y,y)\ ,

(5.75)

exactly as in (
5.31
) but now for the
\widetilde{G}_{n}
instead of the
G_{n}
. One can then do the same manipulations, obtaining the analogue relations of (
5.32
) (again we do drop the arguments
y
)

\int{\rm d}y\,\delta\sigma(y)\,{\rm tr}\,\widetilde{G}_{n}=\frac{1}{2n}\,\delta\,\int{\rm d}y\,\,{\rm tr}\,\widetilde{G}_{n}+m^{2}\int{\rm d}y\,\delta\sigma(y)\,\,{\rm tr}\,\widetilde{G}_{n+1}\ .

(5.76)

This can be iterated to obtain a relation analogous to (
5.33
) :

m^{2n}\int{\rm d}y\,\delta\sigma(y)\,{\rm tr}\,\widetilde{G}_{n}=\delta\,\int{\rm d}y\,\sum_{k=n}^{N}\frac{m^{2k}}{2k}\,\,{\rm tr}\,\widetilde{G}_{k}+m^{2(N+1)}\int{\rm d}y\,\delta\sigma(y)\,\,{\rm tr}\,\widetilde{G}_{N+1}\ .

(5.77)

This looks like an expansion in powers of
m^{2}
, but one should be aware that the Green’s functions
\widetilde{G}_{k}
are the Green’s function of the massive theory. However, and this was the whole point of having subtracted the zero-modes, each
\widetilde{G}_{k}
now has a finite limit as
m\to 0
and can be expanded itself in powers of
m^{2}
, as we will do shortly.

Let us give an upper bound on the term on the right-hand side.
We have
\int{\rm d}y\,\,{\rm tr}\,\widetilde{G}_{N+1}=\sum_{n\neq 0}\frac{2}{\lambda_{n}^{2(N+1)}}=\widetilde{\zeta}(N+1)
. Furthermore,
\lambda_{n}^{2}=\lambda_{n}^{2}|_{m=0}+m^{2}
and the eigenvalues of the massless theory have a simple scaling with the area, namely
26
26

26

This follows rather trivially from the fact that a conformal rescaling with
constant

\sigma
changes
A\to e^{2\sigma}A
and
{\nabla\hskip-8.53581pt/}\to e^{-\sigma}{\nabla\hskip-8.53581pt/}\
so that
{{\nabla\hskip-8.53581pt/}\ }^{2}\to e^{-2\sigma}{{\nabla\hskip-8.53581pt/}\ }^{2}
and, hence,
\lambda_{n}^{2}\to e^{-2\sigma}\lambda_{n}^{2}
.

\lambda_{n}^{2}|_{m=0}^{A}=\frac{1}{A}\lambda_{n}^{2}|_{m=0}^{A=1}\ .

(5.78)

If we denote
M_{\delta\sigma}=\max|\delta\sigma|\geq 0
we have the following bound

\displaystyle\Big|\int{\rm d}y\,\delta\sigma(y)\,\,{\rm tr}\,\widetilde{G}_{N+1}\Big|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle M_{\delta\sigma}\ \Big|\int{\rm d}y\,\,{\rm tr}\,\widetilde{G}_{N+1}\Big|=M_{\delta\sigma}\ \widetilde{\zeta}(N+1)\leq M_{\delta\sigma}\ \widetilde{\zeta}_{m=0}(N+1)

(5.79)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle A^{N+1}M_{\delta\sigma}\ \widetilde{\zeta}_{m=0}^{A=1}(N+1)\ .

Now, just as Riemann’s zeta-function
\zeta_{\rm R}(s)
has a finite limit as
s\to\infty
, our
\widetilde{\zeta}_{m=0}^{A=1}(N+1)
has a well-defined “limit behaviour” as
N\to\infty
and this is given in terms of the smallest eigenvalue that contributes.
If we denote
a_{0}\equiv\lambda_{1}|_{m=0}^{A=1}
is the smallest (positive) non-zero eigenvalue for zero mass and unit area
A=1
, and
d_{a_{0}}
its degeneracy, then
\zeta_{m=0}^{A=1}(N+1)\sim_{N\to\infty}2\frac{d_{a_{0}}}{a_{0}^{2(N+1)}}
. Hence

\Big|m^{2(N+1)}\int{\rm d}y\,\delta\sigma(y)\,\,{\rm tr}\,\widetilde{G}_{N+1}\Big|\leq m^{2(N+1)}A^{N+1}M_{\delta\sigma}\ \widetilde{\zeta}_{m=0}^{A=1}(N+1)\sim_{N\to\infty}2d_{a_{0}}M_{\delta\sigma}\,\Big(\frac{m^{2}A}{a_{0}^{2}}\Big)^{N+1}\ .

(5.80)

Thus for small enough mass,
m^{2}A<a_{0}^{2}
the right-hand side goes to zero as
N\to\infty

and we may drop the corresponding term in (
5.77
). We may similarly estimate the radius of convergence of the series

\int{\rm d}y\ \sum_{k=n}^{\infty}\frac{m^{2k}}{2k}\,\,{\rm tr}\,\widetilde{G}_{k}=\sum_{k=n}^{\infty}\frac{m^{2k}}{2k}\widetilde{\zeta}(k)=\sum_{k=n}^{\infty}\frac{(m^{2}A)^{k}}{2k}\widetilde{\zeta}^{A=1}(k)\ .

(5.81)

If we now denote by
a
the smallest positive eigenvalue (of the massive theory) appearing in
\widetilde{\zeta}^{A=1}
(such that its zero-mass limit is
a_{0}>0
), then the series converges for
\big|\frac{m^{2}A}{a^{2}}\big|<1
. We conclude that for small enough mass we can take the
N\to\infty
limit in (
5.77
) and obtain

m^{2n}\int{\rm d}^{2}y\,\sqrt{g}\,\delta\sigma(y)\,{\rm tr}\,\widetilde{G}_{n}=\delta\,\int{\rm d}^{2}y\,\sqrt{g}\ \sum_{k=n}^{\infty}\frac{m^{2k}}{2k}\,\,{\rm tr}\,\widetilde{G}_{k}\ .

(5.82)

5.3.6
Variation of the gravitational action to all orders in
m^{2}
for arbitrary genus

We insert this result (
5.82
) for
n=2
into eq. (
5.3.4
) and get

\displaystyle\hskip-56.9055pt\delta\Bigg[S_{\rm grav}+\frac{1}{48\pi}S_{\rm Liouville}+\log\det{\cal P}_{0}+\frac{\gamma}{4\pi}\,m^{2}A

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt+\frac{1}{4}\int{\rm d}^{2}u\sqrt{g}\,\Big(m^{2}\,{\rm tr}\,\widetilde{G}_{\rm R}(u)+\sum_{k=2}^{\infty}\frac{m^{2k}}{k}\,{\rm tr}\,\widetilde{G}_{k}(u,u)\Big)\Bigg]=0\ .

(5.83)

As before, the integrals of the traces of the higher Green’s functions
\widetilde{G}_{k}
at coinciding points are just the
\widetilde{\zeta}(k)
so that equivalently

\delta\Bigg[S_{\rm grav}+\frac{1}{48\pi}S_{\rm Liouville}+\log\det{\cal P}_{0}+\frac{\gamma}{4\pi}\,m^{2}A+\frac{m^{2}}{4}\int{\rm d}^{2}u\sqrt{g}\,\,{\rm tr}\,\widetilde{G}_{\rm R}(u)+\sum_{k=2}^{\infty}\frac{m^{2k}}{4k}\widetilde{\zeta}(k)\Bigg]=0\ .

(5.84)

This looks very similar to the result (
5.35
) where we did not separate the zero-mode contributions. The only difference (apart from replacing the
G
’s by the
\widetilde{G}
’s, or replacing the
\zeta(k)
by the
\widetilde{\zeta}(k)
) is the presence of the zero-mode term
\log\det{\cal P}_{0}
which only contributes at order
m^{0}
.
Indeed, we have seen in sect.
4.2
that
\delta\lambda_{0}=0
so that
\delta\zeta(s)=\delta\widetilde{\zeta}(s)
.

As already remarked above,
\widetilde{G}_{\rm R}
and the
\widetilde{\zeta}(k)
are the quantities defined with non-vanishing mass. Since we subtracted the zero-mode parts, these quantities have a well-defined series expansion in
m^{2}
involving quantities defined in the massless theory. We will now work out these expansions. First, we have
\lambda_{n}^{2}=\lambda_{n,(m=0)}^{2}+m^{2}
, cf (
2.92
), so that

\frac{1}{\lambda_{n}^{2k}}=\frac{1}{(\lambda_{n,(m=0)}^{2}+m^{2})^{k}}=\frac{1}{\lambda_{n,(m=0)}^{2k}}\Big(1+\frac{m^{2}}{\lambda_{n,(m=0)}^{2}}\Big)^{-k}=\frac{1}{\lambda_{n,(m=0)}^{2k}}\sum_{r=0}^{\infty}(-)^{r}\frac{(k+r-1)!}{(k-1)!\ r!}\Big(\frac{m^{2}}{\lambda_{n,(m=0)}^{2}}\Big)^{r}\ .

(5.85)

Then

\displaystyle\hskip-28.45274pt\sum_{k=2}^{\infty}\frac{m^{2k}}{k}\frac{1}{\lambda_{n}^{2k}}=\sum_{k=2}^{\infty}\sum_{r=0}^{\infty}(-)^{r}\frac{(k+r)!}{k!\ r!}\frac{1}{k+r}\Big(\frac{m^{2}}{\lambda_{n,(m=0)}^{2}}\Big)^{r+k}=\sum_{l=2}^{\infty}\Big(\sum_{r=0}^{l-2}(-)^{r}\frac{l!}{(l-r)!r!}\Big)\frac{1}{l}\Big(\frac{m^{2}}{\lambda_{n,(m=0)}^{2}}\Big)^{l}

\displaystyle\hskip-28.45274pt=\sum_{l=2}^{\infty}\Big[\sum_{r=0}^{l}(-)^{r}\frac{l!}{(l-r)!r!}-(-)^{l-1}l-(-)^{l}\Big]\frac{1}{l}\Big(\frac{m^{2}}{\lambda_{n,(m=0)}^{2}}\Big)^{l}=\sum_{l=2}^{\infty}\Big[0-(-)^{l-1}l-(-)^{l}\Big]\frac{1}{l}\Big(\frac{m^{2}}{\lambda_{n,(m=0)}^{2}}\Big)^{l}

\displaystyle\hskip-28.45274pt=\sum_{l=2}^{\infty}(-)^{l}\frac{l-1}{l}\Big(\frac{m^{2}}{\lambda_{n,(m=0)}^{2}}\Big)^{l}\ .

(5.86)

If we now sum over
n\neq 0
we get the corresponding relation for the sum of zeta-functions

\sum_{k=2}^{\infty}\frac{m^{2k}}{k}\widetilde{\zeta}(k)=\sum_{k=2}^{\infty}(-)^{k}\frac{k-1}{k}m^{2k}\widetilde{\zeta}_{m=0}(k)\ .

(5.87)

Note that to lowest order in
m
this is just
\frac{m^{4}}{2}\widetilde{\zeta}(2)=\frac{2-1}{2}\,m^{4}\widetilde{\zeta}_{m=0}(2)+{\cal O}(m^{6})
, which is indeed obviously correct, and it is similarly easy to directly check also the next order.

We may similarly proceed with
\widetilde{G}_{\rm R}
. First note that the short distance singularity of
\widetilde{G}(x,y)
is the same for vanishing and for non-vanishing mass. Indeed, this singularity depends on the 2-derivative part of
D^{2}
and is not changed by adding the mass or not. On the other hand, as already used repeatedly, the
\widetilde{G}_{r}(x,y)
have no short-distance singularities for
r\geq 2
and the
\widetilde{G}_{r}(y,y)
are finite. Recall the obvious fact that if
\psi_{n}
is an eigenfunction of
D^{2}
for
m\neq 0
, i.e.
D^{2}\psi_{n}=\lambda_{n}^{2}\psi_{n}
where
D^{2}=-{\nabla\hskip-8.53581pt/}^{\ 2}+m^{2}
, then this same
\psi_{n}
is also an eigenfunction of
D^{2}
for
m=0
, i.e. of
-{\nabla\hskip-8.53581pt/}^{\ 2}
with eigenvalue
\lambda_{n}^{2}-m^{2}=\lambda^{2}_{n,(m=0)}
.
(Note that this argument is correct for the eigenfunctions of
D^{2}
, but not for the eigenfunctions of
D
and the following reasoning for
\widetilde{G}
can not be transposed to
\widetilde{S}
.)
Hence, using (
5.85
) for
k=1

\displaystyle\widetilde{G}(x,y)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{n\neq 0}\frac{1}{\lambda_{n}^{2}}\big(\psi_{n}(x)\psi_{n}^{\dagger}(y)+\psi_{n}^{*}(x)(\psi_{n}^{*})^{\dagger}(y)\big)

(5.88)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{r=0}^{\infty}(-)^{r}m^{2r}\sum_{n\neq 0}\frac{1}{\lambda_{n,(m=0)}^{2r+2}}\big(\psi_{n}(x)\psi_{n}^{\dagger}(y)+\psi_{n}^{*}(x)(\psi_{n}^{*})^{\dagger}(y)\big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{r=0}^{\infty}(-)^{r}m^{2r}\widetilde{G}_{r+1}^{m=0}(x,y)=\widetilde{G}^{m=0}(x,y)+\sum_{r=1}^{\infty}(-)^{r}m^{2r}\widetilde{G}_{r+1}^{m=0}(x,y)\ .

Subtracting the short-distance singularity and letting then
x\to y
gives

\widetilde{G}_{\rm R}(y)=\widetilde{G}_{\rm R}^{m=0}(y)+\sum_{r=1}^{\infty}(-)^{r}m^{2r}\widetilde{G}_{r+1}^{m=0}(y,y)\ ,

(5.89)

so that

m^{2}\int{\rm d}^{2}y\sqrt{g}\,\widetilde{G}_{\rm R}(y)=m^{2}\int{\rm d}^{2}y\sqrt{g}\,\widetilde{G}_{\rm R}^{m=0}(y)-\sum_{k=2}^{\infty}(-)^{k}m^{2k}\widetilde{\zeta}_{m=0}(k)\ .

(5.90)

We can then rewrite (
5.84
) as

\displaystyle\hskip-56.9055pt\delta\Bigg[S_{\rm grav}+\frac{1}{48\pi}S_{\rm Liouville}+\log\det{\cal P}_{0}+\frac{\gamma}{4\pi}\,m^{2}A

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt+\frac{m^{2}}{4}\int{\rm d}^{2}u\sqrt{g}\,\,{\rm tr}\,\widetilde{G}_{\rm R}^{m=0}(u)+\sum_{k=2}^{\infty}\frac{(-)^{k+1}}{4k}m^{2k}\,\widetilde{\zeta}_{m=0}(k)\Bigg]=0\ .

(5.91)

This looks exactly like (
5.84
) - which was written in terms of the massive Green’s function and massive zeta-function - except that now the terms in the sum over
k
have an alternating sign !

The last sum in this equation (
5.3.6
) ressembles, of course, the Taylor expansion of
\log(1+x)
and, indeed, formally we have

\displaystyle\log\Big(\frac{\,{\rm Det}\,^{\prime}D^{2}}{\,{\rm Det}\,^{\prime}D^{2}_{m=0}}\Big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2\log\prod_{n\neq 0}\frac{\lambda_{n}^{2}}{\lambda^{2}_{n,m=0}}=2\sum_{n\neq 0}\log\Big(1+\frac{m^{2}}{\lambda^{2}_{n,m=0}}\Big)\ =2\sum_{n\neq 0}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{(-)^{k+1}}{k}\frac{m^{2k}}{\lambda^{2k}_{n,m=0}}

(5.92)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{(-)^{k+1}}{k}m^{2k}\,\widetilde{\zeta}_{m=0}(k)\ .

It looks a bit as if we have been going in circles and we could have written this expansion right away at the beginning.
However, this formal manipulation results in a sum over
k
including also the singular
k=1
term
\widetilde{\zeta}_{\rm m=0}(1)
. Our more careful treatment has produced instead, the integral of the Green’s function
\widetilde{G}^{m=0}_{\rm R}
, which can be rewritten in terms of
\widetilde{G}^{m=0}_{\zeta}
which in turn is related to the regular part of
\widetilde{\zeta}_{m=0}
, i.e.
\widetilde{\zeta}^{\rm reg}_{m=0}(1)
, as well as the piece
\log\det{\cal P}_{0}
that originated from the zero-modes.

5.4
Integrating the variation of the gravitational action

We have obtained the variation of the gravitational action - to all orders in an expansion in powers of
m^{2}
- under an infinitesimal variation of the conformal factor, corresponding to an infinitesimal variation of the metric. This variation was expressed in terms of variations of integrals of the renormalized Green’s function
\widetilde{G}_{\rm R}^{m=0}
of the massless theory and of the higher Green’s functions
\widetilde{G}_{n}^{m=0}(x,x)
(
n\geq 2
), or equivalently of the finite values of zeta function
\widetilde{\zeta}_{m=0}(n)
, also of the massless theory. We can then immediately “integrate” these infinitesimal variations to obtain
S_{\rm grav}[g,\widehat{g}]
.
Note that
{\cal P}_{0}
as defined in (
2.111
) depends on
\widehat{g}
(through the zero-modes
\widehat{\psi}_{0,i}
) and
\sigma
. We write
{\cal P}_{0}[g]\equiv{\cal P}_{0}[\widehat{g},\sigma]\equiv{\cal P}_{0}[g,\widehat{g}]
. Since the zero-modes
\widehat{\psi}_{0,i}
are orthonormalized for the metric
\widehat{g}
we obviously have
{\cal P}_{0}[\widehat{g}]\equiv{\cal P}_{0}[\widehat{g},\widehat{g}]={\bf 1}
. It follows that

\delta\,\log\det{\cal P}_{0}
integrates to
\log\frac{\det{\cal P}_{0}[g,\widehat{g}]}{\det{\cal P}_{0}[\widehat{g},\widehat{g}]}=\log\det{\cal P}_{0}[g,\widehat{g}]
.
We get

\displaystyle S_{\rm grav}[g,\widehat{g}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{48\pi}S_{\rm Liouville}[g,\widehat{g}]-\det{\cal P}_{0}[g,\widehat{g}]-\frac{\gamma}{4\pi}\,m^{2}(A-\widehat{A})

(5.93)

\displaystyle-\frac{m^{2}}{4}\big({\cal G}[g]-{\cal G}[\widehat{g}]\big)+\sum_{k=2}^{\infty}\frac{(-)^{k}}{4k}m^{2k}\,\big(\widetilde{\zeta}_{m=0}^{\,g}(k)-\widetilde{\zeta}_{m=0}^{\,\widehat{g}}(k)\big)\ ,

where we defined

{\cal G}[g]=\int{\rm d}^{2}u\sqrt{g(u)}\,\,{\rm tr}\,\widetilde{G}_{\rm R}^{m=0}[g](u)\ ,

(5.94)

with the obvious notation that
\widetilde{G}_{\rm R}^{m=0}[g]
is the renormalized Green’s function of the massless theory, with the zero-mode subtracted, for the metric
g
.
Let us insist that the right-hand side of (
5.93
) is entirely expressed in terms of quantities defined in the massless theory. We now want to determine, in particular, the dependence of the functional
{\cal G}[g]
on the conformal factor
\sigma
.

Just as the Liouville action only involves the local quantities
\sigma,\ \widehat{g}
and
\widehat{R}
, ideally one would like to express the higher-order terms similarly in terms of such local quantities. For the simpler case of the effective gravitational action obtained by integrating out a massive scalar field
[
9
]
it turned out that the order
m^{2}
-term could be expressed in terms of the Mabuchi action which is local in
\sigma
and the Kähler potential
\Phi
, and some relatively simple term that involved
\widetilde{G}_{\rm R}^{m=0}[\widehat{g}]
. (In this case
\widetilde{G}_{\rm R}^{m=0}
is the renormalized Green’s function of the
scalar
Laplacian). For such a massive scalar field the higher-order (in
m^{2}
) terms were computed in
[
10
]
and were expressed in terms of higher Green’a functions which encode much non-local information about the manifold.

At present,
\widetilde{G}^{m=0}
is the Green’s function of
D^{2}=-\Delta_{\rm sp}+\frac{{\cal R}}{4}
which, in particular, involves the spinoral Laplacian which is different from the scalar Laplacian.
In order to express
{\cal G}[g]
in terms of local quantities and possibly
\widetilde{G}_{\rm R}^{m=0}[\widehat{g}]
we have tried to determine the variation of
\widetilde{G}_{\rm R}^{m=0}
and tried to write it in such a form that one can integrate the infinitesimal variations to obtain its variation for finite
\sigma
. We tried to mimic the computation for the scalar field where it was useful to express the variation of the conformal factor as the scalar Laplacian of the variation of the Kähler potential and then integrate by parts this scalar Laplacian. However, at present, this turns out to be more complicated since
\widetilde{G}
is
not
the Greens’s function of the scalar Laplacian, and we did not manage to obtain a useful form of the variation of
\widetilde{G}_{\rm R}^{m=0}
along this line.

Here, we will instead proceed along a different avenue which, nevertheless, will allow us to express
{\cal G}[g]-{\cal G}[\widehat{g}]
in terms of the (local) conformal factor
\sigma
, as well as several quantities pertaining to the metric
\widehat{g}
only, such as the Green’s function
\widetilde{S}_{\widehat{g}}
or the zero-mode projector
\widehat{P}_{0}
.

To simplify the notations in this subsection we will introduce further abbreviations for the different integration measures including various powers of
e^{\sigma}
:

{\rm d}\widehat{z}={\rm d}^{2}z\sqrt{\widehat{g}(z)}\quad,\quad{\rm d}\widehat{z}_{\sigma}={\rm d}^{2}z\sqrt{\widehat{g}(z)}\,e^{\sigma(z)}\quad,\quad{\rm d}z={\rm d}^{2}z\sqrt{g(z)}\equiv{\rm d}^{2}z\sqrt{\widehat{g}(z)}\,e^{2\sigma(z)}\ .

(5.95)

5.4.1
The variations of
\widetilde{S}
under finite conformal rescalings for
m=0

We now want to relate
\widetilde{S}\equiv\widetilde{S}_{g}
, which is the Green’s function for the metric
g
, to
\widetilde{\widehat{S}}\equiv\widetilde{S}_{\widehat{g}}
, which is the Green’s function for the metric
\widehat{g}
, where as usual
g=e^{2\sigma}\widehat{g}
.

We will again exploit
{\nabla\hskip-8.53581pt/}_{x}=e^{-3\sigma(x)/2}\widehat{\nabla\hskip-8.53581pt/}_{x}e^{\sigma(x)/2}
but now for finite
\sigma(x)
.
This relation will be particularly fruitful when relating the Green’s function of the massless theory.
Throughout the remainder of this subsection all Green’s functions
\widetilde{S}
,
\widetilde{\widehat{S}}
and
\widetilde{G}
refer to the massless Green’s functions, although we will not indicate it explicitly any more.
For
\sigma=0
, i.e. for the metric
\widehat{g}
, we have

i\widehat{\nabla\hskip-8.53581pt/}_{x}\widetilde{\widehat{S}}(x,y)=\frac{\delta^{(2)}(x-y)}{\sqrt{\widehat{g}}}-\widehat{P}_{0}(x,y)\ ,

(5.96)

where
\widehat{P}_{0}
is the projector on the zero-modes of
i\widehat{\nabla\hskip-8.53581pt/}\,
, as given in (
2.114
). Recall also from (
2.112
) that the projector on the zero-modes of
i{\nabla\hskip-8.53581pt/}
is

P_{0}(x,y)=e^{-\sigma(x)/2}{\overline{P}}_{0}(x,y)e^{-\sigma(y)/2}
, where
{\overline{P}}_{0}
was defined in (
2.113
).
In particular it involves the “area-like” ortho-normalisation factors
{\cal P}_{0,ij}=\int{\rm d}\widehat{z}\,e^{\sigma(z)}\widehat{\psi}^{\dagger}_{0,i}(z)\widehat{\psi}_{0,j}(z)
, cf (
2.111
). The differential equation for
\widetilde{S}
then is

\displaystyle\hskip-28.45274pti{\nabla\hskip-8.53581pt/}_{x}\widetilde{S}(x,y)=\frac{\delta^{(2)}(x-y)}{\sqrt{g}}-P_{0}(x,y)

\displaystyle\hskip-71.13188pt\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad i\widehat{\nabla\hskip-8.53581pt/}_{x}\big(e^{\sigma(x)/2}\widetilde{S}(x,y)e^{\sigma(y)/2}\big)=\frac{\delta^{(2)}(x-y)}{\sqrt{\widehat{g}}}-e^{\sigma(x)}{\overline{P}}_{0}(x,y)\ .\quad

(5.97)

We now set

{\overline{S}}(x,y)=e^{\sigma(x)/2}\widetilde{S}(x,y)e^{\sigma(y)/2}\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad\widetilde{S}(x,y)=e^{-\sigma(x)/2}{\overline{S}}(x,y)e^{-\sigma(y)/2}\ ,

(5.98)

so that (
5.4.1
) becomes

i\widehat{\nabla\hskip-8.53581pt/}_{x}{\overline{S}}(x,y)=\frac{\delta^{(2)}(x-y)}{\sqrt{\widehat{g}}}-e^{\sigma(x)}{\overline{P}}_{0}(x,y)\ .

(5.99)

This is very similar to (
5.96
) except for the term involving the zero-mode “projector”
{\overline{P}}_{0}
. We then set

{\overline{S}}(x,y)=\widetilde{\widehat{S}}(x,y)+T(x,y)\ ,

(5.100)

and obtain the following equation for
T
:

i\widehat{\nabla\hskip-8.53581pt/}_{x}T(x,y)=\widehat{P}_{0}(x,y)-e^{\sigma(x)}{\overline{P}}_{0}(x,y)\ .

(5.101)

Changing
x\to z
and multiplying with
\widetilde{\widehat{S}}(x,z)
and integrating we get

\int{\rm d}\widehat{z}\,\widetilde{\widehat{S}}(x,z)i\widehat{\nabla\hskip-8.53581pt/}_{z}T(z,y)=-\int{\rm d}\widehat{z}\,\widetilde{\widehat{S}}(x,z)e^{\sigma(z)}{\overline{P}}_{0}(z,y)\equiv-\int{\rm d}\widehat{z}_{\sigma}\,\widetilde{\widehat{S}}(x,z){\overline{P}}_{0}(z,y)\ ,

(5.102)

where the piece
\sim\widehat{P}_{0}
dropped out since
\int{\rm d}\widehat{z}\,\widetilde{\widehat{S}}(x,z)\widehat{P}_{0}(z,y)=0
.
On the left-hand side we integrate by parts the
i\widehat{\nabla\hskip-8.53581pt/}_{z}
and use (
5.96
) to get

T(x,y)-\int{\rm d}\widehat{z}\,\widehat{P}_{0}(x,z)T(z,y)=-\int{\rm d}\widehat{z}\,\widetilde{\widehat{S}}(x,z)e^{\sigma(z)}{\overline{P}}_{0}(z,y)\ .

(5.103)

This determines
T
up to a certain zero-mode part which is subtracted on the left-hand side. Just like
{\overline{S}}
and
\widetilde{\widehat{S}}
,
T
must also satisfy
\big(T(x,y)\big)^{\dagger}=T(y,x)
, and we can also expand
T(x,y)
on products of eigenfunctions of
i\widehat{\nabla\hskip-8.53581pt/}
. We separate the zero-mode and non-zero-mode parts, and we have schematically

T(x,y)\ ``="\ \sum_{i,j}\widehat{\Psi}_{0,i}(x)t_{ij}\widehat{\Psi}_{0,j}^{\dagger}(y)+\sum_{i,n}\widehat{\Psi}_{0,i}(x)c_{in}\widehat{\Psi}_{n}^{\dagger}(y)+\sum_{i,n}\widehat{\Psi}_{n}(x)c_{in}^{*}\widehat{\Psi}_{0,i}^{\dagger}(y)+\sum_{n,k}\widehat{\Psi}_{n}(x)d_{nm}\widehat{\Psi}_{m}^{\dagger}(y)\ ,

(5.104)

where
\widehat{\Psi}_{0,i}
stands generically for the zero-modes
\widehat{\psi}_{0,i}
and
\widehat{\psi}^{*}_{0,i}
,
and
\widehat{\Psi}_{n}
stands generically for the non zero-modes
\widehat{\psi}_{n}
and
\widehat{\psi}^{*}_{n}
.
Then, the left-hand side of equation (
5.103
) only contains the terms with the
c_{in}^{*}
and
d_{nm}
while the right-hand side contains terms of the form
\widehat{\Psi}_{n}(x)\widehat{\Psi}^{\dagger}_{0,i}(y)
only. This shows that
d_{nm}=0
, and that all
c_{in}^{*}
are determined, and hence also all
c_{in}
. Thus, (
5.103
) determines
T
uniquely, up to the pure zero-mode piece involving the
t_{ij}
. The latter corresponds to the arbitrary solution to the homogeneous equation
T(x,y)-\int{\rm d}\widehat{z}\,\widehat{P}_{0}(x,z)T(z,y)=0
one may always add. Now, obviously, as for
\widetilde{\widehat{S}}
, we do not want to include such a pure zero-mode piece in
T
and we make the choice to set it to zero. Then
T
only contains the pieces with
c_{in}
and
c_{in}^{*}
and, hence

T(x,y)=-\int{\rm d}\widehat{z}_{\sigma}\,\big(\widetilde{\widehat{S}}(x,z){\overline{P}}_{0}(z,y)+{\overline{P}}_{0}(x,z)\widetilde{\widehat{S}}(z,y)\big)\ .

(5.105)

Obviously, this satisfies
T(x,y)^{\dagger}=T(y,x)
and one may, of course, check directly that it is a solution
27
27

27

Indeed, since
i\widehat{\nabla\hskip-8.53581pt/}_{x}{\overline{P}}_{0}(x,y)=0
one has
i\widehat{\nabla\hskip-8.53581pt/}_{x}T(x,y)=-e^{\sigma(x)}{\overline{P}}(x,y)+\int{\rm d}\widehat{z}\,e^{\sigma(z)}\widehat{P}_{0}(x,z)){\overline{P}}_{0}(z,y)
, and it is not difficult to show that
\int{\rm d}\widehat{z}\,e^{\sigma(z)}\widehat{P}_{0}(x,z)){\overline{P}}_{0}(z,y)=\widehat{P}_{0}(x,y)
.

of (
5.101
).
Inserting the solution (
5.105
) into (
5.100
), and using (
5.98
), we get

\widetilde{S}(x,y)=e^{-\frac{\sigma(x)}{2}}\Big(\widetilde{\widehat{S}}(x,y)-\int{\rm d}\widehat{z}_{\sigma}\,\big(\widetilde{\widehat{S}}(x,z){\overline{P}}_{0}(z,y)+{\overline{P}}_{0}(x,z)\widetilde{\widehat{S}}(z,y)\big)\Big)e^{-\frac{\sigma(y)}{2}}\ .

(5.106)

This can be equivalently rewritten in terms of

{\cal S}(x,y)\equiv e^{-\frac{\sigma(x)}{2}}\widetilde{\widehat{S}}(x,y)e^{-\frac{\sigma(y)}{2}}

(5.107)

as

\widetilde{S}(x,y)={\cal S}(x,y)-\int{\rm d}z\,\big({\cal S}(x,z)P_{0}(z,y)+P_{0}(x,z){\cal S}(z,y)\big)\ ,

(5.108)

which shows that in the absence of zero-modes (spherical topology) one simply has
\widetilde{S}={\cal S}
.

Both (
5.106
) and (
5.108
) express
\widetilde{S}
solely in terms of the conformal factor
\sigma
and quantities computed with the reference metric
\widehat{g}
, i.e.
\widetilde{\widehat{S}}
and the zero-modes
\widehat{\psi}_{0,i}
. To make the dependencies slightly more explicit we introduce the notations

\widetilde{S}\equiv\widetilde{S}_{g}
,
\widetilde{\widehat{S}}\equiv\widetilde{S}_{\widehat{g}}
and

{\overline{P}}_{0}\equiv{\overline{P}}_{0,\widehat{g},\sigma}
, so that (
5.106
) is rewritten once more as

\widetilde{S}_{g}(x,y)=e^{-\frac{\sigma(x)}{2}}\Big(\widetilde{S}_{\widehat{g}}(x,y)-\int{\rm d}\widehat{z}_{\sigma}\,\big(\widetilde{S}_{\widehat{g}}(x,z){\overline{P}}_{0,\widehat{g},\sigma}(z,y)+{\overline{P}}_{0,\widehat{g},\sigma}(x,z)\widetilde{S}_{\widehat{g}}(z,y)\big)\Big)e^{-\frac{\sigma(y)}{2}}\ .

(5.109)

Let us insist, that without the presence of the factor
e^{\sigma(z)}
(hidden in the integration measure
{\rm d}\widehat{z}_{\sigma}
) the integral of
\widetilde{S}_{\widehat{g}}{\overline{P}}
and of
{\overline{P}}_{0}\widetilde{S}_{\widehat{g}}
would of course vanish due to the orthogonality of the zero and non zero-modes.

5.4.2
The variations of
\widetilde{G}
and
\widetilde{G}_{\rm R}
under finite conformal rescalings for
m=0

The dependence of the Green’s function
\widetilde{G}_{g}(x,y)
on the conformal factor can then be obtained straightforwardly by inserting the previous result for
\widetilde{S}_{g}
into
\widetilde{G}_{g}(x,y)=\int{\rm d}^{2}z\sqrt{\widehat{g}}\,e^{2\sigma(z)}\,\widetilde{S}_{g}(x,z)\widetilde{S}_{g}(z,y)
:

\displaystyle\hskip-56.9055pt\widetilde{G}_{g}(x,y)=e^{-\frac{\sigma(x)}{2}}\int{\rm d}\widehat{z}_{\sigma}\,\Big(\widetilde{S}_{\widehat{g}}(x,z)-\int{\rm d}\widehat{u}_{\sigma}\,\big(\widetilde{S}_{\widehat{g}}(x,u){\overline{P}}_{0,\widehat{g},\sigma}(u,z)+{\overline{P}}_{0,\widehat{g},\sigma}(x,u)\widetilde{S}_{\widehat{g}}(u,z)\big)\Big)

\displaystyle\hskip 42.67912pt\times\Big(\widetilde{S}_{\widehat{g}}(z,y)-\int{\rm d}\widehat{v}_{\sigma}\big(\widetilde{S}_{\widehat{g}}(z,v){\overline{P}}_{0,\widehat{g},\sigma}(v,y)+{\overline{P}}_{0,\widehat{g},\sigma}(z,v)\widetilde{S}_{\widehat{g}}(v,y)\big)\Big)e^{-\frac{\sigma(y)}{2}}\ .

(5.110)

Developing the product of the two brackets we get 9 terms. To further simplify the notation, we will suppress the arguments in the multiple integrals. Then we find

\displaystyle\hskip-42.67912pt\widetilde{G}_{g}(x,y)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle e^{\frac{-\sigma(x)}{2}}\Bigg[\int{\rm d}\widehat{z}_{\sigma}\,\widetilde{S}_{\widehat{g}}\widetilde{S}_{\widehat{g}}-\int{\rm d}\widehat{z}_{\sigma}\,d\widehat{u}_{\sigma}\,\Big(\widetilde{S}_{\widehat{g}}\widetilde{S}_{\widehat{g}}{\overline{P}}_{0}+\widetilde{S}_{\widehat{g}}{\overline{P}}_{0}\widetilde{S}_{\widehat{g}}+\widetilde{S}_{\widehat{g}}{\overline{P}}_{0}\widetilde{S}_{\widehat{g}}+{\overline{P}}_{0}\widetilde{S}_{\widehat{g}}\widetilde{S}_{\widehat{g}}\Big)

(5.111)

\displaystyle\hskip 8.53581pt+\int{\rm d}\widehat{z}_{\sigma}\,{\rm d}\widehat{u}_{\sigma}\,{\rm d}\widehat{v}_{\sigma}\,\Big(\widetilde{S}_{\widehat{g}}{\overline{P}}_{0}\widetilde{S}_{\widehat{g}}{\overline{P}}_{0}+\widetilde{S}_{\widehat{g}}{\overline{P}}_{0}{\overline{P}}_{0}\widetilde{S}_{\widehat{g}}+{\overline{P}}_{0}\widetilde{S}_{\widehat{g}}\widetilde{S}_{\widehat{g}}{\overline{P}}_{0}+{\overline{P}}_{0}\widetilde{S}_{\widehat{g}}{\overline{P}}_{0}\widetilde{S}_{\widehat{g}}\Big)\Bigg]e^{-\frac{\sigma(y)}{2}}\ .

Again, due to the presence of the
e^{\sigma}
factors (hidden in
{\rm d}u_{\sigma}
etc) for each integration, no simplification occurs
28
28

28

The only slight simplification comes from
\int{\rm d}\widehat{u}_{\sigma}\,{\overline{P}}_{0}(z,u){\overline{P}}_{0}(u,v)={\overline{P}}_{0}(z,v)
which one can show by rewriting
{\overline{P}}_{0}
in terms of
P_{0}
and using that
\int{\rm d}^{2}u\sqrt{g}\,P_{0}(z,u)P_{0}(u,v)=P_{0}(z,v)
.

in the integrals of products of
\widetilde{S}_{\widehat{g}}
and
{\overline{P}}_{0}
.

The short-distance singularity of (
5.111
) comes from the first term in the bracket and we know that it is
-\frac{1}{4\pi}\log\frac{\mu^{2}\ell^{2}_{g}(x,y)}{4}{\bf 1}_{2\times 2}
. As already discussed, we have for finite conformal transformations

\ell_{g}^{2}(x,y)\sim_{x\to y}e^{\sigma(x)+\sigma(y)}\ell^{2}_{\widehat{g}}(x,y)\quad\Rightarrow\quad-\frac{1}{4\pi}\log\frac{\mu^{2}\ell^{2}_{g}(x,y)}{4}\sim_{x\to y}-\frac{1}{4\pi}\log\frac{\mu^{2}\ell^{2}_{\widehat{g}}(x,y)}{4}-\frac{\sigma(x)+\sigma(y)}{4\pi}\ .

(5.112)

We see again that the singular parts of
\widetilde{G}_{g}
and
\widetilde{G}_{\widehat{g}}
are the same and it follows that
\widetilde{G}_{g}(x,y)-\widetilde{G}_{\widehat{g}}(x,y)
has no short-distance singularity.
We also see that

\widetilde{G}_{\rm R}[g](y)-\widetilde{G}_{\rm R}[\widehat{g}](y)=\lim_{x\to y}\Big(\widetilde{G}_{g}(x,y)-\widetilde{G}_{\widehat{g}}(x,y)+\frac{\sigma(y)}{2\pi}\,{\bf 1}_{2\times 2}\Big)\ .

(5.113)

Combining this with (
5.111
) we get

\displaystyle\hskip-28.45274pt\widetilde{G}_{\rm R}[g](y)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\widetilde{G}_{\rm R}[\widehat{g}](y)+\frac{\sigma(y)}{2\pi}\,{\bf 1}_{2\times 2}\ +\int{\rm d}\widehat{z}\Big(e^{\sigma(z)-\sigma(y)}-1\Big)\widetilde{S}_{\widehat{g}}(y,z)\widetilde{S}_{\widehat{g}}(z,y)

(5.114)

\displaystyle\hskip-14.22636pt-e^{-\sigma(y)}\int{\rm d}\widehat{z}_{\sigma}\,d\widehat{u}_{\sigma}\,\Big(\widetilde{S}_{\widehat{g}}\widetilde{S}_{\widehat{g}}{\overline{P}}_{0}+\widetilde{S}_{\widehat{g}}{\overline{P}}_{0}\widetilde{S}_{\widehat{g}}+\widetilde{S}_{\widehat{g}}{\overline{P}}_{0}\widetilde{S}_{\widehat{g}}+{\overline{P}}_{0}\widetilde{S}_{\widehat{g}}\widetilde{S}_{\widehat{g}}\Big)\Big|_{x=y}

\displaystyle\hskip-14.22636pt+e^{-\sigma(y)}\int{\rm d}\widehat{z}_{\sigma}\,{\rm d}\widehat{u}_{\sigma}\,{\rm d}\widehat{v}_{\sigma}\,\Big(\widetilde{S}_{\widehat{g}}{\overline{P}}_{0}\widetilde{S}_{\widehat{g}}{\overline{P}}_{0}+\widetilde{S}_{\widehat{g}}{\overline{P}}_{0}{\overline{P}}_{0}\widetilde{S}_{\widehat{g}}+{\overline{P}}_{0}\widetilde{S}_{\widehat{g}}\widetilde{S}_{\widehat{g}}{\overline{P}}_{0}+{\overline{P}}_{0}\widetilde{S}_{\widehat{g}}{\overline{P}}_{0}\widetilde{S}_{\widehat{g}}\Big)\Big|_{x=y}\ .

The functional
{\cal G}[g]=\int{\rm d}^{2}y\sqrt{g}\,{\rm tr}\,\widetilde{G}_{\rm R}[g](y)=\int{\rm d}\widehat{y}\,e^{2\sigma(y)}\,{\rm tr}\,\widetilde{G}_{\rm R}[g](y)\equiv\int{\rm d}\widehat{y}_{\sigma}\,e^{\sigma(y)}\,{\rm tr}\,\widetilde{G}_{\rm R}[g](y)
then is easily obtained from the previous relation. In particular, one can now use the cyclicity of the trace to simplify the many terms involving
{\overline{P}}_{0}
. Writing out explicitly all factors of
e^{\sigma}
we get

\displaystyle\hskip-42.67912pt{\cal G}[g]={\cal G}[\widehat{g}]+\int{\rm d}\widehat{y}\,\big(e^{2\sigma}-1\big)\,{\rm tr}\,\widetilde{G}_{\rm R}[\widehat{g}](y)+\frac{1}{\pi}\int{\rm d}\widehat{y}\,e^{2\sigma}\sigma

\displaystyle\hskip-14.22636pt+\int{\rm d}\widehat{y}\,{\rm d}\widehat{z}\,\Big(e^{\sigma(z)+\sigma(y)}-e^{2\sigma(y)}\Big)\,{\rm tr}\,\widetilde{S}_{\widehat{g}}(y,z)\widetilde{S}_{\widehat{g}}(z,y)

\displaystyle\hskip-14.22636pt-4\int{\rm d}\widehat{y}\,{\rm d}\widehat{z}\,{\rm d}\widehat{u}\,e^{\sigma(y)+\sigma(z)+\sigma(u)}\,{\rm tr}\,\widetilde{S}_{\widehat{g}}(y,z)\widetilde{S}_{\widehat{g}}(z,u){\overline{P}}_{0}(u,y)

\displaystyle\hskip-14.22636pt+2\int{\rm d}\widehat{y}\,{\rm d}\widehat{z}\,{\rm d}\widehat{u}\,{\rm d}\widehat{v}\,e^{\sigma(y)+\sigma(z)+\sigma(u)+\sigma(v)}\,\,{\rm tr}\,\Big(\widetilde{S}_{\widehat{g}}{\overline{P}}_{0}\widetilde{S}_{\widehat{g}}{\overline{P}}_{0}+\widetilde{S}_{\widehat{g}}\widetilde{S}_{\widehat{g}}{\overline{P}}_{0}{\overline{P}}_{0}\Big)\ .

(5.115)

Again, in the absence of zero-modes, i.e. for spherical topology, the last two integrals are absent.
Let us recall, once more, that all quantities in this equation are those of the massless theory.

5.4.3
The gravitational action to order
m^{2}
as an explicit functional of
\sigma

We can then insert this result into (
5.93
) to get the contributions to the effective gravitational action at order
m^{2}
:

\displaystyle\hskip-28.45274ptS_{\rm grav}[g,\widehat{g}]=-\frac{1}{48\pi}S_{\rm Liouville}[g,\widehat{g}]-\log\det{\cal P}_{0}[g,\widehat{g}]-\frac{\gamma}{4\pi}\,m^{2}(A-\widehat{A})

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt-\frac{m^{2}}{4}\Bigg[\int{\rm d}^{2}y\sqrt{\widehat{g}}\big(e^{2\sigma}-1\big)\,{\rm tr}\,\widetilde{G}_{\rm R}[\widehat{g}](y)+\frac{1}{\pi}\int{\rm d}^{2}z\,\sqrt{\widehat{g}}e^{2\sigma}\sigma

\displaystyle\hskip 42.67912pt+\int{\rm d}^{2}y\sqrt{\widehat{g}}\,{\rm d}^{2}z\sqrt{\widehat{g}}\Big(e^{\sigma(z)+\sigma(y)}-e^{2\sigma(y)}\Big)\,{\rm tr}\,\widetilde{S}_{\widehat{g}}(y,z)\widetilde{S}_{\widehat{g}}(z,y)

\displaystyle\hskip 42.67912pt-4\int{\rm d}^{2}y\sqrt{\widehat{g}}\,{\rm d}^{2}z\sqrt{\widehat{g}}\,d^{2}u\sqrt{\widehat{g}}\,e^{\sigma(y)+\sigma(z)+\sigma(u)}\,{\rm tr}\,\widetilde{S}_{\widehat{g}}(y,z)\widetilde{S}_{\widehat{g}}(z,u){\overline{P}}_{0}(u,y)

\displaystyle\hskip 42.67912pt+2\int{\rm d}^{2}y\sqrt{\widehat{g}}\,{\rm d}^{2}z\sqrt{\widehat{g}}\,d^{2}u\sqrt{\widehat{g}}\,{\rm d}^{2}v\sqrt{\widehat{g}}\,e^{\sigma(y)+\sigma(z)+\sigma(u)+\sigma(v)}\,\,{\rm tr}\,\Big(\widetilde{S}_{\widehat{g}}{\overline{P}}_{0}\widetilde{S}_{\widehat{g}}{\overline{P}}_{0}+\widetilde{S}_{\widehat{g}}\widetilde{S}_{\widehat{g}}{\overline{P}}_{0}{\overline{P}}_{0}\Big)\ \Bigg]

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt+\ {\cal O}(m^{4})\ .

(5.116)

We see that at order
m^{0}
we get the Liouville action and a specific zero-mode contribution involving the area-like constants
{\cal P}_{0,ij}
. Of course, if present, the zero-modes are zero-modes of the massless Dirac operator
i{\nabla\hskip-8.53581pt/}\,
, while the massive Dirac operator never has zero-modes. In the massless theory, one does not do the functional integral over these zero-modes and then there is a certain freedom how exactly we define the matter partition function. In particular, one can then redefine the matter partition by dividing
Z_{\rm mat}[g]
by
\det{\cal P}_{0}[g]
and then the corresponding term would have been absent in the effective gravitational action. However, we are really computing in the massive theory and then the massive Dirac operator has no zero-modes. Hence, there is no reason to change the definition of the matter partition function and the term
\log\det{\cal P}_{0}[g,\widehat{g}]
should be genuinely present.

At order
m^{2}
we find the cosmological constant term
\sim m^{2}(A-\widehat{A})
, but also a term
\int\sqrt{\widehat{g}}e^{2\sigma}\sigma
characteristic of the Mabuchi action

S_{\rm M}[g,\widehat{g}]=\frac{4}{A}\int\sqrt{\widehat{g}}\,e^{2\sigma}\,\sigma+\ldots,

(5.117)

Obviously, this term is local in
\sigma
.
If we trace things back, this term originated from the conformal transformation of the singular part of the Green’s function
\widetilde{G}
one had to subtract to get the renormalized Green’s function
\widetilde{G}_{\rm R}
(see e.g. (
5.113
)). It is interesting to note, that the Mabuchi action appears at present with a coefficient
-\frac{m^{2}A}{16\pi}
while in the case of the massive scalar field one exactly obtained the opposite coefficient
+\frac{m^{2}A}{16\pi}
.

The other terms present in (
5.4.3
) are multi-local in
\sigma
and also involve the various Green’s function for the reference metric
\widehat{g}
, as well as the area-like parameters
{\cal P}_{0,ij}
. Such “non-local” terms involving the Green’s functions on the manifold also are present in the effective gravitational action for massive scalars
[
10
]
at higher orders in
m^{2}
, starting at
m^{4}
.
However, it is quite remarkable that, at least at order
m^{2}
, the gravitational action

S_{\rm grav}[g,,\widehat{g}]\equiv S_{\rm grav}[\sigma,\widehat{g}]
can be expressed as a functional that is local or multi-local in
\sigma
.

One might wonder why our gravitational action (
5.4.3
) at order
m^{2}
involves the zero-mode projectors when we have shown that corresponding variation (
5.3.6
) could be written solely in terms of
{\cal G}[g]=\int\sqrt{g}\,\widetilde{G}_{\rm R}[g]
? The reason is that upon integrating the variation from
\sigma=0
to
\sigma
(from
\widehat{g}
to
g
) the zero-mode projector changes in a non-trivial way, and subtracting the zero-modes from
G[g]
is not the same as subtracting them from
G[\widehat{g}]
.

6
Discussion and outlook

In these notes, we have studied the effective gravitational action for massive fermions in two dimensions, continuing and completing what had been initiated in
[
1
]
.
The appropriate mass term is a Majorana type mass term
\int\psi^{\dagger}m\gamma_{*}\psi
, and the spectral analysis we performed was based on the Dirac operator
D=i{\nabla\hskip-8.53581pt/}+m\gamma_{*}
whose eigenfunctions necessarily are complex. What might have looked as a simple generalisation of the massive scalar case, actually turned out to be technically quite involved. We performed a detailed study of the corresponding Green’s functions, local zeta-functions and local heat kernels of this Dirac operator
D
and of its square
D^{2}
. One of the crucial insights of the present paper was to obtain a simple relation, not only between the Greens’s functions
S
of
D
and
G
of
D^{2}
, but also between the corresponding local zeta-functions
\zeta_{-}(s,x,y)
and
\zeta_{+}(s+\frac{1}{2},x,y)
. By the (inverse) Mellin transform this allowed us to obtain much information about the local heat kernel
K_{-}(t,x,y)
from the small-
t
expansion of the heat kernel
K_{+}(t,x,y)
which we worked out in the appendix. (This confirms some of the conjectures made in
[
1
]
but also infirms some others.) In particular, we could obtain all the necessary information about the short-distance singularities and the poles of the local zeta-functions
\zeta_{\pm}(s,x,x)
at coinciding points. Most importantly we studied the variations of these quantities under infinitesimal conformal rescalings of the metric, and then “integrated” these infinitesimal variations to get the finite effective gravitational action
S[g,\widehat{g}]
.

The gravitational action was obtained by performing an expansion in powers of the (small) mass and, accordingly, we need to define all our Green’s and local zeta functions also for vanishing mass. In this case there are generically (for genus larger or equal to one) zero-modes of the massless Dirac operator that must be excluded from the spectral decomposition of these Green’s or local zeta functions and, hence, a detailed understanding of the contributions of these fermionic zero-modes was necessary.
Of course the number and properties of the zero-modes crucially depend on the topology of the manifold. Nevertheless, we were able to sufficiently characterise these zero-mode parts of the Green’s functions, allowing us to make general statements for arbitrary genus. In particular, the dependence of the zero-modes (and only of the zero-modes) on the conformal factor turned out to be rather simple, which allowed us to completely characterise the different projectors on these zero-modes we needed to introduce. This was one of the important new insights in these notes as compared to what was done in
[
1
]
.

We could thus obtain an expansion of the gravitational action, for arbitrary genus, to all powers in
m^{2}
, involving higher and higher Green’s functions at coinciding points. At order
m^{0}
the effective gravitational action contains the well-known Liouville action with the appropriate coefficient for a Majorana fermion, namely
\frac{1}{2}
times the one for a single scalar field. There is also a certain contribution involving the normalisation factors of the zero-modes which depend on a set of “area-like” parameters. While in the massless theory this contribution could be removed by a correspondingly different definition of the matter partition function from the outset, there is no reason to do this in the massive theory and then these terms constitute a genuinely new contribution at order
m^{0}
.

At order
m^{2}
we found a local contribution
\int\sqrt{\widehat{g}}\,\sigma\,e^{2\sigma}
, characteristic of the Mabuchi action. This term appeared with the same coefficient as for a massive scalar field, but with the opposite sign, as one might perhaps have expected.
But we also found, at this
order
m^{2}
, contributions that can be expressed in terms of integrals and multiple integrals involving the conformal factor
\sigma
at the different integration points, and the Green’s function of
D
for the background metric
\widehat{g}
and the renormalized Green’s function of
D^{2}
at coinciding points, also in the background metric
\widehat{g}
, as well as on the area-like parameters
{\cal P}_{0,ij}
. To appreciate the degree of non-locality of these additional terms, one must remember that the knowledge of a Green’s function encodes much non-local information about the manifold. But the Green’s function which appear are only those for metric
\widehat{g}
, so in this sense these contributions are “non-local in
\widehat{g}\,
” only, and local or multi-local in the conformal factor
\sigma
. Such multi-local or non-local terms are to be expected and were also present in the scalar case starting at order
m^{4}

[
10
]
. Finally we argued that the area-like constants
{\cal P}_{0,ij}
that appear in the zero-mode projectors are not more or less non-local than a term
\frac{1}{A}
.

The effective gravitational action is of course to be used in the next step, if one wants to compute the functional integral over the geometries. This will involve in particular a functional integration over the conformal factor
{\cal D}\sigma
. The form of the effective gravitational action we have obtained explicitly displays this
\sigma
-dependence, having it separated from the quantities that only depend on the fixed “background” metric
\widehat{g}
. Our explicit separation should simplify this subsequent integration over the geometries ! We hope to return to this issue elsewhere.
